<summary>This is the title page for the first volume of the Zenon Papyri catalogue, published in 1925 by C. C. Edgar as part of the General Catalogue of the Cairo Museum.</summary>

<keywords>Zenon Papyri, Egyptian Antiquities, Cairo Museum, C. C. Edgar, IFAO, Papyrology, Egyptology</keywords>

--- Page 6 ---
<meta>This introduction establishes the provenance and historical context of the Zenon Papyri, following the discovery of the archive in the Fayoum region.</meta>

# INTRODUCTION.

The papyri associated with the name of Zenon, of which those published in the present volume form but a small part, were found about ten years ago at Kharabet el Gerza, the ancient Philadelphia, on the eastern edge of the Fayoum. Little is known about the circumstances of this remarkable find. The <term>sebakh-diggers</term> <note>Local farmers who dug up ancient mud-brick ruins to use the nitrogen-rich decomposed organic matter as fertilizer; in doing so, they often stumbled upon papyrus caches.</note> who divided the spoil were naturally shy of speaking about it to any-one connected with the Antiquities Department, and I have tried in vain to ascertain the exact spot of the discovery. A large portion was bought by the *Italian Society for the search of Greek and Latin Papyri* <note>original: "Società Italiana per la ricerca dei Papiri greci e latini"</note>, and this has been edited in an admirable manner by G. Vitelli, with the aid of Medea Norsa and others, in vols. IV, V, VI and VII of the Society’s publications (referred to in the following pages as *P. S. I.*). Other portions have gone to England and America; some pieces are in France and Germany; and a considerable amount is still in private hands. Our own collection has been formed gradually; it is now larger than any of those already mentioned; and, even as I write, one or two pieces have come into our possession just too late to be inserted in their chronological place among the texts here printed. The relations between antiquity-dealers and the Antiquities Department are not always cordial; but in the present case I have much pleasure in acknowledging that it was by the active aid of Mr. M. Nahman that the greater part of our collection was acquired.

Zenon, to whom we are indebted for the preservation of this mass of extraordinarily interesting documents, was the business man of a great personage called Apollonios, who held the post of <term>dioiketes</term> <note>The highest financial official in Ptolemaic Egypt, similar to a Minister of Finance or Chancellor.</note> or Minister of Finance during the last fifteen years of the reign of Ptolemy II. When the correspondence begins we find Zenon travelling abroad, trading and transacting business, in the service of Apollonios. Later on he accompanies his master on the long tours which the latter made in the interior of Egypt. Finally, in 256 B.C., he settled down at Philadelphia where Apollonios held a large estate as a gift or loan from the king. Most fortunately Zenon brought with him the papers which he had already amassed, and he continued to add to the pile till well into the reign of Ptolemy III. Sometimes clearly, more often vaguely, we can follow his fortunes and

<summary>This introduction details the discovery of the Zenon Papyri at Philadelphia and introduces Zenon as the business manager for Apollonios, the Finance Minister of Ptolemy II.</summary>
<keywords>Zenon Papyri, Philadelphia, Fayoum, Apollonios, Ptolemy II, dioiketes, papyrology, Kharabet el Gerza</keywords>

--- Page 7 ---
<meta>This page continues the preface of the Zenon papyri collection, detailing the archival history and the editorial methodology used for the publication.</meta>

# — VI —

those of Apollonios <note>Apollonios was the finance minister (Dioiketes) of Ptolemy II Philadelphus; Zenon was his chief manager.</note> from year to year. It is probable that he died in Philadelphia <note>A town in the Fayum region of Egypt, named after the Queen.</note>; but whether he died there or migrated once again, the papers remained and lay untouched where he had left them for more than twenty centuries.

We propose to publish the whole of our share of Zenon's archives in the *General Catalogue*, and the present volume is the first instalment of the work. The dated texts will be given in chronological order and the undated material will follow. As we learn more about Zenon and his correspondents we shall become able to assign to their proper places many of the documents whose date and exact import are not yet obvious. Even in the present volume a few undated pieces have been inserted where they seem to belong, and still more might have been included with a fair measure of probability. But it is better not to run the risk of obscuring the picture by mixing together documents which may possibly refer to different times and circumstances. The texts published here range from year 26, when the correspondence begins, to the end of year 29, when Zenon was finally established at Philadelphia, and they therefore cover what is for us the most interesting period of his life. I have not attempted to comment, except occasionally and briefly, on the many points of interest, — philological, historical, legal and economical, — which the papyri contain, but it has been my aim to present them as a connected series against the background of Zenon's career. Rostovtzeff's book called *A large estate in Egypt in the third century B. C.* may be recommended as a most valuable introduction to the whole subject.

More than a hundred of our most important texts have already been published in a series of articles in the *Annals of the Antiquities Service*, vols. XVIII-XXIV. This preparatory publication is referred to by some scholars as *P. Zen.* and by others as *P. Edg.* I have reluctantly adopted the latter title as less likely to be misunderstood and suggest that *P. Cairo Zen.* should be reserved for the new publication. It would have been greatly to the advantage of the complete edition if these articles had been subjected to more detailed criticism (such as Schubart and Wilcken have devoted to nos. 59022 and 59036, and Father Vincent to no. 59003); and it is for lack of better guidance that many of the notes and remarks in the *Catalogue* are merely abridged or taken without much alteration from those in the *Annals*.

The fragments, of which there must be several hundred in our collection, have given me more trouble than the complete texts. The latter, even if one does not fully understand them, one can copy as faithfully as possible and leave the interpretation to others; the piecing together of fragments, which demands

<page-num>VII</page-num>
# — VII —

constant recourse to the original material, must be done chiefly by oneself. Though I have succeeded in recovering in this way a fair number of complete or nearly complete texts, it is disappointing to find what a large residue of isolated fragments still remains. There is good reason to hope that some at least of the odds and ends printed in this volume will be completed some day; but I have thought it better to publish them as they are, in the place which they occupy in the series, rather than wait for the fulfilment of that expectation. And few of the dated fragments are so insignificant as not to add something to our knowledge on some point or other.

The dates of the papyri are converted into dates on the Julian calendar in accordance with the system followed in my articles in the *Annals* and confirmed by the researches of Beloch (1). A new system has lately been elaborated by <term>Ernst Meyer</term> <note>A German historian specializing in ancient chronology.</note>, *Investigations into the Chronology of the first Ptolemies*, according to which all the dates ought to be put back one year; but the evidence which he brings forward will be found, if one examines it carefully, to contradict on several essential points the conclusions which he draws from it, and his theory seems to me to be both unsound and improbable. When a text is dated by the Macedonian month alone, I insert an 'about' before the dating by the Julian calendar; but during the period covered by the present volume the margin of error is very small, not more than a day or two.

The texts are printed in accordance with the method followed by Grenfell and Hunt <note>The pioneering papyrologists who established the standard system for transcribing ancient texts.</note> and thus described by them : 
> «Square brackets [ ] indicate a <term>lacuna</term> <note>A gap or missing part in a manuscript.</note>, round brackets ( ) the resolution of a symbol or abbreviation, angular brackets < > a mistaken omission in the original, braces { } a superfluous letter or letters, double square brackets [[ ]] a deletion. Dots placed within brackets represent approximately the number of letters lost or deleted; dots outside brackets indicate mutilated or otherwise illegible letters. Letters with dots under them are to be considered doubtful.» 

Intervals between two words, as in no. 59075, pl. XVII, are not emphasized in the printed text, as they seem to me to be of small importance. For convenience' sake the top line of the <term>docket</term> <note>A brief summary or filing note written on the back of a document.</note> on the <term>verso</term> <note>The back side of the papyrus sheet.</note> is uniformly printed opposite the address, though in the original it may be a little higher or a little lower, being simply written, in small characters, about the middle of the left end of the fold on which the

***

(1) *Archive*, VII, p. 161 (Beloch); *Annals*, XVII, p. 209; *Champollion Collection*, p. 119. With regard to the last-cited article I wish to add that, accepting Beloch's view that the Egyptian years of Ptolemy II were accidentally post-dated owing to his joint kingship with Soter <note>Ptolemy I Soter, the founder of the dynasty.</note>, I now agree with him that the Macedonian or regnal year was counted from the anniversary of the accession.

<summary>The editor explains the chronological arrangement of the Zenon papyri, the challenges of reconstructing fragments, and the specific notation system used to indicate missing or corrected text.</summary>
<keywords>Zenon Archive, papyrology, Ptolemaic Egypt, chronology, Julian calendar, editorial method, Philadelphia, Apollonios</keywords>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page concludes the introductory remarks on the physical characteristics and folding of the Zenon papyri and begins the formal catalogue entries, starting with a loan agreement from 273 B.C.</meta>

<page-num>VIII</page-num>

address stands. Similarly the note which is sometimes printed to the right of the address and in a line with it (e. g. no. 59125, 6) is in reality on the next fold and, in relation to the address, upside down; but to print it thus would be eccentric. The measurement 0 m. 14 x 0 m. 325 means that the height of the papyrus across the writing is 14 cm. and its width along the writing 325 mill., quite independently of the direction of the fibres. In the case of letters I have thought it unnecessary to state whether the writing is along or across the fibres, because with scarcely an exception they are either written in long lines across the fibres of the recto <note>The "recto" is the side of the papyrus where the fibers run horizontally, usually the side written on first.</note> (e. g., no. 59027, pl. IX) or in short lines along the fibres of the recto (e. g., no. 59031, pl. XI). Each letter was of course written on a strip of papyrus cut off the end of the roll (whether before the scribe began or after he had finished), and the height of the roll is represented by the height of a short-lined letter and by the width of a long-lined one. The finished letter was then folded up along its length and doubled over; or, if very narrow, it might be folded up along its breadth and not doubled over. No. 59027, pl. IX, and no. 59057, pl. XVI, show how a letter looked when folded and tied up by a strip of fibre detached from the papyrus. The letters were of course sealed when delivered to Zenon, but merely refolded when placed among the files.

Among the many scholars who have helped me in one way or another, I am particularly indebted to G. Vitelli, not only for providing a model by his edition of the Florentine texts and for information on many points of detail, but also for sending us a large number of utilizable fragments; to H. I. Bell for showing me his transcripts of the unpublished material in the British Museum and for kindly enabling me to complete several of the texts in this volume; and to Grenfell, Boak and Schubart for most generously ceding to us a considerable quantity of Zenon papyri which they had themselves purchased in Cairo. One or two of the photographs from which the plates have been reproduced were taken by Mr. E. Busuttil, all the rest are the work of Mr. F. Oropesa. In order to illustrate a large number of the texts we have been obliged to reproduce most of them on a reduced scale; and it is therefore advisable, in studying the plates, to refer to the dimensions given in the Catalogue.

# ZENON PAPYRI.

**59001. Loan of money.** — (*a*) 0 m. 178 x 0 m. 063; (*b*) 0 m. 10 x 0 m. 068; (*c*) 0 m. 125 x 0 m. 07. — Date : Spring of 273 B. C.

The fragment (*a*) is the left side of the text of which the right side has been already published in *P. S. I.*, 321. The two separated portions are transcribed below, without any mark of division, as the inner text; for the position of the names of the witnesses on the verso <note>The back side of the papyrus.</note> indicates that this part of the document had been folded up and sealed. The fragment (*b*), published in my *Selected Papyri*, no. 1, and the fragment (*c*), recently acquired, form part of the outer text. The writing on the recto is across the fibres.

Though this document seems to have been found among Zenon's files, it has no apparent connection with them. It is a contract, of a common type, by which Dionysios, a native of Gaza in Palestine, lends 34 drachmæ to a Thracian <term>cleruch</term> <note>A soldier-settler who was granted a plot of land by the state in exchange for military service.</note> called Isidoros. This sum is said to be the price of the <term>royal corn</term> <note>original: "βασιλικός σῖτος"</note> and is to be repaid in three months' time, after the harvest. As Rostovtzeff has pointed out, the <term>royal corn</term> was probably the corn which the <term>cleruchs</term> were obliged to sell to the king at a given price, and the 34 drachmæ would be the sum due to Isidoros for that portion of his corn which the king would take. Apart from this the papyrus gives us an interesting glimpse into a <term>cleruchy</term> of Thracian soldiers in the early Ptolemaic period. They were settled in the Memphite <term>nome</term> <note>An administrative district in ancient Egypt.</note>, in a village called Pitos (cf. *P. Par.*, 13), and held allotments of 40, 60, 70 and 110 <term>arourai</term> <note>An ancient unit of land area, roughly 2/3 of an acre.</note>. Leaving out of account the <term>descendants of the twenty-five aroura holders</term> <note>original: "ἐπίγονοι εἰκοσιπεντάρουροι"</note> (*P. Hib.*, 87; *P. Lille*, 39), we find in these figures a very marked difference from the scale of 30, 70, 100, which seems to have prevailed in the second half of the 3rd century B. C. (LESQUIER, *Military Institutions*, p. 175).

*Lines 2-3.* As the beginning of the regnal year fell between Peritios and Artemisios, the period of the loan was three months.
*Lines 3-6.* It is very probable that the persons mentioned in *Paris Papyri* 13, 3-5, were descendants of Isidoros and the other Thracian settlers.
*Line 16.* The term <term>as if for debts to the crown</term> is supposed to be used of debts to Government and <term>as if for debts to the crown</term> of private debts (Meyer, *Hamburg Papyri*, p. 104, note 5). In the present case, if Rostovtzeff is right, the loan was a private one but was secured on an expected payment from the Treasury.
*Line 18.* Damon, not Ammon. Vitelli has kindly verified this correction.

*Line 46.* Between <term>joint-holder</term> and <term>arourai</term> read <term>with Damon</term> or some other name (see note on line 51).
*Line 51.* Though Zipyros was in fact a son of Bithys, the position of the interpolated words shows that <term>son of Bithys</term> is to be taken with <term>joint-holder</term>, the meaning being that Zipyros owned a share of 60 <term>arourai</term> in his father's holding. In line 24 of the inner text the interpolated <term>son of Bithys</term> means 'son of Bithys', and <term>joint-holder</term> means 'partner with the same'. On this analogy I have suggested <term>Damon</term> in line 46.
*Line 54. E. g., "this contract is to be valid."*

<summary>This page contains a legal contract for a loan of 34 silver drachmae between Dionysios, a Gazan, and Isidoros, a Thracian soldier-settler, dating to the 12th year of Ptolemy II. It details the interest rates, repayment terms, and lists several Thracian witnesses who were also military landholders.</summary>

### 59002. Letter from Apollonios to Zenon. — 0 m. 145 × 0 m. 305. — Date: about 24th November, 260 B.C. (pl. I).

A letter of introduction, requesting Zenon to give the two persons mentioned a berth on one of the coasting vessels and to assign them the usual salary. It is probable that the letter was written in Alexandria and that Zenon was at present staying somewhere on the coast, perhaps in Egypt or perhaps in Palestine. We may assume that Nikomachos and Zoilos were to be employed abroad, and probably in the private service of Apollonios. Zoilos may be the individual who wrote no. 59057 and *P. S. I.*, 330.
The letters of Apollonios, though written in various hands, are almost always fine, imposing specimens of calligraphy. They were of course written out by his clerks, from his notes or dictation or verbal orders.

> Apollonios to Zenon, greetings. We have sent to [you]
> Nikomachos and Zoilos. Introduce them, therefore, [ ]
> <gloss>or the *kybaia*</gloss> <note>original: "ἢ τὴν κυβαίαν". A *kybaia* was a type of merchant ship.</note>
> onto the *keletes* <note>original: "κέλητα". A fast, light sailing vessel.</note> so that they may sail, and the salary which is also
> paid to the others, let these men also receive. And they have an advance payment

*Lines 2-3.* The missing word is not obvious. "Having put them on board" <note>original: "ἐμβιβάσας"</note>, or "having taken them along" <note>original: "ἀναλαβών"</note>, followed by "into" <note>original: "εἰς"</note>, is too long, unless the writing became very cramped at the end of the line. "[Letting] them go" <note>original: "[προ]εὶς"</note> would be suitable for the space but does not seem possible in the sense of "having sent them forth." Perhaps simply "having taken" <note>original: "λαβών"</note>.
*Line 3.* The *cybaea* of Verres is described by Cicero as <note>original: "navem maximam, triremis instar, pulcherrimam atque ornatissimam"</note> "a very large ship, like a trireme, most beautiful and richly adorned," but the *kybaiai* of the Zenon papyri were small trading vessels which had no difficulty in sailing up the river (see no. 59012 and *P. S. I.*, 594), and which could even be used for transporting corn from the countryside <note>original: "χώρα" (*chora*)</note> to Alexandria (*P. Edg.*, 12, intro.).

<page-num>5</page-num>
<header>ZENON PAPYRI.</header>

> 5 from us, each [received] twenty silver drachmae.
> ->Farewell. Year 26, Hyperberetaios 25.<- <note>Hyperberetaios was a month in the Macedonian calendar used by the Ptolemies.</note>

REVERSE SIDE:
->Year 26, Hyperberetaios. To Zenon.<-
->5<-

*Line 8.* I do not know what the "5" <note>original: "ε"</note> means. It can scarcely be equivalent to "the fifth day of the waning month" <note>original: "πέμπτηι φθίνοντος"</note>, while to suppose that Zenon had been numbering the letters received by the same mail would be too far-fetched.

BIBLIOGRAPHY: *Journal of Entry of the Museum*, no. 40449; *P. Edg.*, no. 2.

### 59003. Sale of a girl slave. — 0 m. 295 × 0 m. 21. — Date: April-May, 259 B.C. (pl. II).

A duplicate deed of sale, attested by six witnesses. The inner text was, as usual, sealed up separately. Three seal-impressions on clay were still adhering to the strings of fibre when the papyrus came into our possession, the subjects being: 1) head of Herakles to right; 2) upper part of winged goddess with left hand raised holding sceptre; 3) indistinct. The writing is across the fibres.
The text records the sale of a little girl called Sphragis, perhaps a Babylonian. The seller was Nikanor, the buyer Zenon, and the price paid was fifty drachmae. The sale took place in the land of Ammanitis <note>Modern-day Amman, Jordan.</note>, in a place called Birta, which has been identified by Father Vincent with the ancient site known as Araq el-Emir. Several of the persons mentioned in the text are said to be <term>cleruchs</term> <note>original: "κληροῦχος" (*klerouchos*). These were soldier-settlers who were granted land by the king in exchange for military service.</note> belonging to the troops of Toubias, an Ammonite chief of the great Toubiad family, which played a prominent part in Palestinian history (see the articles cited below). Toubias, however powerful in his native district, was a loyal subject of Ptolemy and cultivated the friendship of the minister Apollonios. The position of the cleruchs is not quite clear. One may suppose that the king found it advisable to keep a military force on the east side of the Jordan, to control the country and protect the frontier, and that the soldiers were put under the command of the native chief. Apparently they had allotments in the neighbourhood of his stronghold; but even if these were parcelled out from his land, they were probably held as a gift not from Toubias himself, but from his overlord the king.

> [In the reign of Ptolemy son of Ptole]maios and his son Ptolemy, in the twenty-seventh year,
> [the name of the priest of Alexander... and the] <term>kanephoros</term> <note>original: "κανηφόρου" (*kanephoros*), literally 'basket-bearer,' a prestigious priestess-hood in the cult of the deified Arsinoe Philadelphus.</note> of Arsinoe Philadelphus of those who are in Alexandria,
> [in the district of Am]manitis. Nikanor son of Xenokles, a Knidian, sold
> [to Z]enon son of Agreophon, a Kaunian, of the circle of Apollonios
> <gloss>...lonios or...</gloss>
> 5 [a slave girl] named Sphragis, about seven years old, for fifty drachmae.
> <gloss>military settler</gloss> <note>original: "κληροῦχος"</note>
> [of] those of Toubias [[the horsemen of Toubias, military settler]]. Witnesses:

->VERSO :<-

->Purchase of a slave girl<-

> *Line 11.* One of the latest instances in which the "son" <note>original: "υἱός"</note> appears in the protocol. The formula was changed about the beginning of regnal year 27 (early summer of 259 B. C.).
> *Line 13.* The names of the eponymous priest and priestess, who probably assumed office at the beginning of the regnal year, were not yet known in Palestine. As is frequently, but not always, the case in such deeds, the day of the month is omitted; see <term>LEWALD</term>, *P. Frankfurt*, p. 12.
> *Line 16.* The letter before *onios* in line 5 is either λ or α. My former restoration "something for sale" <note>original: "τινὰ ὤνιον"</note> is unsatisfactory and I have ventured to suggest "Babylonian" <note>original: "Βαβυλώνιον"</note>.
> *Line 17.* Read "guarantor" <note>original: "βεβαιωτής"</note> (<term>Wilcken</term>) or perhaps "so-and-so is the surety" <note>original: "ἔγγυος ὁ δεῖνα"</note>.
> *Line 18.* The letter before *ona* seems to be ι or ν. The space is scarcely sufficient for "so-and-so son of so-and-so of the staff of Dion," but the patronymic may have been omitted; or *-ona* may be a genitive. For "judge" <note>original: "δικαστής"</note>, compare no. 59006, l. 25.
> *Line 19.* Read "land-holding soldier" <note>original: "κληροῦχος"</note>.

Edg., 3; Biblical Review, 1920, pp. 161-202 (Vincent); Archive, VI, p. 449 (Wilcken); Proceedings of the Berlin Academy, 1921, pp. 663-671 (Gressmann); Archive, VII, pp. 61-64 (Willrich); Byzantine-Modern Greek Yearbook, 1921, p. 275 (Deissmann).</note>

### 59004. A flour account. — 0 m. 18 x 0 m. 16. — Date : 259 B. C. (?) (pl. I).

This is an account of the quantities of flour received and used by a party travelling in Palestine. It probably belongs to the beginning of year 27, or the early summer of 259 B. C., when Zenon was himself in Palestine in Apollonios' service. The first column gives a list of eleven towns in which flour was obtained; and this geographical list forms the main interest of the document, especially as there is a presumption that the towns are mentioned in the order in which they were visited. The second column and the two columns of the verso state what quantities were given out from time to time, to bakers and other persons, in a period of about four months, from Audnaios to Xandikos.

The papyrus seems to be nearly complete, though damaged at top and bottom. The writing is along the fibres on the recto and across them on the verso. The account has been checked over and the entries ticked off, the cancelled entries being enclosed in round brackets (represented in my transcription by double square brackets).

The itinerary has been studied by <term>Father F. M. Abel</term> in a very learned and instructive article (see bibliography), but on several points there is still some doubt.

->Column I.<-

| | | |
|---|---|---|
| <margin>5</margin> | Of flou[r | |
| | in Straton's [T]ower | 5 <term>artabae</term> <note>An artaba was a dry measure roughly equal to 30-40 liters</note> |
| | in Jerusalem | 6 |
| | in Jericho | 5 |
| | in Abila | 3 |
| | Surabit . ois | 9 <term>artabae</term> |
| | Lakasa | 14 |
| | Noe | 10 |
| | Eitoum | 7 |

> *Line 5.* Perhaps the Abila opposite Jericho. But <term>Abel</term> prefers to identify it with Abila or Abella in the Decapolis near Gadara.
> *Line 6.* Identified with Umm es-Surab by <term>Abel</term>, who suggests the reading "of the Surabitenes" <note>original: "Σουραβι-τηνοις"</note>. The fourth last letter is more probably τ or λ (e. g. *Surabitylois*).
> *Line 7.* For Lakasa <term>Abel</term> proposes el-Kisweh near Damascus. It is probable that this region still belonged to Egypt (see note on no. 59006, 20), but the identification is nevertheless doubtful.
> *Line 8.* Probably Nawa in the Hauran.
> *Line 9.* The last letter is either iota or the number 6, and the second last letter looks like τ or ν, as there is a horizontal stroke above it. Regarding this stroke as accidental we might read "of the Eitoi" <note>original: "Ειτοις"</note>. Possibly Heit in the Hauran (this and other suggestions in <term>ABEL</term>, *art. cit.*).

*Line 10.* Probably Bethanath, though the reading is not quite certain; in two other papyri we find the alternative spelling "Baitanata."
*Line 11.* Kedish, probably located in Galilee.
*Lines 13-17.* The fractions and the note "the artaba is 1/2 1/4" were added later and seem unnecessary. The initial amounts in Column I use a standard 30-choinix artaba, but the rest of the account converts these into the "expenditure" unit, which is a larger 40-choinix artaba.
*Line 18.* 1/2 artab: or possibly 1 1/2.
*Line 30.* The last two letters of "Alexandria" are scribbled or incomplete.
*Line 32.* Perhaps the name is Nikanor.

### 59006. Account. — 0.305 m. $\times$ 0.33 m. — Date: probably 259 B.C.

Written along the fibres and folded vertically. The back is blank. The list has been checked, most of the names in the first two columns having a slanting stroke against them on the left, while one or two entries are bracketed, a sign that they are to be counted as cancelled.

---

<page-num>11</page-num>
<header>ZENON PAPYRI.</header>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<sig>2.</sig>

The papyrus gives a list of persons to whom pickled fish, <term>mackerel</term> <note>original: "σκόμβροι"</note>, had been sold or given out from the store, somewhere in Palestine and probably not far from Gaza (see line 64). From the titles borne by several of these persons, citadel commander <note>original: "ἀκροφύλαξ"</note>, chief assistant <note>original: "ἀρχυπηρέτης"</note>, captain of the guard <note>original: "φυλακάρχης"</note>, secretary <note>original: "γραμματεύς"</note>, it may be inferred that most of them belonged to a military garrison stationed in these parts. It is possible that in line 2 we have the termination of the name of their garrison town; for *Iemnai* may be an earlier transliteration of the word which appears in Greek authors in the form of Jamnia <note>modern Yavne</note>. In column 3 most of the fish are given out to the cook, probably for the dinner-table of Zenon and his companions, and in lines 64-66 we have a brief record of a journey from the coast to the district of Idumea. It may have been on this occasion that Zenon bought the slaves about whom he writes in no. 59015, on the back.

> ]pnai <note>possibly Jamnia</note>

*Lines 1-2. For example, "we have given] mackerel [to those in Jam]nia." The first of the remaining letters in line 2 looks like π (p), but might also be μ (m). Iemnai occurs in a mistranslated passage of the Septuagint <note>original: "LXX"; the Greek Old Testament</note> (Joshua 15, 46).*
*Line 5. citadel commander: see <term>Polybius</term> <note>Greek historian</note> Book V, 50, 10; <term>Dittenberger</term>, <term>Orientis Graeci Inscriptiones Selectae</term> <note>Select Inscriptions of the Greek East</note>, 254, note 5.*
*Line 20. It seems probable from this entry that Damascus was still included in the Ptolemaic empire. On the date of the expulsion of Dion, see <term>Bouché-Leclercq</term>, <term>History of the Lagids</term> <note>History of the Ptolemaic dynasty</note>, Vol. I, 176; <term>Beloch</term> in <term>Archives for Papyrus Research</term>, Vol. II, 231; <term>Svoronos</term>, <term>Coins of the Ptolemies</term>, 192 following.*

### 59007. Fragments of an account. — (a) 0.19 m. x 0.105 m.; (b) 0.12 m. x 0.09 m.

These are two out of several decayed and discoloured fragments of a long account, stating the quantities of wine given out day by day to certain persons. It resembles 59006 and probably<sup>(1)</sup> belongs to the same series. The other fragments are so mutilated that I do not transcribe them. One of them contains the entry <note>original: "οἴνο]υ Σικελικοῦ βικίον"</note> “a jar of Sicilian wine,” and in another we find <note>original: "μέλι]τος Χαλυβωνίου καψάκ(ης)"</note> “a flask of Chalybonian honey.”

#### (a)
For the chariot horse:
2 <term>kotylai</term> <note>original: "κο"; a small cup or liquid measure, about 0.25 liters</note> of mild wine
To Artemidoros: 3 kotylai
[ ... ] 6 kotylai of mild [wine]
<margin>5</margin> <insert>Taken in advance for daily needs:</insert>
1 1/2 jars <term>banotos</term> <note>original: "βανωτὸς"; a specific type of Egyptian vessel</note> of wine
To the cook: 3 kotylai
To Artemidoros: 3 kotylai
To Nikanor: 1 kotyle of mild wine

*Line 1.* Likely a word for "charioteer" <note>original: "ἁρματίτηι"</note> rather than a proper name. The horse was probably undergoing medical treatment <note>Mixing wine with animal feed was a common ancient veterinary practice.</note>.
*Line 2.* The figures are written throughout above the abbreviation for *kotylai*.
*Lines 5-6.* These lines and the similar entry in (b) have been added. The word <note>original: "προειρέθη"</note> stands for "taken in advance." The symbol <term>L</term> is equivalent to "of this."

### 59008. Account. — 0.275 m x 0.385 m — Date: about 259 B.C. (Plate III).

This is for the most part an account of the quantities of bran given out for the use of mules, camels, and donkeys in Palestine, in the autumn of a certain year, perhaps 259 B.C. It has been much corrected, but probably by the original writer. Herakleides the <term>chariot-manager</term> <note>original: *συνωριστής* (synoristēs); one who manages or drives a pair-horse carriage</note>, the author of *P.S.I.* 406, appears again in column 2, and in column 3 we find a reference to a journey to the Hauran <note>A volcanic plateau in modern-day southwestern Syria and northwestern Jordan.</note>. The animals mentioned in column 2 were no doubt being used for travel and transport, not for agricultural work.

->Column I.<-

Only the following endings are preserved: ] of Patrokles, ] to be, ] -ia, ] 6, ] -ai, [ to -nos, ] days, ] -ps.

->Column II.<-

> Per day, sometimes 4 *choinikes* <note>A *choinix* was a dry measure approximately equal to one liter.</note>, sometimes 3.
> The muleteers to receive them in turn.
> <gloss>has received an order</gloss>
> <insert>and of the bran being received</insert>
> [[from the first of Gorpiaios <note>A month in the Macedonian calendar, roughly August/September.</note> until Hyper-]]
> <gloss>Gorpiaios</gloss>
> 5: 12 sacks per day from the [[[[Hyper]]bere]]-
> [[taios]] until the 30th of Hyperberetaios. <note>Hyperberetaios was the Macedonian month roughly corresponding to September/October.</note>
> [[30th of Hyperberetaios per day]]

*Line 1.* Read 4 *choinikes* and 3 *choinikes*. An allowance of corn, whether for the mules or for the muleteers.
*Line 3.* "Has got an order": to draw so much per day.
*Line 4.* This line is bracketed; the other cancelled lines are deleted in the usual way by a stroke of the pen.
*Line 5.* The word for "vessels" (*aggeia*) here means "sacks".

*Line 8.* Two consecutive months in the Macedonian calendar contained as a rule 59 days.
*Line 10.* "From" added afterwards.
*Lines 11-12.* The term for "chariot-manager" here does not mean the drivers, but qualifies the mules: "carriage mules" as opposed to "pack mules."
*Line 16.* The total was probably 540 rather than 528; the corrections in lines 8 and 9 were made later. The "80" at the end of the line has been corrected.
*Line 18.* Apparently "days" rather than "day," but not quite certain.
*Line 28.* Perhaps "the things recorded" or "the things entered." Apparently "with Milkias," though "of Milkias" is just possible.
*Line 29.* The first letter after the gap is either *r* or *o*. Perhaps "and the fodder."
*Line 30.* "He says not" is required, but may possibly have been inserted later.

### 59010. Account. 
— 0.19 m x 0.32 m. — Date: about 259 B.C.

This papyrus gives an account of the money handled and used by a commercial agent, who had been travelling between Alexandria and the Syrian coast, visiting the towns of Sidon and Askalon <note>Major port cities in modern-day Lebanon and Israel respectively.</note>. It may have been written abroad (see lines 10-11), but this is not certain. Zenon was neither the agent nor the writer (see line 12). The total at the end shows that we have only recovered part of the whole account. The writing is along the fibers and the reverse side is blank.

#### ->Column I.<-

| | |
|---|---|
| [ . ] | ✝ [ ] |
| [ . ]enos | ✝ 13 drachmae |
| having owed | |
| [ ]. [ ]. [ ]ton | ✝ 3 drachmae 2 obols <note>The symbol = represents two obols; there were six obols to a drachma.</note> |
| 5 and the loan which you recovered | |

*Line 1.* ✝[603], if this line began a new paragraph.
*Line 5.* *ekoniso* : 'recovered' is the usual sense of this word in connection with a loan. The money may have been lent to the <term>ship-owners</term> <note>original: ναύκληροι (nauklēroi)</note> by the writer.

*Line 7.* The interest amounts to 1/8th of the loan, but the rate is not stated. Regarding marine loans <note>Loans taken to finance maritime trade, often with high interest due to the risk of shipwreck.</note>, see Wilcken in *Egyptian Journal*, vol. 60, pp. 93, 100.
*Line 12.* Bracketed in original. The last six lines of column 1 are smudged and difficult to decipher.
*Line 15.* The reading is doubtful. Hikesios was an agent of Apollonios <note>The finance minister of Ptolemy II.</note>, stationed abroad (*Italian Society Papyri*, 325, 558).
*Line 17.* For example, *and those to Milkias*.
*Lines 18-20.* Reading and restoration very doubtful; but forty drachmae is a possible price for sixty paper rolls.
*Line 21.* The price of barley seems to have been higher in Palestine than in the interior of Egypt, but in comparing prices the cost of transport has to be reckoned with.
*Line 23.* For the hire, or at least not for the purchase, of camels.
*Line 25.* Syria: compare *Hibeh Papyri*, 51, 5-6, *take Syrian units at six drachmae each*, which is about the same price as here.

### 59011. Fragments of a series of letters. — 0.155 m × 0.15 m.

This papyrus, of which we have only a badly preserved fragment, would have been extremely interesting had it been complete. The letters, which are well written and comparatively free from corrections, may be either copies or carefully prepared drafts. Those on the front side <note>original: recto</note> evidently refer to the Palestinian period of Zenon's career. The second letter in column 1 speaks of Bethanath and Joppa <note>Modern Jaffa</note>, while the first in column 2 seems to be about a consignment of frankincense and myrrh. Those on the back side <note>original: verso</note> are in a similar, perhaps the same hand. They mention Philadelphia <note>A city in the Faiyum region of Egypt named after Ptolemy II Philadelphus.</note>, and the second letter is perhaps concerned with the work of the quarrymen there. It reminds us of the correspondence of Kleon on the same subject (*Petrie Papyri*, II, 4), about which Rostovtzeff has written an interesting but misleading note, based on the unfortunate assumption that the author was Apollonios the finance minister (*Large Estate*, pp. 162-164).
The writing on the front side is along the fibers, while that on the back runs across them. It is not clear whether the letters on the back are contemporary with those on the front or date from a later period (compare no. 59009).

It also describes a series of fragmentary letters regarding the trade of luxury goods like frankincense and labor management in Philadelphia.</summary>
<keywords>Zenon Papyri, maritime loans, Alexandria, Sidon, Ascalon, barley prices, paper rolls, frankincense, myrrh, Philadelphia, Ptolemaic economy, Hikesios</keywords>

### 59012. A valuation of imported goods. — 0.315 m x 0.62 m — Date: May-June, 259 B.C. (Plate IV).

This papyrus contains a detailed valuation of a consignment of goods which had passed through the Customs at Pelusium <note>The eastern gateway city to Egypt, near the Sinai.</note> on their way to Alexandria. They were carried on two vessels commanded by Patron and Herakleides, which probably formed part of Apollonios’ private fleet. The list is divided into four main sections headed: **[Property] of Apollonios**, **[Property] of Iatrokles**, **[Property] of Nikanor** (the importers or recipients) and **ship's goods** <note>original: ναυτικά</note> (the property of the ship or crew). Within these sections, the goods are classed in four grades: **those [taxed at] half** <note>original: ὧν τὸ ἥμισυ</note>, or those which paid 50 per cent import duty; **those [taxed at] a third**; **those [taxed at] a fourth**; and **those [taxed at] a fifth**, which paid 33 1/3, 25, and 20 per cent respectively<note>(1)</note>. In each of these grades, they are subdivided into the articles carried by Patron and those carried by Herakleides. The importance of the text consists in this: besides throwing light on Alexandrian commerce, it reveals, for the first time, the outlines of the Ptolemaic tariff system.

The papyrus is not a <term>customs declaration</term> <note>original: ἀπογραφή</note>, though it may be based on one. It is an account of the total expenses incurred at Pelusium on behalf of the...

<note>(1) See also my note on line 127.</note>

<summary>This page records business correspondence regarding estates in Bethanath and Philadelphia, including a legal oath regarding contractors. It also introduces a major document detailing the Ptolemaic import tariff system for luxury goods arriving at the port of Pelusium.</summary>
<keywords>Bethanath, Joppa, Philadelphia, Pelusium, customs duty, import tariff, frankincense, stone-cutters, Apollonios, Zenon Papyri, Ptolemaic economy</keywords>

<meta>This page follows the introduction of customs duties and administrative records for the Zenon archive. The previous page detailed the transport of luxury goods like frankincense and rose perfume. This section moves into specific itemized lists of imported goods, valuation, and the calculation of various taxes applied at the port of Pelusium.</meta>

<page-num>22</page-num>
<header>CATALOGUE OF THE CAIRO MUSEUM.</header>

various importers. In addition to Customs duty they had some minor taxes to pay: the <term>hekatoste (one-percent tax)</term>, the <term>trierarchema (naval maintenance tax)</term> and the <term>diapylion (gate-toll)</term>. The <term>hekatoste</term>, which may perhaps have been the local harbour dues<note>On the question of harbour dues, see Lehmann-Hartleben, *Harbor Installations*, p. 45, note 1. Compare also no. 59015, front side, lines 39-44.</note>, was an *ad valorem* charge of one per cent on the total value of the goods. The <term>trierarchema</term> and the <term>diapylion</term> were assessed separately on each article, the amounts payable being jotted down in the left margin and added up at the end of each section. When two charges appear opposite the same entry (e. g., 104-106), the sum on the right, which was written first, is the <term>diapylion</term>, that on the left the <term>trierarchema</term>. The latter was an exaction for the upkeep of the navy, and there were various forms of <term>trierarchema</term>; here it was a small charge on imported oil, the other goods not being taxed. The <term>diapylion</term>, which was an inland toll, was levied in the present case on goods leaving Pelusium for Alexandria; but certain of the articles mentioned in the list, particularly those of small value, were for some reason exempted from payment. It is not quite clear on what principle the charges for <term>diapylion</term> and <term>trierarchema</term> were assessed (see introduction to *Papyri Edgar*, 73, of which the above paragraphs are a summary). A fragment of the papyrus, comprising the first half of lines 2-10 and the docket on the back, is in Florence and has been published, though not in full, as no. 327 of the *Papyri Società Italiana*. The writing is along the fibres.

*Line 44. jars (salousia)*: see line 116 and P.S.I., 428, 55. In no. 59014 the word is written *salousia* (which should perhaps be read in place of *saloutia* in *P. Ryl.*, 172, 11 and *Sammelb.*, 1, 25). It appears to mean an earthenware, or at least a water-tight vessel.
*Line 49.* Probably 1 1/4 corrected from 3/4, but I cannot quite reconcile the entries in this and the following line with the charges in no. 59013, 6, 7.
*Lines 56-57.* The total in line 75 makes it probable that there was a small charge here for the <term>gate-toll</term><note>original: διαπύλιον</note>.
*Lines 62-63.* The proposed restoration would give a reasonable price for imported wool (see *P. Edg.*, 107, intro.), but the figure which I have read as 2 is more like 100 or 10. The sum of 600 must be the gate-toll, but is so small that I suspect it has been calculated by mistake on one mina instead of on the whole amount.

->Pelusium.<-

Line 127. The total import duty on Apollonios' consignment minus the <term>pempte</term><note>original: πέμπτη; a one-fifth or 20% tax</note>. One might infer from this that the following figures are a recalculation of this item on a lower scale; and in fact 7 stands in the proportion of 1 : 7 1/2 to the value of the wool. The remaining figures ought then to be the difference between the one-fifth tax and the suggested duty. The real difference, however, is not 1 6-c but 3 3-c. Nevertheless I think that the figures relate in some way to the duty on wool.

# 59013. Porterage account. — 0.17 m x 0.135 m. — Date: 259 B.C. (plate III).

The articles mentioned in this account, including the marginal list, comprise practically all the goods imported by Herakleides for Apollonios with a few additional things. The <term>phoretron</term><note>original: φόρετρον; a fee for carrying goods</note> was probably the charge for porterage between the Mareotic harbour and the store-house in Alexandria rather than that at the ports where the cargo was picked up. The few objects not mentioned in the valuation <note>original: τίμησις</note> appear to have paid no duty at the Customs; but several of them may have counted as ship's furniture, while the others were of small value. Crosses opposite the entries in the left margin of the main account show that the list has been checked over. Written along the fibers.

->Expenditure for laborers through Herakleides the <term>pilot</term><note>original: κυβερνήτου (kybernetou)</note><-
<margin>2nd</margin>
61 Chian [wine jars] at 1/2 obol each; porterage for these: ⊢ 5 drachmae 1/2 obol
<margin>$\times$</margin> 9 jars of dried figs; porterage: 6 — 1/2 obol
<margin>$\times$</margin> 6 jars of meat at — 1/2 obol each; porterage: ⊢ 1 drachma 6 [obols?]
<margin>4th</margin>

<margin>$\times$</margin> a basket of royal walnuts; porterage: 6 [obols?]

**59014. Porterage account.** — (a) 0 m. 09 $\times$ 0 m. 155; (b) 0 m. 22 $\times$ 0 m. 155. — Date: 259 B.C.

This papyrus, which is the porterage account for Apollonios' share of the cargo of Patron, is written in the same hand as no. 59013. Many of the goods can be easily identified with those entered in the *valuation* <note>original: τίμησις</note>. The articles of which there is no mention in the latter—lampstands, beds, table, sideboard etc.—were either not subject to import duty or were admitted free because they counted as ship’s furniture. The account has been checked over in the same way as no. 59013. Written across the fibers.

The upper part of the account is in Florence and has been published in *P. S. I.*, vol. VII, 867. The readings in the second and third lines have been revised by Vitelli, but the word "for the laborers" <note>original: ἐ[ρ]γά(ταις)</note> in the fourth line is merely a conjecture of my own.

> Porterage was paid via Patron the pilot:
> 70 Chian [jars], 4 half-Chian, 3 Thasian
> Porterage for these: 6 drachmae 2 1/2 obols
> 7 half-casks of honey: for the laborers, 6 1/2 obols
> Other items <gloss>and a jar</gloss>
> 5 small tripods: porterage 1 drachma 6 1/2 obols
> <gloss>and for the jar</gloss>
> [[15 half-casks of honey, porterage 6...]]

-> Below is the Cairo portion (b): <-

### 59015 (recto). Account concerning a consignment of oil. — 0.30 m x 0.62 m. — Date: 259 or 258 B.C.

The importer seems to have been Apollonios and the account, which is written along the fibers in a large clear hand, seems to have been drawn up in his office. Its object is to show the value, or nominal value, of the shipment after payment of Customs duty, freight, and minor taxes. Some marginal notes have been added in a smaller hand, whether by Zenon or by an accountant. The fact that the oil was carried in Milesian and Samian jars indicates its origin <note>Miletus and Samos were famous centers of production in the Greek world</note>, and a comparison of the account with no. 59012 leads us to believe that it came by sea to Alexandria and was unloaded at that sea-port. The text contains new and interesting information concerning Alexandrian commerce, shipping expenses, Customs duty and the control of imported oil by the royal monopoly. It has been

<page-num>31</page-num>
<header>ZENON PAPYRI.</header>

commented on in the introduction to *P. Edg.*, 75 and will no doubt be discussed more fully and learnedly in future works on Ptolemaic economy. A few errors of reading and restoration in the first edition are corrected below.

*Line 24.* H[... : perhaps the beginning of a proper name of a ship or captain.
*Line 27. "They report breakages":* This phrase refers to items damaged during transit.
*Line 28.* "Jar" is written as a monogram; as is "fast boat" in line 32.
*Lines 34-35.* The writer forgot to cancel the 'chous' symbol in line 34 and seems to have mistakenly struck out the symbol in line 35. The figure 5 is written above the 'choes' symbol, while 'metretai' is abbreviated.
*Line 37.* The fraction of 4 choes was overlooked in the calculation.
*Line 39.* The one-two-hundredth tax (for the form, compare one-four-hundredth in document no. 59012) may correspond to the one-hundredth tax seen elsewhere, conjectured to be local harbor dues.
*Line 40. "Safe Sailing":* possibly a tax for the upkeep of the Alexandrian lighthouse.

(6) Represents the difference between the above totals.

### **59015 (verso). Drafts of five letters from Zenon (pl. V).**

These drafts were written by Zenon in Alexandria, on the verso <note>The "back" side of a papyrus sheet, often used for drafts or secondary notes</note> of the oil account, either in the summer of 259 B.C. or at least before the summer of 258 B.C. At Marisa in Idumea <note>An ancient district in the southern Levant</note>, in which district he had lately been travelling (cf. no. 59006), he had bought a number of slaves from the stock of a certain Zaidelos, and three of these had escaped on the journey to Egypt. His agent Krotos had now brought him word that the runaway slaves were reported to be with Kollochoutos and his brother Zaidelos and that a hundred drachmæ was demanded as the price of returning them. The drafts are addressed to various persons either residing in Palestine or connected with Palestine: (1) and (2) to Pasikles and Epikrates, asking them to recover the slaves and hand them over to Straton, Zenon’s messenger ; (3) to Peisistratos, to keep Pasikles reminded of his duty ; (4) and (5) to Epainetos and Ammon, to see that Straton is not interfered with and requisitioned by officious authorities.

From the rewards offered in *P. Par.*, 10 one sees that a hundred drachmæ was not an unreasonable sum to pay for the recovery of three slaves. But if Kollochoutos and Zaidelos, the partners who had lately sold the slaves and were now harbouring the runaways, were also the persons who demanded the money (see note on line 5) there would be a considerable difference, morally and legally, between the two cases.

<column-break/>

<page-num>35</page-num>
<header>ZENON PAPYRI.</header>
<sig>5.</sig>

->Column I.<-

To Pasikles. If you are well, it would be good; we ourselves are also in good health. Krotos reported to me that you had written to him that the boys who ran away were being informed upon <gloss>thus also</gloss> <gloss>to him the</gloss> as being with Kolloch[[.. .]] the brother of Zaidelos, 5 and they were asking for [[a mina <note>A mina was equal to 100 drachmae</note>]], in exchange for which they will bring them back, 100 silver drachmae. You would do well, then, to make every effort to have them seized [[so that also...]] and handed over to Straton, the man bringing this letter to you. For by 10 doing this you will place us in your debt. <gloss>it</gloss> [[...]] Whatever you spend [[we will give]] back to you. An <gloss>letter-fee</gloss> embroidered tunic [[has been sold]] 12 [...] if you do not [[wish, write]] [and the] buyer will pay. [And if you] have need of [anything] of the things here in [the country, write to] us; 15 for we shall act as a friend. Farewell.

To Epikrates. While staying in Marisa we purchased from the stock of Zaidelos some slaves, [[of whom have run away]] <gloss>4</gloss> as we were traveling into Egypt <gloss>3 of them boys, of these</gloss> two brothers ran away, [[whose names]] whose [names] 20 and descriptions I have written below for you. [It has been reported] to us that these [are with] Kollochoutos [...] You would do well [then to make] every effort [to have them] <gloss>after</gloss> seized and handed over to Straton. [Whatever you spend for those] who bring them back [...] 25 [...]

*Line 5.* This line may have been written before the preceding line was corrected ; but in any case it is a little doubtful whether the subject to **they were asking** <note>original: αἰτοίησαν</note> is Kollochoutos and Zaidelos or the informers.
*Line 8.* **Straton** : see no. 59018, 3.
*Line 12.* Or **|μαι**. The meaning and restoration of the passage are rather uncertain.
*Line 20.* **descriptions** <note>original: εἰκόνας; literally "images" or physical descriptions used to identify fugitives</note> : compare no. 59076, 6. The descriptions were of course not subjoined in the draft.
*Line 22.* The restoration is rather long. Perhaps merely **you will do**.

Zenon seeks the assistance of local contacts to seize the fugitives and return them via his messenger, Straton, for a reward of 100 drachmae.</summary>

->Column II.<-

To Peisistratus. If you are in good health, it would be well; we ourselves are also healthy. Crocus reported to us that Pasicles has written that the runaway slaves, whom we bought [[t]] in Marisa [[from]] among those belonging to Zaidēlos, are being tracked. We have written, therefore, asking you to take every care so that they might be apprehended and that he might hand them over to Strato, the man who is bringing [[the]] the letter<gloss>s</gloss> to you. Therefore, you would do well to both remind him and to assist him <insert>with zeal</insert> so that [[the slaves]] do not escape. And you will do us a favor by writing if you want anything from the country; for we will act for you in a friendly manner. Farewell.

To Epainetos. Certain slaves of [[ours]] happen to have run away from us, who have been reported to be in Idumaea <note>A region in the southern Levant, south of Judea</note>, and we have dispatched Strato for this purpose. You would do well, therefore, by instructing your son not to trouble him regarding his public duties <note>original: λειτουργίας (leitourgias); these were compulsory state services or taxes</note>, so that he may apprehend the slaves.

To Ammon. The same <note>original: τὴν αὐτήν; understand: "The same letter"</note>.
[[So that to Dorotheos and Demainetos]]
You would do well, therefore, by writing to Dorotheos and Demainetos so that he is not troubled regarding his public duties.

*Line 26.* Read "Peisistratos."
*Line 36.* "make good their escape."
*Line 42.* "for this purpose": or possibly "for them."
*Line 43.* The papyrus has "son," but "son," used insultingly of some official, does not seem likely in this context.
*Line 44.* Compare *Tebtunis Papyri* 32, 3: "do not trouble Asclepiades regarding his travel duties."
*Line 45.* To Ammon: or to Ammonios. — "The same": understand: "the same letter."

**59016. Letter from Demetrios to Zenon.** — 0 m. 12 × 0 m. 245. — Date: about 30th December, 259 B.C. (Plate VI).

> Demetrios, a clerk <note>original: γραμματεύς (grammateus)</note> stationed in Cyprus, had gone over to Phoenicia on some business, and having expended his travelling allowance on purchases in Tyre he begs Zenon to send him 150 drachmae to Beirut. Zenon himself at this time was probably leading a wandering life, as Demetrios asks him where he will have to send him the money. The Charmos mentioned in line 4 was one of the ubiquitous commercial agents employed by Apollonios.

Demetrios to Zenon, greetings. Having made certain purchases in Tyre, I have spent my travel allowance. You would do well, therefore, to give to Nicadas, the man who has delivered this letter to you, 150 drachmae. Charmos happened to leave before me, otherwise I would have sent him with him. Take care also that you send Nicadas to Beirut with safety. <gloss>And</gloss> write to me where I will need to send the small change <note>original: κερμάτιον (kermation); meaning the repayment of the sum</note>.
Farewell. Year 27, 13th of Apellaios.

regarding 150 drachmae.

### 59018. Letter from Alexandros (?) to Oryas. — 0 m. 165 x 0 m. 27. — Date: about 5th April, 258 B.C.

The incident described in this letter took place, without much doubt, in Palestine. Zenon had written to Oryas to recover some money from a Jew called Jeddous <note>Jeddous is a Greek rendering of the Hebrew name Yaddua</note>, or else to seize securities for it. Oryas sent on Zenon's messenger, a man called Straton, with a copy of the letter, to Alexandros. The latter having taken a dose of medicine was unable to leave his house (or perhaps he did not relish the task), but he sent a youth along with Straton to give a note to Jeddous and to recover the money. The two returned empty-handed, reporting that Jeddous had paid no attention to the note, but had assaulted them and driven them out of the village. «I have therefore reported to you», says Alexandros, declining further responsibility.
Probably the transaction between Zenon and Jeddous was a private one, while Oryas and Alexandros belonged to the local administration, Oryas being the superior officer. The letter gives a good picture of the unruly Jewish character.

[Alexan]dros to Oryas, greetings. I received the letter from y[ou],
[in which you] subjoin[ed] for me the letter wr[itten] from Zenon to Jeddous,
[stating that], if he does not pay the mon[ey] to Straton, the one [arri]ving from Zenon,
we might po[int] out his securities to him. I, for my part, happened
5 [to be ill] because I was undergoing a course of medicine <gloss>k</gloss>, but I sent along [with St]raton
a youth [from] us and I wr[o]te a letter to Jeddous. When they
arrived,
[then they tel]l me that he had paid no attention to [my] let[ter], but had
laid [hands] on them and cas[t] them out of the village. I have therefore written to you.
->Farewell. Year 27 <note>original: Lκζ; referring to the 27th year of the reign of Ptolemy II Philadelphus</note>, 20th of the intercalary month of Peritios <note>The Macedonian calendar added an extra month, "intercalary Peritios," periodically to stay aligned with the seasons</note>.<-

VERSO. ->To Oryas.<-

<note>Line 1. Alexandros fits into the lacuna, but of course other names are possible.</note>
<note>Line 2. "You wrote off" (original: ἀπέγραψας) in the first edition was a slip, corrected by Wilcken. — "and" (original: τε) is used here in the sense of 'also'.</note>
<note>Lines 2-3. Or "written in order that" (original: γεγραμμένην ἵνα).</note>
<note>Line 6. "Those of mine" (original: Τῶν ἐμῶν) in the first edition.</note>

BIBLIOGRAPHY: *Museum Accession Journal, no. 48467; P. Edg. 4; Biblical Review, 1920, p. 168.*

### 59019. Letter to Zenon. — 0 m. 15 x 0 m. 30. — Date: 260-258 B.C. (pl. VI).

The loquacious writer, whose name is lost, speaks in the first part of the letter about

<column-break/>

<page-num>39</page-num>
<header>ZENON PAPYRI.</header>

an expert who had been called in to cure dice made of gazelles' bones and who according to his own story had been employed by members of the Court, including Antipatros the Etesian. In the second part he tells about a dispute between Patron the captain of a merchant vessel (see no. 59012) and a trader or commercial agent called Apollophanes. The letter was probably written in Alexandria, shortly after Zenon had started on a voyage, and it evidently belongs to the early years of the correspondence.
The text was originally published in *P.S.I.*, vol. IV, no. 444, from a copy made by Gentilli but not revised by him before publication. I have not thought it necessary to note the various points in which our readings and restorations differ. The division of lines 1-4, as printed below, differs slightly from that adopted in the *Annales*.

[. . . . .]l[. .]s to Zenon, greetings. If you are well, it would be good; I myself am also healthy. After 〈you〉 sailed out, I brought in the [one]
[trea]ting the gazelle-bone dice, and having inspected them he said they had been taken from raw meat, and because of this they had [failed]
them. He said it was not possible for them to be made wax-like, for after a year he said they would change [deteriorate],
[but] he said he would make them [. . .]ly, though he said it would take much trouble, so that he said [they were] not worthy of such [effort].
5 [Regard]ing the treatment, we shall try to have them done for a copper coin <term>chalkous; a very small denomination</term> each, or if not, for two copper coins; for he himself says [he treats]
the knucklebone <term>astragalos; a bone used as a four-sided die</term> at the court for [half an ob]ol; and he said you could ask Antipatros
the Etesian, for he sa[id] he had [trea]ted them for [hi]m.
You then, as soon as you receive the letter, wr⟦. ⟧ite
to me about these things . . . . . . . . [. . . .]
[be]fore the time runs out. Know also that Patron is not willing to take
Apollophanes on board, b[ut troub]le—

<note>Line 1. 〈you〉: the papyrus has "you" (original: σο) instead of "for you to" (original: τὸ σε).</note>
<note>Line 2. The last word began with g or p. Though "to have suffered/labored" (original: πεπονηκέναι) is suitable, it is rather long.</note>
<note>Lines 3-4. E.g., "becom]ing" or "suitab]le." Or "wool-like"?</note>
<note>Line 5. 'We shall try to have them done for a copper coin each, or at most for two copper coins'.</note>
<note>Line 6. Antipatros: nephew of Kassandros and formerly king of Macedonia, where he had reigned for no more than forty-five days. This being the duration of the Etesian winds <note>Seasonal Mediterranean winds that blow for 40 days in summer</note>, he received the nickname of "The Etesian." Twenty years after his brief sovereignty we find him an idle refugee playing dice at the court of Ptolemy. See Wilcken's note, cited below.</note>
<note>Line 7. "To take on board" (original: ἀναλαμβάνειν): 'to take as a passenger'.</note>

<summary>This page contains two letters: one reporting a violent confrontation in a Palestinian village over a debt owed to Zenon, and another discussing the repair of gazelle-bone dice and maritime travel arrangements.</summary>

### 59021. Fragment of a letter to Zenon. — 0.085 m x 0.19 m. — Date: August or September, 258 B.C.

From the mention of "to the sister" <note>original: τῆι ἀδελφῆι—in this period "sister" was often used as an affectionate term for a wife</note> in line 3, one is tempted to connect this letter with no. 59029 and no. 59052. But the handwriting is not that of Antimenes. It resembles that of a fragment which I have seen in the British Museum, written by a certain [...]esilaos, and it also resembles that of no. 59019, but not very closely.

"If you are in good health, it would be well; we ourselves are also healthy. [He/She] declined us [...] him. For it was useful also for us so that [we might] bring [...] in the former journey fleeces <note>original: κοίδια (koidia)—meaning sheepskins or perhaps bedspreads</note> and a garment for the sister [...] For the autumn he will be with you bringing some of the soft [wine?] <note>original: μαλακοῦ—likely referring to a mild or sweet wine</note> [...]"
Year 28, [Month of] Panemos. ->Fare[well].<-

### 59021. Letter from Demetrios to Apollonios. — 0.305 m x 0.33 m. — Date: about 24th October, 258 B.C. (plate VII).

Demetrios, who was probably head of the Alexandrian mint, informs Apollonios <note>Apollonios was the *dioiketes*, the highest financial official in Egypt under Ptolemy II</note> that

---

<page-num>41</page-num>
<header>ZENON PAPYRI.</header>
<sig>6</sig>
<meta>Catalogue of the Museum, no. 59001.</meta>

following his instructions he has received a certain quantity of gold and converted it into new coin. The gold was brought to him from abroad in accordance with a decree, of whose exact terms we are ignorant. But owing to a certain obstacle the amount was not nearly so much as he might have obtained. When merchants and other people brought him foreign money and pentadrachms <note>a large silver or gold coin worth five drachmae</note> of the old issue, all in good condition, there was no difficulty. Such coins were accepted at their recognized value, were at once melted down and re-coined, and the owners received an equivalent amount in new money. But when they brought him gold plate, he could not accept it, as he had no assaying office <note>a facility to test the purity of the metal</note> to which to refer it. His business was to coin gold, not to appraise it. So the gold was lying idle and the merchants were dissatisfied. In the next place (line 29) there was in Alexandria itself a good deal of worn gold money which tradesmen refused to accept, and there was no assaying office at which people could have their worn gold valued and receive its value in good gold or silver. Thus still more gold was being wasted which might have been used by the mint. Demetrios therefore asks Apollonios to write to the king in order that an assayer may be appointed for such cases (for both cases, according to my view; for the latter case only, according to Schubart).

Such seems to me to be the probable meaning of the text; but lines 15-23 are difficult both to decipher and to understand, and my reading may well have to be amended. Schubart has proposed a very different explanation of lines 16-18. Reading "but to accept from the one providing security," he paraphrases: <note>original German: « Der Erlass befiehlt, Schalen zu erfassen und zu verarbeiten, aber nur von dem anzunehmen, der Gewähr leistet, nämlich für den Feingehalt des Metalls »</note> "The decree orders [us] to take and process bowls, but to accept them only from one who provides a guarantee, specifically for the fineness of the metal." But this is very awkward Greek, and the whole interpretation, though ingenious, strikes me as too far-fetched. Segrè also has propounded a different view of the same passage, reading a good deal more into the text than I can find there. See the bibliography below. I am afraid the final word still remains to be said about this interesting problem.

The letter was written in Alexandria and was probably received in the Fayoum <note>the agricultural oasis where Zenon managed a large estate</note>, where Apollonios and Zenon were staying in the month of Gorpiaios. As usual in the case of short-lined letters, the writing is along the fibers and the papyrus was folded across them. It has been possible to complete some of the lines which are defective on the front (recto) by the impressions which the ink has left on the back (verso). The signs read in this way are given in my transcription without any distinguishing mark.

->Column I.<-

->To Apollonios, greetings from Demetrios.<-
->It is well if you yourself are healthy and<-
->other things are to your liking.<-
->I also, just as you wrote to me,<-
<margin>5</margin> ->am seeing to it and have received<-

<summary>This page contains fragments of Zenon's correspondence, including a domestic note about clothing and wine, and a significant letter from Demetrios, likely the master of the Alexandrian mint. Demetrios discusses technical difficulties in re-coining foreign gold and gold plate due to the lack of an official assaying office.</summary>
<keywords>Zenon, Apollonios, Demetrios, Alexandria, gold coinage, minting, assaying, currency reform, Ptolemaic Egypt, Gorpiaios, Fayoum</keywords>

The letter addresses technical difficulties in processing foreign gold and the exchange of old currency for new royal coinage.</meta>

...from <unclear>gold</unclear> and having processed it, I gave it back. We would have accepted even many times more, but as I wrote to you before, both the foreigners
<margin>10</margin> who sail in and the merchants and the wholesalers <note>original: ἐγδοχεῖς (egdocheis)—intermediaries who received and stored goods for distribution</note> and others bring both the local currency <note>original: ἐπιχώριον νόμισμα (epichōrion nomisma)—referring to the coinage of the merchants' home regions</note> in full weight and the "three-gold" coins <note>likely gold staters or heavy gold coins</note>, so that it may become new [currency] for them, according to the decree which
<margin>15</margin> orders us to receive and process it. Since this person <note>perhaps a specific official or the person previously mentioned in the lost part of the text</note> does not allow me to accept gold bowls <note>original: φιάλας (phialas)—shallow ceremonial bowls</note>, and as we do not have anyone to whom we should make a report <note>original: ἀναφοράν (anaphoran)—an official referral or appeal to a higher authority</note>
<margin>20</margin> concerning these matters, we are forced not to accept them. And the people are indignant because neither at the [exchange] banks <note>original: τραπεζῶν (trapezōn)</note> nor into our scales <note>original: τάλαντα (talanta)—though unusual in this sense, the context implies weighing equipment for currency</note> do we receive them, nor are they able to send [gold] into the country

*Line 6.* Read "from gold" (chrysiou). What the following number refers to is not quite clear. Perhaps the equivalent of 57,000 gold coins or staters.
*Line 8.* The second 'o' in "many times more" (pollaplasion) is written over an 'a'.
*Line 11.* *Egdocheis*: see Dittenberger's note on *O.G.I.S.*, 140, 9 for the distinction between merchants (*emporoi*) and wholesalers (*egdocheis*).
*Line 12.* *Epichōrion*: this must mean the local money of the foreigners, not of the Alexandrians.
*Line 16.* The reading given here, which is partly due to Grenfell, corresponds better with the traces of letters on the back of the papyrus than Schubart's restoration. I take "this" (*toude*) to refer to the following clause "as we do not have...", used ungrammatically but intelligibly for "because we do not have."
*Line 19.* I have followed Schubart in reading "we are forced" (*anagkazometha*) in place of "being forced" (*anagkazomenoi*)—either is possible based on the handwriting—but have retained "these" (*tautas*) in preference to "so many" (*tosautas*). Grammatically "so many" is better, but "these" seems to be the word required. In any case, the writer appears to have lost himself in the maze of an overlong sentence.
*Line 21.* "At" (*epi*): or perhaps, as Grenfell proposes, "of the."
*Lines 22-23.* Schubart rightly remarks that there is no authority for the use of *talanta* in the sense of "scales" in Hellenistic Greek, but I have tried in vain to find a suitable word of the same length beginning with 't' and ending in 'ta'. "Valuables" (*timēta*) seems just possible, or "melted items" (*tēkta*).

<margin>25</margin> for [payment of] goods, but they claim to have their gold lying idle and to be suffering no small loss,

->Column II.<-

having sent for it from abroad, and not being able to easily dispose of it even to others at a lower price. Even everyone in the city
<margin>30</margin> uses worn gold coins <note>original: ἀποτετριμμένωι χρυσίωι (apotetrimmenōi chrysioi)—coins that have lost weight due to wear and tear</note> with difficulty. For none of them has anyone to whom they can make a report and, by paying a small fee <note>literally "adding something"</note>, receive <gloss>either</gloss> good gold or silver in its place. For now, as things stand this

*Line 28.* This line is inserted between lines.
*Line 39.* "You might write" (*grapsēs*): the 'ē' is corrected from 'e'.
*Lines 45-46.* "Without expense on his part."
*Line 49.* There are traces on the back of the papyrus, but I cannot read them into words.

--- Page 30 ---
<meta>This page continues the cataloging of the Zenon Papyri, moving from official correspondence about minting to a specific, detailed private money account. It provides crucial evidence for the fluctuating exchange rates between gold and silver in the mid-3rd century BCE.</meta>

<page-num>44</page-num>
<header>CATALOGUE OF THE CAIRO MUSEUM.</header>

# 59022. A money account. — 0.405 m. x 0.18 m. — Not dated (Plate VIII).

This account, which probably belongs to the 28th or 29th year of Ptolemy II Philadelphus <note>reigned 283–246 BCE</note>, gives us the ratio between gold and silver at that time. The <term>mnaieia</term> <note>original: μναιεῖα—literally "one-mina coins," gold octadrachms worth 100 silver drachmae</note> and the <term>pentekontadrachma</term> <note>original: πεντηκοντάδραχμα—50-drachma coins, gold tetradrachms</note> are the gold octadrachms and tetradrachms of the new issue. Nominally equal to 100 and to 50 silver drachmae, they were really exchangeable at 104 and 52. Thus the nominal ratio of gold to silver was 12 1/2 : 1, and the market ratio, if we assume that an octadrachm was equal in weight to eight silver drachmae, was 13 : 1. But comparing the actual weights of the coins we find that in reality coined gold was worth 13 1/3 times its weight in coined silver. The <term>trikhrysa</term> <note>original: τρίχρυσα—literally "three-gold" coins, the older gold pentadrachms</note> are the old pentadrachms issued by Ptolemy I Soter and Philadelphus, and the value of 66 2/3 silver drachmae assigned to them here corresponds exactly with the above rate. <term>trikhryson</term> means a coin worth three <term>chrysoi</term> <note>gold staters</note>, and in Alexandria the word <term>chrysous</term> was currently used as the equivalent of 20 silver drachmae, though there was no gold coin of that value⁽¹⁾. The nominal value of the <term>trikhryson</term> was 60 silver drachmae (see line 6), which indicates that formerly the ratio between gold and silver had been 12 : 1.

As for the general meaning of the text, lines 1-9 give a detailed account of a sum of money owned by the writer, or rather by his employer, the total being 2 talents, 2,124 drachmae (this total is probably incorrect, see note on line 9). Lines 10-20 state that out of this sum 2 talents in gold of various denominations had been deposited in the bank of Stratokles. The gold coins did not exactly make up the amount of 2 talents; there remained a balance of 10 drachmae 4 obols in favor of the depositor, and this he had drawn out of the bank. Below is a line in smaller handwriting, evidently a correction of lines 10-20. The gist of it is that the gold deposited in the bank was less by one octadrachm than the amount mentioned above, so that, instead of 10 drachmae 4 obols having been withdrawn, a sum of 93 drachmæ 2 obols had been paid in to complete the two talents.

The account is written in a large, elegant hand across the fibers, the breadth of 18 cm. probably representing half the height of the roll.

1. There are 37 <term>mnaieia</term> <note>gold octadrachms</note>
2. and the agio <note>original: ἐπαλλαγή (epallage)—the exchange premium</note> on this: 148 drachmae
3. of <term>pentekontadrachma</term> <note>50-drachma coins</note>: 46 1/2 minae <note>one mina = 100 drachmae</note>
4. and the agio on this: 186 drachmae
5. Total: 1 talent, <gloss>2</gloss>884 drachmae <note>The scribe originally wrote a different number and corrected it</note>
6. and 85 <term>trikhrysa</term> <note>old gold pentadrachms</note>, of which the value is 51 minae
7. and the agio on this

<margin>Line 2. *apallage* in the first edition was a misprint.</margin>

> ⁽¹⁾ See *Aegyptus*, IV, p. 79, where I have pointed out an example of this use in a Zenon papyrus, *P. Michigan* 45.

<column-break/>

<page-num>45</page-num>
<header>ZENON PAPYRI.</header>

8. of the <term>trikhryson</term>: 66 [drachmae] 4 obols — [Total] 5566 drachmae 4 obols —
9. and for the same total: 2 talents, 8,124 drachmae
10. ->From this:<-
11. A deposit at Diospolis at the royal
12. bank where Stratokles is [cashier]: 2 talents of silver
13. of these, 15 <term>mnaieia</term>, and the agio on this: 60 drachmae
14. of <term>pentekontadrachma</term>: 46 minae
15. and the agio on this: 184 drachmae
16. 85 <term>trikhrysa</term>, of which the value is 51 minae
17. the agio of this <term>trikhryson</term>: 66 [drachmae] 4 obols — [Total] 5566 drachmae 4 obols —
18. and for the same the total becomes: 2 talents, 10 drachmae 4 obols —
19. ->of this:<-
20. we have received: 16 drachmae 4 obols — the remainder the banker holds: 2 talents

Below, in a smaller hand:
21. 106, 58, 1 talent 240 drachmae and for the <term>trikhrysa</term> <gloss>60</gloss> 66 drachmae 4 obols — and there has been added 93 drachmae 2 obols = 2 talents

22. <margin>VERSO <note>the back of the papyrus</note>:</margin> ->Payment from the money-pouch.<-

*Line 9.* In this total the 85 *trikhrysa* are counted as 51 minae (5100 drachmae) plus the agio, but the agio is calculated on 51 instead of 85, though it had been correctly given in the preceding line. See also line 17.
*Line 11.* Probably Diospolis Inferior, the town where Demetrios Phalereus was buried. For its site, at Tell el Balamoun near Ras el Khalig, see Hogarth in *J. H. S.*, XXIV, p. 11. Apollonios and Zenon were in this corner of the Delta in the spring of 257 BCE, though it is not certain that the account was connected with their journey.
*Line 13.* The depositor paid in the whole of his smaller gold coins and kept 133, or more probably 134, of his octadrachms.
*Line 20.* "holds": i.e., the banker.
*Line 21.* The first letters are illegible, but we can safely read (of <term>mnaieia</term>), (of <term>pentekontadrachma</term>). I have corrected "prosaorithmeta" of the first edition to *proserithmetai* <note>original: προσηρίθμηται—has been added to the sum</note>.
*Line 22.* 'Sum paid in from the money-bag'. Cf. *P. Teb.*, 112, 31, 33, "from the purse" and no. 59010, 27.

BIBLIOGRAPHY: *Journal d’entrée du Musée*, no. 48471; *P. Edg.*, 59; *Zeitschrift für Numismatik*, XXXIII, p. 68 ff. (Schubart); *Mitteil. der num. Gesellsch. in Wien*, XV, 164-166 (Kubitschek); SEGRÈ, *Circolazione tolemaica*, p. 25 (Segrè had not yet seen the whole text and, as he tells me in a private letter, does not agree with the above explanation of line 9).

<summary>This page details an ancient Egyptian money account from the Zenon archive, providing a rare look at the complex exchange rates and premiums (agio) between gold and silver coins under Ptolemy II. It records a massive deposit of over 2 talents into a royal bank at Diospolis, managed by the banker Stratokles.</summary>
