**59024. Letter from Kriton to Zenon.** — 0.082 m. x 0.32 m. — Date of reception: about 29th October, 258 B.C. (?) (plate IX).

Kriton, who asks Zenon to redeem a pledge for an absconding sailor and send him back to his boat, was the <term>stolarchēs</term> <note>original: στολάρχης—a commander of a fleet or admiral</note> of Apollonios’ little fleet. I have assigned the letter to year 28, because there is evidence that he and Zenon were at that time in close touch with each other, and I assume that he accompanied or occasionally joined Apollonios on the tour and looked after the boats (compare *P. S. I.*, 858, 11). If that is right, the letter was written while the party was still in the Fayoum or about to leave it (*Edgar, Papyri*, 77). But so far as we know, year 29 is also a possible date for the letter; or it may even be earlier than year 28.

<column-break/>

<page-num>47</page-num>
<header>ZENON PAPYRI.</header>

Kriton to Zenon, greetings. One of the sailors has deserted. If therefore he has been left behind because of some security-pledges <note>original: ἐνέχυρα—items left as collateral for a debt</note>, give him 4 drachmae <note>The symbol ⊢ represents drachmae</note> so that he may recover the pledge, and send him back as quickly as possible.

->On the back:<-

Year 28, Gorpiaios <note>A Macedonian month</note> 20. To Zenon.
Kriton: 4 drachmae.

<meta>BIBLIOGRAPHY: Museum Entry Journal, no. 48475; Edgar, Papyri, 17.</meta>

**59025. Letter from Archelaos to Kriton.** — 0.315 m. x 0.10 m.

Archelaos, who was starting on a voyage, asks Kriton the <term>stolarchēs</term> <note>admiral</note> to buy him two spars and a boat with three sculls and to look after his family in his absence. It is probable that the letter was written in Alexandria in year 28 or 29.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;therefore and the gods, do not
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;hesitate to go to the

### 59026. Two fragments of a letter to Zenon. — (a) 0.095 m. x 0.095 m.; (b) 0.15 m. x 0.055 m. — Date: 258 or 257 B.C.

### 59027. Letter from Aristeus to Apollonios. — 0.09 m. x 0.33 m. — Date: about 26th October, 258 B.C. (plate IX).

Aristeus, an employee in the household of Apollonios, informs his master that, by order of Amyntas, he has paid the wages of the servants <note>original: σώμασιν—literally "bodies," a common term for slaves or household staff</note> left behind in Alexandria. Apollonios and Zenon had been travelling in the <term>chora</term> <note>original: χώρα—the Egyptian countryside outside Alexandria</note> for the last three months and were now in the Fayoum (*P. Edg.*, 77). For Kallianax the carpenter, see *P. S. I.*, 483. A clay sealing was found loose inside this letter. It bore a representation of Athena Promachos <note>the goddess Athena depicted as a warrior "fighting in the front line"</note> to the right, with shield and spear.

Aristeus to Apollonios, greetings. Amyntas having given us instructions, we have paid the wages to the staff left behind for two months, and to Kallianax the carpenter for three months. I have therefore also written to Zenon so that he may know. Farewell. Year 28, Gorpiaios 17.

**Verso:**
| | |
|---|---|
| To the <term>dioiketes</term> <note>original: διοικητῆι—the chief financial official of Egypt</note>. | From Aristeus. |
| 5 Year 28, Hyperberetaios. | To Apollonios. |
| [Wages] for the staff. | |

On the other side of the same fold, in the opposite direction, an obliterated address:
[[To Python ...]]

**Verso.** The title <term>dioiketes</term> goes with the address and "From Aristeus" with the filing note.

*Bibl.: Museum Entry Journal, no. 48473.*
Catalogue of the Museum, no. 59001.

### 59028. Letter from Satyra to Zenon. — 0.145 m. x 0.295 m. — Date: 258 B.C. (?) (pl. IX).

The girl called Satyra was a <term>kitharōdos</term> <note>original: κιθαρῳδός—a professional performer who sang while playing the kithara, a type of lyre</note> or harper in the Alexandrian household of Apollonios. In the present letter, which is more emphatic than grammatical, and which was probably written in year 28 when Apollonios and Zenon were travelling in the <term>countryside</term> <note>original: χώρα (chōra)—the Egyptian provinces outside the capital of Alexandria</note>, she complains on behalf of herself and her mother that they have received no dress allowance for more than a year and very little in the way of salary. In two other papyri, which may be of later date, we hear of her receiving both <term>wages</term> <note>original: ὀψώνιον (opsōnion)—wages or a food allowance</note> and linen garments (nos. 59059, 59087).

Satyra to Zenon, greetings. Since Apollonios gave instructions to give us a dress allowance, both to me and my mother—and you will also find the memorandum that Apollonios wrote about these matters—we have received nothing since that time, and this is now the second year. You would therefore do well to look into this and point it out to Apol-
loni-
<margin>5</margin> [os] . . . . . . [ ] to make mention of us, so that we are not naked. And let him grant this to be held through you personally. And regarding the salary, we have received nothing at all except once, and that which you sent to the Demetrios family to give to us. You would therefore do well to look <gloss>look into</gloss> <note>The writer appears to have repeated the verb for emphasis or as an interlinear correction</note> into these things also, if it seems good to you, as quickly as possible.
->Farewell.<-

Verso:
From Satyra ->to Zenon.<-
<margin>10</margin> From the girl.

*Line 1.* dress allowance <note>original: ἱματισμόν</note>: either an allowance of actual clothing or a cash sum for clothing (*P. S. I.*, 571, 16).
*Line 6. See* Wilcken, *U. P. Z.*, no. 24, 20-21, "I ask . . . that you give these things to me personally." The interpolated "look into" is superfluous.
*Line 7.* the Demetrios family <note>original: τοῖς Δημητρίοις</note>: an interesting reference to the Alexandrian festival <note>The Demetreia, a festival for the goddess Demeter</note> described in one of the hymns of Callimachus.

BIBL.: *Journal d’entrée du Musée*, no. 48474; *P. Edg.*, 78.

### 59029. Letter from Antimenes (?) to Zenon. — 0.115 m. x 0.225 m. — Date: November, 258 B. C. (pl. X).

Zenon’s correspondence is almost all about business matters, but this letter is a pleasant exception. Comparing it with no. 59052, which is in the same hand and seems to refer to the same subject, I have ventured to restore the text and to interpret it as follows.

<column-break/>

<page-num>51</page-num>
<header>ZENON PAPYRI.</header>

Zenon came from Kaunos in Caria <note>An ancient region in what is now southwestern Turkey</note> and had many friends and relations living there. Although he himself was too busy to visit his native country during his service under Apollonios, other members of the family occasionally made the voyage from Caria to Egypt, or from Egypt to Caria. The present letter, which was written by a friend of his called Antimenes, sends him word about the voyage of a lady in whom he evidently took an affectionate interest. From no. 59052 we gather that her name was Doris and that she was escorted by Ariston. Now in *P. Edg.* 54 Ariston is mentioned in a letter from Caria along with Epharmostos, whom we know to have been Zenon’s younger brother, and it therefore seems very probable that Ariston was another of his brothers or at least that he and Doris and Zenon were all closely related. Whether she was sailing to Egypt or elsewhere is not quite clear (see no. 59052), but we may suppose that she embarked at Kaunos. A sea voyage in those days, especially in the winter season, was apt to prove eventful, and we shall learn from a later communication from Antimenes how the travellers fared.

It is scarcely necessary to add that the restorations given below are only suggestions.

[Antimene]s to Zenon, greetings. If you are well, it would be good; I too am healthy. At the [beginning, indeed]
[because] the son of Herakleides, Zenon, frequently misled us and because [Doris]
[fell ill] and was unable to sail, we hesitated to write to you; but now know that [with Aris]-
[ton] we have sent her off in the boat of Zenon, and we [enjoined] [Zenon]
<margin>5</margin> to take every care of her, and we ourselves provided [her] [with all she requested] for the voyage.
->Farewell. Year 28, Hyperberetaios<- <note>original: Ὑπερβερεταίου—a month in the Macedonian calendar, roughly November in this context</note>

Verso: ->To Zenon,<-
->subordinate of Apollonios.<-

*Line 1.* Probably not "in Arsinoe," as in no. 59052 Sosipatros writes to Antimenes from Arsinoe.

BIBL.: *Journal d’entrée du Musée*, no. 48476.

---

### 59030. Letter from Amyntas to Zenon. — 0.13 m. x 0.19 m. — Date: about 4th December, 258 B. C.

The letters of Amyntas, like those of Apollonios, are written in several different hands.

The writing of the present fragment is exactly the same as that of no. 59039 and quite different from that of no. 59044 (pl. XIV). As regards the subject of the letter, the <term>small horses</term> <note>original: ἱππάρια (hipparia)—literally "ponies" or small horses</note> which he had been ordered to send from Alexandria were no doubt required by Apollonios and his party for inland excursions. > 10 the [small]
> horses.

*Line 2.* Doubtful whether "therefore" or "not."
*Line 3.* The "o" of "the" has been corrected from "ō."
*Line 4.* Perhaps "beside what is necessary," or perhaps "the groom"; but the reading is uncertain.
*Line 8.* My first copy gives **Hyperberetaios 27**, but the end of the line is now lost.
*Lines 9-11.* Probably a note of the sender.

### 59031. Letter from Poseidonios to Apollonios. — 0.355 m. x 0.175 m. — Date: about 28th December, 258 B.C. (pl. XI).

The writer was a member of the Alexandrian court, holding the title of <term>king's taster</term> <note>original: ἐδέατρος (edeatros)—an official responsible for the royal table or tasting the king's food</note>, perhaps the father of the Arsinoe who had been the eponymous priestess <note>The priestess for whom the year was officially named in the dating formula</note> in year 26. He complains that the employees of Sosistratos, the head of the <term>guard post</term> <note>original: φυλακή (phylakē)—here referring to a customs or toll station</note> at Memphis, had confiscated and sold some iron which they had found on one of his boats, whereas according to Poseidonios the iron was really a necessary part of the boat's equipment. The letter is written in the tone of a man speaking to his equal and no doubt received Apollonios' attention, especially as the latter was at this very time staying in Memphis. As the letter remained in Zenon's hands, it may be that Zenon was ordered verbally to inquire.

<column-break/>

<page-num>53</page-num>
<header>ZENON PAPYRI.</header>

> Poseidonios to Apollonios
> greetings. From a certain grain-ship
> of ours sailing up
> to the <term>countryside</term> <note>original: χώρα (chōra)—the Egyptian provinces outside the capital, Alexandria</note>, those [men]
> 5 under Sosistratos boarded it at Memphis
> and took away the iron which was necessary to have
> on the boat for emerging
> needs; for it seems
> to me to be impossible to sail the ships
> 10 without the necessary
> equipment.
> And although I wrote to them many times
> to return it, they paid no attention, but sold it.
> I have therefore written to you so that you may know
> 15 what sort of people those stationed at the guard posts
> are.
> ->Farewell. Year 28, Dios 21.<- <note>Dios: A Macedonian month, roughly corresponding to December.</note>

->VERSO:<-
> ->From Poseidonios &emsp;&emsp;&emsp; To Apollonios.<-
> ->the king's taster, regarding<-
> ->Sosistratos<-
> ->20 the tax collector.<-

*Line 2. From* : for this, quite regular form compare *P.S.I.*, 324, 2, where it occurs in a letter of Apollonios.
*Line 3. ours* : there is no need to doubt that the boats were the private property of Poseidonios (*Annales*, XIX, p. 98, note 1).

BIBLIOGRAPHY: *Journal d'entrée du Musée*, no. 48479; *P. Edg.*, 6.

**59033. Letter from Nikias to Apollonios. — 0.21 m x 0.31 m. — Date: 19th January, 257 B.C.**

Apollonios had written to a certain Lysimachos—clearly a high-ranking official—asking him to provide fruit trees for his plantations. Since Lysimachos had traveled down to Alexandria, the letter was answered by his agent Nikias. Nikias writes that he has shown the messengers around all the <term>orchards</term> <note>original: παραδείσους (paradeisous)—enclosed gardens or parks</note> and advised them to send a representative to Lysimachos, who could provide a better supply from the lower districts. The messengers, however, preferred to take what was available from Nikias. He includes a list of the varieties they took: six types of fig trees, pomegranates, apricots, apples, and eleven varieties of vine. We do not yet know exactly who Lysimachos and Nikias were or where their orchards were located. From other dates, we can infer this correspondence occurred while Apollonios was staying at Memphis.

> Nikias to Apollonios, greetings. You wrote to Lysimachos regarding plants, so that [he might give some to your men]. Lysimachos happened to be in Alexandria, but I, having taken those who arrived around all the **orchards**, showed them that <term>cuttings</term> <note>original: μοσχεύματα (moscheumata)</note> are not available here, nor are they usually grown here. They themselves were not unaware of this, but we send for them from Arsinoe on the island, for there, by work <insert>of machines?</insert>, water exists always; and I thought it best to—

*Line 2.* Perhaps "distribute" or "give to your men."
*Lines 4-5.* The meaning seems to be that water was provided at "Arsinoe on the island" by some mechanical irrigation system.

<column-break/>

<page-num>55</page-num>
<header>ZENON PAPYRI.</header>

—send one of your men to Lysimachos, for he would have provided them from the **lower districts**. But they said it was enough for them if they received plants for the plantation from those available with us. We therefore gave them as many as they wanted, which we have listed below.

->Farewell. Year 28, Hathyr 25.<- <note>Hathyr: An Egyptian month; Hathyr 25 of Year 28 corresponds to Jan 19, 257 B.C.</note>

> Chian figs, wild figs, Lydian figs, sweet figs, Phoenician figs, early-fruiting figs; seedless pomegranates; spring apples <note>likely apricots</note>, and those that bear twice; and vines of the following types: smoky, Cilician, Mendaean, Eudamideian, Maroneian, pumpkin-shaped, Phoenician, smoky <note>repeated in text</note>, Alexandrian, cow-teat, [unclear], and sharp.

**Verso:**
> From Nikias. To Apollonios.
> Regarding plants.

*Line 12.* "Early-fruiting figs": <note>original: ὀλονθοφόρα (olonthophora)—figs that carry the winter fruit</note>.
*Line 13.* "Spring apples": probably apricots, similar to the Latin <note>original: "mala præcocia"</note>, while "those that bear twice" are equivalent to the Latin <note>original: "malus bifera" (twice-bearing apple)</note>.
*Line 14.* "Phoenician": <note>original: Φοινίσσης (Phoinisses)</note>. Compare Virgil, *Georgics* II, 95, "purple and early-ripening" <note>original: "purpureæ preciæque"</note>; the scholar Schnebel prefers to read "Phoenician" as a geographical origin.

BIBLIOGRAPHY: *Museum Entry Journal, no. 48481; P. Edg.*, 79; SCHNEBEL, *Agriculture*, pp. 252, 301, 314, 315.

**59034. Petition from Zoilos to Apollonios. — 0.30 m x 0.315 m. — Date of reception: about 12th February, 257 B.C. (Plate XII).**

Zoilos, who lived abroad, had been ordered by the god **Sarapis** in a vision to sail to Alexandria and tell Apollonios to build a temple in the Greek quarter where Zoilos lived. Trying to avoid the task, Zoilos fell dangerously ill, recovering only after promising to obey. Meanwhile, a man from Knidos began building a temple to Sarapis (a "Sarapeion") on that spot, but the god forbade him and he left. When Zoilos finally reached Alexandria, he spoke to Apollonios about other business instead of the temple, causing him to fall ill again for a long time. He now writes to Apollonios, who is traveling, telling the story and begging for the funds to build the temple. 

The scholar Rostovtzeff suggests this is the same Zoilos employed by Apollonios in Syria, but this is uncertain. This agent was likely the Zoilos present in Ptolemais in the month of Hyperberetaios of Year 28.

<summary>This page contains a detailed list of fruit trees and vines sent to Apollonios and a petition from Zoilos regarding a divine command from the god Sarapis to build a temple.</summary>

<keywords>Nikias, Apollonios, Lysimachos, Zenon, horticulture, fig trees, pomegranates, viticulture, irrigation, Sarapis, temple construction, dreams and visions, petition, Alexandria</keywords>

--- Page 36 ---
<meta>This page continues the catalog of the Zenon archive, focusing on one of the most famous documents in the collection: a petition regarding the establishment of a temple for the god Sarapis. It follows the correspondence of Apollonios, the finance minister of Ptolemy II.</meta>

<page-num>56</page-num>
<header>CATALOGUE OF THE CAIRO MUSEUM.</header>

Zoilos the petitioner seems to have been lying ill in Alexandria (*P.S.I.*, 494). But in truth, apart from Zoilos the Arsinoite <term>economos</term> <note>a provincial financial administrator</note>, we cannot yet distinguish the various persons who bore this name. Nor can we identify the place in which the temple was to be founded. It was a town on the sea-coast with, apparently, a non-Greek population and a Greek quarter. Père Vincent suggests Gaza. That is a possibility, but only one of many; and I should be inclined to look somewhere nearer Knidos. The petition was probably sent from Alexandria and not presented personally to Apollonios.

<margin>1</margin>To Apollonios, greetings, from Zoilos of Aspendos, [one of those]
<margin>2</margin>who was also introduced to you by the friends of the King. It happened to me,
<margin>3</margin>while serving the god Sarapis for the sake of your health and prosperity
<margin>4</margin>with King Ptolemy, that Sarapis [revealed] to me <note>original: χρημα[τίζει]ν; a term often used for divine oracles or communications</note>
<margin>5</margin>in my sleep that I should sail to you and [reveal to you] this
<margin>6</margin>revelation: that [a temple] must be completed for him <note>Original restoration suggests "at your expense."</note>
<margin>7</margin>and a sacred precinct in the Greek quarter by the harbor, and a [priest] to preside and
<margin>8</margin>offer sacrifices upon the altar on your behalf. But when I begged [the god]
<margin>9</margin>to release me from the [task] there, he cast me into a great
<margin>10</margin>illness, so that I was even in danger [of my life]. Having prayed to him [that if he]
<margin>11</margin>healed me, I would endure the <term>liturgy</term> <note>original: ληιτο[υρ]γίαν; a mandatory public service or religious duty</note> and would do what was
<margin>12</margin>commanded by him. As soon as I was healed, someone arrived from Knidos
<margin>13</margin>who attempted to build a temple of Sarapis in this place and had
<margin>14</margin>brought stones; but later the god forbade him from building, and he
<margin>15</margin>departed. When I arrived in Alexandria and was hesitant to approach you
<margin>16</margin>concerning these matters, but rather about the business you had agreed to with me,
<margin>17</margin>I suffered a relapse for four months; therefore I was not able to come
<margin>18</margin>immediately to you. It would be well then, Apollonios, if you followed the
<margin>19</margin>god's commands, so that Sarapis may be gracious to you and make you

Line 5. "this [revelation]" <note>original: τόνδε τόν</note> seems more probable than my first reading, "this [same revelation]."
Line 6. Restore "[a temple at your expense]" <note>original: τοῖς σοῖς ἀναλώμασιν ἱερόν τε</note> or something similar.
Line 7. "in the Greek [quarter]" <note>original: ἐν τῆι Ἑλληνικῆι</note>: supply "part" or "district."
Line 8. Deissmann proposes "having [entreated the god Sarapis]." That gives the required sense, but at this stage of the letter I think that Zoilos would merely have written either "the god" or "Sarapis."
Lines 9-10. The second halves of these lines have been read from the impressions left by the ink on the back of the sheet. In the sentence beginning "Having prayed," we must supply a verb such as "I was healed."
Line 11. "I would endure" <note>original: ὑπομενῶ</note>, as Deissmann says, is clearly right. I read "to do" <note>original: ποιή[σει]ν</note> in preference to the reading in *P.S.I.*, 435.
Line 16. After "but rather" understand "[to approach you] as a petitioner."

Line 22. *will be incurred* <note>original: ἐσομένης</note>: Wilcken corrects to the masculine/neuter form; see also the note in *P.S.I.*, 435.
Line 27. For the position of *Berenike’s Harbor* <note>original: Βερενίκης ὅρμος</note> see *P. Edg.*, 80, introduction and Wilcken, *U.P.Z.*, I, p. 452. I think it lay north or north-east of Memphis, possibly, as Wilcken suggests, on the canal leading to the Red sea. It was visited immediately after Memphis; the travellers stayed there for more than a month, and then went on to Boubastis and the east of the Delta.

### 59035. Letter from Statios (?) to Zenon. — 0.14 m × 0.325 m. — Date: about 31st January, 257 B. C.

A badly preserved letter, the meaning of which is not quite clear. The writer asks Zenon to give some money to Zenodoros in order that he may buy him a sword-belt, and he says that he has written to Zenodoros to carry out the order. The remaining sentence I do not understand. The letter was probably written in Alexandria. Zenodoros appears elsewhere as a commercial, travelling agent in the employment of Apollonios (no. 59090); he is also mentioned, unless this was a different man of the same name, in connection with a weaving establishment at Memphis (no. 59080; *P.S.I.*, 854).

<margin>1</margin>Stati...s to Zenon, greetings. You would do well to give to Zenodoros
<margin>2</margin>[...] so that he may buy a sword-belt <note>original: ξιφιστῆρα</note>. For we have written
<margin>3</margin>to him.

Line 1. Perhaps *Statios* (see *P.S.I.*, 585).
Line 2. *sword-belt* <note>original: ξιφιστῆρα</note>: compare *P.S.I.*, 341, 6. The first 'a' of the Greek word for "to buy" was written over an 'i'.

### 59036. Letter from Apollodotos to Charmides. — 0 m. 39 x 0 m. 32. —
**Date : about 1^st^ February, 257 B. C. (pl. XIII).**

Apollodotos, the writer of this important letter, was a subordinate of Apollonios the <term>dioiketes</term> <note>the chief finance minister of Egypt</note>, possibly a royal <term>oikonomos</term> <note>a local provincial manager or steward</note>, residing in Caria <note>a region in modern-day Turkey then under Ptolemaic control</note>. Charmides to whom he writes was an agent of his, probably staying in Alexandria. He sends Charmides, along with his instructions, copies of two letters addressed to a certain Xanthippos. We learn from the text that Xanthippos was <term>trierarch</term> <note>a wealthy citizen appointed to fit out and maintain a trireme, or warship, at his own expense</note> of a ship and we gather from the context that he was a trierarch in the Athenian sense; that is to say, he was a rich man on whom was laid the burden of equipping a ship for the Ptolemaic navy. While he himself was inhabiting or visiting Alexandria, the ship was stationed at Halikarnassos under the charge of his representative Antipatros. The latter being short of money for upkeep or repairs had received, through the influence of Apollodotos, the following advances, or friendly loans without interest, out of certain public funds : (*a*) 2000 drachmae from the *iatrika* <note>a medical tax collected to pay for public physicians</note>, which we are probably to understand as the proceeds of a royal tax, not of a city tax confined to Halikarnassos; (*b*) 465 drachmae, 2 obols, 2 chalkoi, from an unspecified source; (*c*) 3000 drachmae, which had been collected as a <term>stephanos</term> <note>literally a "crown," a traditional gold gift or tax paid to the king on special occasions</note> for the king from the city of Halikarnassos. (*a*) was to be repaid by Xanthippos, directly or indirectly, to a certain Medeios, presumably in Alexandria, to the account of the medical tax. (*b*) was to be sent back to Apollodotos, or else paid to Medeios along with (*a*). The 3000 drachmae forming the crown tax had been advanced on the guarantee of Apollonios to Epikydes, an official in Halikarnassos, and were to be repaid by Xanthippos to Apollonios himself in Alexandria.

The special interest of the letter is that it proves the existence of some form of trierarchy for the upkeep of the navy. In my first edition of the text I took the view that, as Xanthippos was residing in Alexandria, he was presumably a prominent

<page-num>59</page-num>
<header>ZENON PAPYRI.</header>

Alexandrian citizen. But Wilcken has lately advanced a different and very attractive theory on this point. Arguing that the advance of public money collected in Caria is more intelligible if we suppose it to have been made for the benefit of a local trierarch and that the presence of Xanthippos in Alexandria is not inconsistent with the view that he was a citizen of Halikarnassos, he comes to the conclusion that Halikarnassos and, if Halikarnassos, no doubt others of the pseudo-autonomous cities in the Ptolemaic empire were subjected to the burden of trierarchy for the support of the king’s navy (see bibliography). This theory appears very probable on general grounds, but the argument would gain in strength if it could be shown that the royal medical tax <note>original: iatrikon</note>, which Bouché-Leclercq defines as an «subscription for medical care», was levied on the citizens of Halikarnassos and not only on such persons as owed a direct allegiance to the king. This is a point that is still obscure : what classes of the population, in and outside Egypt, paid the medical tax?

Apollodotos to Charmides, greetings. I have appended below for you the copies of the
letters to Xanthippos. After you have met him, find out <gloss>how</gloss> he
wishes to manage the business regarding the Buzes-people, and, if he wishes to pay you, take it from him and
<gloss>pay it to Medeios—that which</gloss>
Straton, the <term>treasurer</term> <note>original: gazophylax; a royal treasurer</note> in Halikarnassos, had given from the medical taxes to Antipatros
<gloss>for</gloss>
5  the representative of Xanthippos for the ship which Xanthippos serves as trierarch: 2000 drachmae; and the
<gloss>465 drachmae, 2 obols, 2 chalkoi</gloss>
send back to us, giving them to someone who will carry them without risk; and regarding the 3000 drachmae, take care
<gloss>that</gloss>
he pays them to Apollonios just as we have instructed him.
->Farewell. Year 28, the 27th of Apellaios.<-

Apollodotos to Xanthippos, greetings. If you are well in body and everything else is according to your
wishes...

Line 1. *Apollodotos* : here and in no. 59037 and no. 59056 we find him active in Caria, while in *P. S. I.*, 324, dating from 261 B. C., he is mentioned, along with his colleague Hikesios, in connection with exports of grain, apparently Government grain, from Syria. But this does not prove that he was then residing in Syria. The grain may have been intended for the Carian market.
Line 4. *Treasurer* <note>original: γαζοφύλαξ</note> : not one of the city treasurers (see *P. Edg.*, 67, introduction), but a treasurer in the king’s service, possibly attached to the royal forces. It seems questionable whether the medical tax mentioned here consisted of money collected in Caria to be remitted to Alexandria or money held by Straton for payment of the doctors who benefited by the tax, especially the army and navy doctors. The fact that the loan was to be repaid in Alexandria is in favour of the former alternative.
<sig>8.</sig>

<summary>This page contains a letter from Apollodotos to Charmides regarding the repayment of large sums of money advanced for the maintenance of a warship in Halikarnassos. It highlights the "trierarchy" system where wealthy individuals were responsible for naval costs and the existence of a royal medical tax used as a source for emergency loans.</summary>

### 59037. Letter or memorandum to Zenon. — 0.17 m. X 0.155 m. — Date : 258-257 B. C. (plate VIII).

This communication, which gives news about certain matters at Halikarnassos <note>a Greek city on the coast of Caria</note>, has apparently been divided into several paragraphs, but only the lower half of the text is preserved. In lines 2-12 the writer had originally used the pronouns "you," "to you" and "of you," addressing Apollonios directly, but afterwards changed them to "him," "to him" and "of him." He informs Zenon, or rather Apollonios through Zenon, that letters had been sent from Apollodotos and Kratinos, son of Danaos, proposing that the affair of Danaos should be settled by the payment of twenty talents <note>a talent was a very large unit of currency, equal to 6,000 drachmae</note> to the Crown, but that the bearer of the letters, instead of delivering them, was wasting his time in Alexandria in the house of Aristoboulos. He also reports that Danaos' friend Pankris was trying to obtain the office of *nomophylax* <note>guardian of the laws</note> through Epikydes and that the question was being referred to Apollonios; and he advises the latter to show no favor to a man who was talking scandal about him. He next asks for letters of recommendation from Apollonios to Apollodotos, Laagos and Hikesios and for another letter to Iason the banker certifying that Apollonios had been appeased through the writer's intercession.
The writing runs along the fibers and the document was folded parallel to the fibers of the front side and not, as was usual in the case of short-lined letters, at right angles to them. As there is no word of greeting at the end, I formerly supposed that the communication was in the form of a memorandum <note>original: ὑπόμνημα</note> rather than a letter <note>original: ἐπιστολή</note>; but this is not certain (see no. 59062).
The writer was evidently one of Zenon's Carian friends. The hand bears a distinct, though not perfect, resemblance to that of *London Inventory 2092*, a letter from Demetrios about the arrival of Zenon's father in Kaunos, and it is possible that Demetrios was also the writer of the present letter.

-> ...that from the [son] of Da[naos] <-
-> was sent and written to [[you]] <gloss>him</gloss> of this [mon]th Xandikos <-
-> the 28th: Year 28 from both Apollodotos and Kra[ti]nos <-
-> <gloss>son of Danaos</gloss> so that it might be settled by the payment of talents... <-

---

*Line 3.* The colon after the month Xandikos is on the papyrus. The word "the" is required before "Year 28." For Apollodotos, see no. 59036.

5 twenty to the king, but the one sent did not deliver the letters to him, but he is in Alexandria <gloss>wasting away;</gloss> <note>original: φθειρόμενος; this suggests he is idling or squandering time and resources in the capital instead of performing his duties.</note>

15 Get [a letter] also to Jason the banker that the matter has been settled since we met him; for he has written also to Apollodotos.

*Lines 4-5.* This large sum suggests that Danaos was implicated in the collection of revenue.
*Line 6.* "To him" (αὐτῶι): written over "to you" (σοι). Perhaps "the letter" (τὸ γράμμα) originally.
*Line 7.* Aristoboulos: possibly the man who appears as the eponymous priest in the 5th year of Euergetes (see Plaumann's list in the encyclopedia *Pauly-Wissowa*, article *Priests* [ἱερεῖς]).
*Line 9.* Epikydes: the official of Halikarnassos mentioned in no. 59036.
*Line 12.* Originally "through you."
*Line 14.* Ikesios: a colleague of Apollodotos (see the papyrus collection *P. S. I.*, 325). The subject of "let them be" is "the letters."
*Line 18.* The meaning probably is: ‘for he, Apollonios, has already written so to Apollodotos’.

### 59038. Letter from Amyntas to Zenon. — 0 m. 32 x 0 m. 11. — Date of reception: about 29th February, 257 B.C.

Amyntas writes about his friend Demetrios, to whom he had lately lent some silver plate belonging to Apollonios, and who in the meantime had been arrested. If

<column-break/>

<page-num>63</page-num>
<header>ZENON PAPYRI.</header>

Demetrios’ goods are being confiscated by the Treasury, Zenon must try to recover the plate; if not, he is requested to let Demetrios keep it. Amyntas also asks him to show Demetrios as much consideration as possible, until he himself finds an opportunity of interceding with Apollonios. For a fuller account of this affair see no. 59044.

-> Amyntas to Zenon <-
-> greetings. To Demetrios... <-
-> [ ... ] <-
-> [ ... ] <-
5 ...as he was unequipped <note>lacking basic household goods</note>, at his request we lent him
small cups, a wine-cooler on a stand, holding a <term>chous</term> <note>approximately 3 liters or 0.8 gallons</note> and a little more, which Apollonios put together from the outside <note>delivered from the palace stores</note> for Tryphera, and two mismatched <term>Antipatridia</term> <note>specialized drinking vessels named after King Antipater or a famous craftsman</note>, and a small cooler and a ladle. But we hear
15 that he is in prison.
[ ... ]
[ ... if then ]
it seems good to you, you would do well to... recover [the items], if his possessions are being registered for the royal treasury; but if not, let him keep them. And as for the rest, you will do me a favor by looking after him as much as possible, until...

---

*Line 8.* "on a stand" (στατόν): compare Dittenberger, *Sylloge²*, 588, 93, "stationary jar" (στατὸς κάδος). Perhaps a vessel standing on a base of its own; but the meaning is uncertain.
*Lines 9-10.* "put together from the outside" (συνέθηκεν ἔξωθεν): perhaps ‘left in Tryphera’s charge outside the palace’. Compare "camping outside" in *P. S. I.*, 340, 10; also "finding those inside" in *P. Lond. Inv.* 2096, 4 (in the *Lumbroso Collection*, p. 16).
*Lines 11-12.* *Antipatridia*: vases called after one of the Antipaters, like the *Antigonides* and *Seleukides* (vases named after Kings Antigonos and Seleukos).
*Lines 23-25.* This passage, together with no. 59044, 17-19, indicates that Zenon was in touch with the prisoner and that the arrest had taken place up the river.

### 59040. Letter from Amyntas to Zenon. — 0 m. 105 X 0 m. 315. — Date of reception : about 3^rd^ March, 257 B. C.

Amyntas acknowledges receipt of the copper vessels from the Troglodyte <note>The "Troglodytes" were a people living along the Red Sea coast, often involved in trade or the transport of elephants for the Ptolemaic army.</note> and of the fish, all but two, from Aigyptos.
The things mentioned had probably been sent from Berenikes Hormos <note>Berenice's Harbor, a key port on the Red Sea.</note>. In *P. Cornell* I we find some Troglodytes attached to the retinue of Apollonios from the 2^nd^ to the 11^th^ of Audnaios ; they disappear then for a fortnight and return on the 26^th^. Amyntas' letter was docketed by Zenon on the 28^th^. It seems probable then that in the meantime the Troglodytes had made a trip to Alexandria on one of the boats by which Apollonios kept up communication with the capital. Wilcken suggests

---

<page-num>65</page-num>
<header>ZENON PAPYRI.</header>

(*U. P. Z.*, I, p. 452) that their presence at Berenikes Hormos may indicate that this port lay on the Red Sea or on the canal leading to it ; and the latter location is quite possible. But what the Troglodytes were doing in Apollonios' camp, whether they were envoys bringing gifts or were engaged in his service, I cannot say. As for the <term>thrisai</term> <note>original: θρίσσαι; a type of fish, likely a variety of shad or herring.</note> see Athenaeus, 328 E : *Dorion, in his work "On Fish," also mentions the river thrisai* and Strabo 824 : *Aristoboulos says that no sea-fish runs up into the Nile except the grey mullet, the thrisai, and the dolphin.*

Amyntas to Zenon, greetings. We have received the copper vessels <note>original: χαλκώματα</note> from the Troglodyte, which you wrote to us about, and the <term>thrisai</term>-fish from Aigyptos <note>Aigyptos is a personal name here, likely an agent or carrier.</note>,
fewer by two.
->Farewell.<-

**VERSO :**

[in the harbor].

### 59041. Letter to Zenon. — 0 m. 245 X 0 m. 10 and fragment containing docket o m. 03 X 0 m. 10. — Date : 19^th^ March, 257 B. C.

This incomplete and rather obscure letter is about a tax-collector who had been arrested and taken to the Arsinoite nome <note>The administrative district of the Faiyum oasis.</note> as though responsible to the authorities there. The writer was probably a tax-farmer at Memphis or in the Memphite nome, and the arrested man had been an employee of his and was now an employee of his colleague or successor Athenodoros. Apollonios had already ordered Theodoros to write to Zoilos, the Arsinoite <term>oeconome</term> <note>a financial administrator or "steward" of a province</note>, and to Mnesistratos to return the man to his employers, and the writer asks Zenon to procure Apollonios' order and send it on to him.

. . . . . . . . . . . for the tax
collections of the 27th year, but having been led

to the Arsinoite [district]
as if being [an employee] of Dioskourides

*Lines 3-4. There must be a grammatical mistake here.*
*Line 4. Dioskourides : probably a tax-farmer or an official in the Arsinoite nome.*

### 59042. Letter from Amyntas to Zenon. — 0.125 m x 0.33 m. — Date of reception : about 19th March, 257 B. C.

The docket tells us that the travelers were now in Bubastos, no doubt the metro-

<column-break/>

<page-num>67</page-num>
<header>ZENON PAPYRI.</header>

polis in the Delta. Amyntas recommends a friend of his called Alexandros, who had been requisitioned for some public service, and asks Zenon to get him excused on the plea of ill health. There is a similar reference to compulsory service in *P.S.I.*, 484, while in another of our papyri we read "unless some Government work <note>original: βασιλικὴ λειτουργία (*basilike leitourgia*), a royal or state service</note> be imposed upon him". Compare also *P. Hib.*, 78. It is unfortunate that in none of these cases is the nature of the work specified.
The papyrus bears the mark of a seal impressed upon it.

> letter to you, is a relative of one of my friends, and I myself am 
> well-acquainted with him; 
> it happens that he has been requisitioned by someone. You would 
> therefore do me a favor by joining in the effort, 
> if it is possible, for him to be released. For he is also rather weak 
> in body 
> 5 because of an illness.

->Year 28, Peritios 15,<-
->at Bubastos.<-

*Line 3.* "joining in the effort" <note>original: συσπουδάσας</note>, or "not being in a hurry": illegible on the front of the papyrus, but deciphered, with more or less probability, from the impressions which the letters have left on the back.

## 59044. Letter from Amyntas to Zenon. — 0.29 m x 0.185 m. — Date of reception: about March 26, 257 B.C. (Plate XIV).

Amyntas writes again about his friend Demetrios (see no. 59038), whose goods were now being sold off and who was himself still in prison. He does not repeat his earlier request that Zenon should recover the silver plate, but he offers proof that it really belongs to the household. Attached is a copy of a letter which he has written to Apollonios <note>Apollonios was the Dioiketes, the highest financial official in Ptolemaic Egypt</note>, but which he does not wish Zenon to deliver unless he and Artemidoros the physician agree that it is advisable. The goal of the letter to Apollonios is not to plead for Demetrios, but to excuse himself for having lent the silver plate. From the story he tells, we learn that Demetrios had formerly been a <term>deputy-controller</term> <note>original: ἀντιγραφεύς (antigrapheus), a local official who kept duplicate accounts to prevent fraud</note> to the manager of the Prosopite region. Another papyrus shows that when Apollonios was staying at Nikiou in the preceding summer, he had sent Demetrios on government business to Herakleia in Phoenicia. On his return, he seems to have been detained in Alexandria by Apollonios’ order; being short of money and comforts, he persuaded Amyntas to lend him the silver plate. Shortly after, as we gather from the context, he was summoned to appear before Apollonios, placed under arrest, and his property confiscated to the Crown.

### **59045. Letter from Amyntas to Zenon.** — 0.11 m x 0.32 m. — Date of reception: about 26th March, 257 B.C. (plate XV).

The letter introduces Zopyros the son of Sosigenes, who like Zenon belonged to a Kaunian family <note>Kaunos was a city in Caria (modern-day Turkey), the homeland of Zenon and many of his associates</note>, and requests Zenon to choose a favourable moment for presenting him to Apollonios, to whom he wished to speak about some private matters. Amyntas had also written a letter of recommendation to Apollonios (see no. 59046), which would probably be delivered by Zenon or by Zopyros himself.

### **59047. Letter from Amyntas to Zenon.** — 0.09 m x 0.31 m. — Date of reception: about 11th April, 257 B. C.

Amyntas sends Dexilaos with a letter to Apollonios about expenses and asks Zenon to deliver the letter and send back the messenger at once, presumably with money or with an order on the bank. Inside the letter was found a clay sealing with an indistinct representation of a man to the right, wearing a conical hat.
It will be noticed that Zenon was now at Mendes in the north-east of the Delta (see no. 59052).

### 59048. Memorandum to Aratos from Aristeus. — 0.055 m x 0.19 m. — Date: 257 B.C. (?) (plate XV).

Aristeus, a man of good position in the household of Apollonios (see no. 59027), asks Aratos, of whom nothing is known, to remind Zenon the <term>oikonomos</term> <note>A manager or steward of a private estate, or a financial official in the government.</note> and Kriton the admiral to purchase some rugs and garments and a Tanitic bed-spread and bring them down to Alexandria, as he had asked them to do before they left town. Everything indicates that this memorandum dates from the time when Zenon was travelling with Apollonios, and the mention of the Tanitic bedspread <note>original: "ἐνκοίμητρον Τανιτικόν"</note> suggests that it was intended to anticipate their arrival in the neighbourhood of Tanis <note>A city in the north-eastern Nile Delta.</note>. The terms <term>oikonomos</term> and <term>stolarchēs</term> <note>A fleet commander or "admiral."</note> applied here to Zenon and Kriton do not mean that they were Government officials, but merely that Zenon was manager of Apollonios’ private finances, while Kriton was in charge of his private fleet of ships (see introduction to *P. Edg.*, 16 and <term>ROSTOVTZEFF</term>, *Large Estate*, chapter 4).

Memorandum to Aratos from Aristeus. Remind Zenon
the manager and Kriton the fleet commander concerning the thin rugs
with borders, and the thick cloak, and the mantle, the tunic,
and the Tanitic bedspread, so that after purchasing them you may bring them down
5 with them, just as I instructed you all while you were present.

*Lines 4-5.* "you may bring them down... with them" <note>original: "κατάγητε ... μεθ’ αὐτῶν"</note>, rather than "they may bring them down... with themselves," either because Aratos was

<column-break/>

<page-num>73</page-num>
<header>ZENON PAPYRI.</header>

himself a member of the party, or because the writer has forgotten that he is not addressing Zenon and Kriton directly.

<meta>BIBL. : Museum Entry Journal, no. 48496 ; P. Edg., 16.</meta>

### 59049. Letter from Nikon to Zenon. — 0.105 m x 0.075 m and 0.10 m x 0.205 m. — Date: 1st April, 257 B.C. (plate XV).

The date of the docket seems to be the 11th of Dystros, on which day Zenon received three other letters from the same correspondent (*P.S.I.*, 492, 493, 638). Nikon is sometimes mentioned along with Addaios, and the two were probably at this time overseers of Apollonios' property near Memphis. In the first part of the letter he asks Zenon to send a man, or allow him to hire a man, who will take delivery of the hay due to the estate from certain farmers. The second part is a personal appeal for better pay.

Nikon to Zenon, g[reet]ing[s. Sen]d someone who will take delivery of the hay 
due to us from the 
farmers and [remin]d Apollon[ios] about these things, so that he may either send someone
<margin>so that</margin>
or authorize a wage [so that] we 
may hire one. For [we ourselves are occupied with the ga]thering of the grain and with 
5 the [illegible...] 
we will not be able to [.................]. And concerning us, you have shown no 
consideration so that a wa[ge] might be 
added for us and a gra[in allowance, even though] we reminded [Apoll]onios and he 
ordered a memorandum to be giv[en] to him. 
10 You would do well then [not to neglec]t us, but to see to it that everything 
is [brought to a better state?] and now reminding 
Apollonios about u[s. For we have n]o one else to remind about 
such things.
->Farewell. Year 28, Mecheir 8.<-

*Line 2.* "so that" <note>original: "ὡς ἂν"</note> is to be regarded as cancelled.
*Line 3.* gathering <note>original: "συναγωγῆι"</note>: or possibly "harvesting." The restoration of the end of the line is uncertain, most of the letters being quite illegible. The traces suggest "being kept awake by the work that continues."
*Line 4. For example:* "therefore [to be also occupied with this]."
*Line 6.* Here again the reading is very doubtful. The letters read as *panlō* might be *teagēl*, from which I can obtain no sense. Not that "everything is brought to a better state" <note>original: "τὸ πᾶν λώιον"</note> is satisfactory either. *echthēis*, if rightly read, is used in the sense of appointing salaries; for the form and meaning, cf. *P.S.I.*, 498, 4.

<meta>Catal. du Musée, n° 59001.</meta>

### 59052. Letter from Antimenes to Zenon. — 0.195 m x 0.205 m. — Date of reception: about 20th April, 257 B.C.

I take this letter to have been written in continuation of no. 59029, in which Antimenes announced the departure of a lady, probably from Kaunos <note>A city in ancient Caria, modern-day Turkey</note> or some other Carian port. The <term>docket</term> <note>A summary or filing note written on the back of the document.</note> on the verso states that Antimenes has written about Doris and has added a copy of a letter received by him from Sosipatros. (Antipatros is evidently a slip of the pen). Sosipatros’ letter is in fact copied out on the <term>recto</term> <note>The front side of the papyrus.</note>. He announces that Ariston and the sister, no doubt Doris, have arrived at Arsinoe after being compelled by stormy weather to put in at Patara, and he thanks Antimenes for the attention he has paid to the travellers. Arsinoe seems to be the town in Cilicia mentioned by Strabo 670 ("then Arsinoe, which has a harbor"). It is true that on one of the Hadra vases we find a reference to a Pamphylian Arsinoe, "Arsinoe [of] Pamphylia" (BRECCIA, *Greek Inscriptions*, no. 191); but from what Strabo 670 says about the boundary between Pamphylia and Cilicia it is probable that this is really the same place as the Cilician Arsinoe.
As the papyrus is in good preservation, the missing part will probably be recovered some day. In the meantime I have inserted some provisional restorations in order to show more clearly what seems to me to be the gist of the text.

Antimenes to Zenon, greetings. If you are well, it would be [good; I too am in good health].
I have written below for you [the copy of the letter] that came to m[e] from Sosipatros,
so that, being informed, you may report it verbally <note>or "enter it in the account"</note> to Apollonios [...
no travel allowance <term>ephodion</term> <note>Money or supplies provided for a journey.</note> was provided for them, nor ... [
<margin>5</margin> they were driven by the storm into Arsinoe [
->[Farewell. Year 28,<-
Sosipatros to Antimenes, greetings. If [you are well] in body [and other things are]
to your liking, it would be good; we ourselves are also well. [Upon arriving to us,]
Ariston and the sister reported having been well cared for b[y you in Kaunos. You therefore]
<margin>10</margin> do well by us in acting not as a stranger. For [we ourselves] will try [to be zealous regarding whatever...]

*Line 3. "report it verbally" <note>original: "ἀναφέρηις ἐν λόγωι"</note>:* this might mean ‘enter in an account’, but I think the more probable meaning in this case is ‘report to Apollonios verbally’. Possibly the writer did not wish Apollonios to see the letter and to think that there had been some negligence about the travel allowance.
*Line 9. "the sister"* <note>original: "ἡ ἀδελφὴ"</note> might mean either ‘my sister’ or ‘his sister’. The context certainly suggests that the travellers were relatives of Sosipatros, while one cannot but

I suppose from the gist of the letters that there was some close tie between Zenon and Doris.

*Line 12.* Patara: according to the geographer Strabo (book 14, chapter 3, section 6), Ptolemy Philadelphos <note>The King of Egypt from 285 to 246 B.C.</note> tried to impose the name of "Arsinoe" on this sea-port in Lycia, but without permanent success. Compare the case of Rabbatammana (as seen in *Greek and Latin Papyri*, 616, line 27), the official name of which was Philadelphia. It is just possible, however, that the Arsinoe of line 5 stands for Patara (note the phrasing of line 12), though I think it more probably means the Cilician town.

## 59053. Letter from Amyntas to Zenon. — 0.255 m x 0.14 m. — Date: about 19th April, 257 B. C.

Amyntas has learned that he will soon be ordered to go on a voyage, and he asks Zenon to have awnings and other things made for his boats and to bring them down with him when he returns to Alexandria. He has sent Hermon to give a list of the things required and to attend to the business of procuring them. Amyntas does not say where he was going, whether to the Syrian coast, where Apollonios <note>The Finance Minister of Egypt and Zenon's employer.</note> had so many commercial interests, or up the river. *Greek and Latin Papyri*, 533 is a reminder to Zenon on the same subject.

->Amyntas to Zenon, greetings.<-
->We suppose that a journey abroad<-
->will be announced to us, but<-
->our boats are unequipped.<-
<margin>5</margin> ->You would do well therefore to take care<-
->that the equipment <gloss>is ready</gloss> for us, both the<-
->fittings for the boats and the<-

->rest of the useful items and<-
->what is satisfactory. As for the money<-
<margin>10</margin> ->for these things, you will do us a favor<-
->by ordering it to be given in Memphis;<-
->and you will receive it from us in the city.<-
->And do this as quickly as possible,<-
->so that when they are ready, you may<-
<margin>15</margin> ->bring them down to us when you<-

**VERSO:**

*Line 9.* The phrase "what is satisfactory" <note>original: "τοῦ καλῶς ἔχοντος"</note> may perhaps mean "on favorable terms," just as "at the current price" <note>original: "τοῦ εὑρίσκοντος"</note> means "at the market rate." Or perhaps simply "satisfactory"?
*Lines 9-12.* This clause is parenthetical, and the following words "as quickly as possible" resume the main sentence.
*Line 12.* In the first edition I read "in" <note>original: "ἐν"</note>, but from the slight traces of letters "in" <note>original: "ἐμ", a variation before certain consonants</note> seems more probable.
*Line 20.* 13 in the first edition.
*Line 21.* Probably the date, Year 28, Dystros... or Xandikos..., followed by Amyntas [son] of Hermon.

## 59054. List of articles required for a voyage. — 0.265 m x 0.28 m. — Date: April, 257 B. C.

As this papyrus was found sticking to no. 59053, there is no doubt that it is the list of which Amyntas speaks at the end of his letter. He was apparently to have the use of two boats, a <term>kerkouros</term> <note>A fast, light rowing vessel, often used for transport or as a messenger ship.</note> and a <term>kybaia</term> <note>A larger, broader merchant vessel or barge designed for carrying cargo.</note>, and for each boat he required a set of four awnings. From the dimensions given in column 1 it appears that the two boats were of equal length, but the <term>kybaia</term> was broader throughout and blunter at the bow. Column 2 contains a list of miscellaneous articles, including a hundred and fifty papyrus rolls for accounts and correspondence.
The list is written, no doubt by a clerk, in an almost literary hand like that of no. 59087, plate XIX, while the accompanying letter is in the same hand as no. 59044, plate XIV, and may perhaps have been written by Amyntas himself.

<summary>This page contains correspondence from Amyntas to Zenon regarding the urgent outfitting of boats for an upcoming voyage, highlighting the logistical coordination required for travel in Ptolemaic Egypt. It includes a detailed mention of maritime equipment, financial transfers between Memphis and Alexandria, and a list of supplies including a large quantity of papyrus for record-keeping.</summary>

### 59056. Letter from Apollodotos to Zenon. — 0.175 m x 0.245 m. — Date of reception: about 27th April, 257 B.C.

Though incomplete, this letter is a good specimen of what the Greeks called <note>original: "ἐπιστολαὶ φιλάνθρωποι"</note> *friendly or benevolent letters*. It was written in Caria <note>A region in southwestern Asia Minor, modern-day Turkey.</note> by Apollodotos, the author of no. 59036. Apollodotos had lately gone to Kaunos <note>A city in Caria and Zenon’s hometown.</note> and met Zenon’s father and brothers, and he writes to let Zenon know that he has been showing them every attention and will be pleased to do anything else that they or Zenon may desire. He wonders why he has not heard from Zenon for such a long time.
If the first line is correctly restored, the number of letters in each of the following lines must have been nearly seventy. In spite of the gaps, the meaning and construction seem fairly clear.

Apollodotos to Zenon, greetings. If you yourself are well and other things are to your 
liking, it would be as we wish; we ourselves are also well.
At Kaunos, both your father and your brothers met [us... when we recently arrived]
we showed them [every attention] and encouraged them that in the future
<margin>5</margin> they should visit us. You would also do well to write to them [and...]

*Line 1.* For the restoration, compare *P.S.I.*, 606, 607.
*Line 2. For example, "when we recently arrived."*
*Line 3.* Kaunos was Zenon’s native town and his father was called Agreophon. To judge by a comparison of the dates, it is improbable that the Agreophon who wrote *P.S.I.*, 491 was the father, though he may have been a member of the same family. For another reference to Zenon’s father, see *P.S.I.*, 533, 9.
*Line 4. For example, "concerning whatever they wish."*
*Line 5. For example, "not to hesitate to come to us."*

<column-break/>

<page-num>81</page-num>
<header>ZENON PAPYRI.</header>
<sig>Catalogue of the Museum, no. 59001. 11</sig>

to speak about whatever they might need and to send word to us as to how
we might please you by our actions. For now, [having not received any letter]
from you, we were wondering what the reason might be.
Farewell. Year 28.

VERSO:
<margin>10</margin> Apollodotos. A friendly letter to Zenon 
concerning his father. Year 28, Dystros 24,
at Memphis.

*Line 6. For example, "what else we might do for them."*
*Line 7. For example, "having not received any letter for a long time."*
*Line 10. Friendly: understand "friendly letter."* Compare *P.S.I.*, 429, 32.

BIBLIOGRAPHY: *Museum Entry Journal*, no. 48504.

### 59057. Letter from Zoilos to Alexandros. — 0.125 m x 0.033 m. — Date: about 25th April, 257 B.C. (plate XVI).

It seems probable that the author of this letter was not the devotee of the god Sarapis who wrote the petition no. 59034, but a person employed by Apollonios <note>The finance minister of Egypt under Ptolemy II.</note> in Syria and Palestine, mentioned in no. 59002 and *P.S.I.*, 495, and author of the letters *P.S.I.*, 330 and 494. If that supposition is right, it will follow that the present letter was written abroad and that the term <note>original: "ἐξάγαγε"</note> *export* in line 7 means ‘export from Egypt’. Zoilos urges Alexandros to help him and bring pressure to bear on Apollonios by means of his friends, but he does not say what exactly he was wanting. The letter may have been handed to Zenon on his return to town. When it came into our possession it was tied up and fastened with a small lump of clay, not the original seal.

Zoilos to Alexandros, greetings. It would be well if you are healthy; we ourselves are also 
healthy.
I received the letters and I am not neglecting the things about which you write to us. And you will 
do well
to work hard so that the things we commissioned to you according to the memorandum may be done for us,
and once this is done, be assured that I will owe you sufficient thanks. Therefore, 
bring 
<margin>5</margin> others of your friends to Apollonios and add something to these, if also Anti-

*Line 1. Alexandros*: presumably not the friend of Amyntas mentioned in no. 59042.
*Line 5. Add something to these*: ‘offer them something’? Or ‘add something to my requests’?

### 59058. Letter from Apollonios to Zenon. — 0 m. 155 x 0 m. 25. — Date of reception: about 28th April, 257 B.C.

The writer is Apollonios the <term>dioiketes</term> <note>The chief finance minister of Ptolemaic Egypt.</note>. Zenon was at present in Memphis, but it is not clear whether Apollonios was also there or was separated for a short time from his faithful secretary. The meaning of the letter, owing to its state of preservation, is obscure. Taking the word <term>for/concerning</term> <note>Original: "ὑπέρ"</note> in line 8 in the sense of <term>about</term> <note>Original: "περί"</note> (as in no. 59075, 6) and reading "drachmae" in line 9, restoring "in Caria" <note>Modern-day Turkey.</note> in line 3 and remembering that 3000 drachmae was the sum which Apollodotos had lately advanced in Halikarnassos on Apollonios' account, I have ventured to connect the present text with no. 59036, 19-26. The reader is warned that my restorations are based on a doubtful conjecture.

> Apollonios to Zenon, greetings. [We have received from]
> [Iat]rokles, three thousand [drachmae] of silver,
> which we ordered to be given [to Apollodotos in]
> Car[ia] from our private account.
> <margin>5</margin> ->Farewell. Year 28, [Dystros...<-

Lines 2-3. 'Which we have ordered to be given from our private account'. The scribe has apparently altered <term>I have written</term> <note>Original: "γέγραφα"</note> to <term>we have written</term> <note>Original: "γεγράφαμεν"</note>.
Line 5. The Macedonian month may have been followed by the Egyptian month; but this is doubtful, for Zenon was not yet resident in the <term>chora</term> <note>The Egyptian countryside/provinces, as opposed to Alexandria.</note>.

### 59059. Letter from Aristeus to Zenon. — 0 m. 135 x 0 m. 245. — Date of reception: about 5th May, 257 B.C.

Aristeus announces that he will pay the servants in Alexandria according to Zenon's instructions and gives some details about previous payments (compare no. 59027). As the papyrus is in good condition and the break is a clean one, the missing part will probably be recovered in time.

> being delayed for a long time. &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; Farewell. Year 28 [

VERSO:
Aristeus concerning the wages &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; To Zenon.
<margin>10</margin> for the slaves. Year 29,
Xandikos 3, in Memphis.

Line 1. For example, <term>the wages being paid to the slaves</term> <note>Original: "τοῖς σώμασιν τὰ γινόμενα ὀψώνια"</note>.
Line 2. <term>giving instructions</term> <note>Original: "συντάσσοντος"</note> may refer to several orders, including the order (<term>having instructed</term>) mentioned in no. 59027.
Line 4. Satyra: the harp-player (see no. 59028).
Line 5. <term>carver</term> <note>Original: "ἐλεάτρωι"</note>: probably not Artemidoros the house steward, but merely a servant connected with the kitchen. Some confusion seems to have arisen between the terms <term>eleatros</term> <note>A meat-carver.</note> and <term>edeatros</term> <note>A taster or table-setter.</note> (see the definitions quoted in the Greek Thesaurus), though they are really quite distinct.
Line 6. <term>I have written</term> or <term>we have written</term>?
Line 7. For example, <term>because the wages [have been delayed] for a long time</term> <note>Original: "ἐπὶ τῶι τὰ ὀψώνια διὰ πολλοῦ"</note>.
Lines 8-10. The new regnal year began between the dispatch and the delivery of the letter.

Bibliography: Journal d'entrée du Musée, no. 48508; P. Edg., no. 10.

### **59060.** Letter from Hierokles to Zenon. — 0.12 m. x 0.24 m. — Date of reception: about 5th May, 257 B.C. (Plate XVI).

The letter concerns a boy named Pyrrhos, who was receiving an education in Alexandria and was specifically being trained to compete in the public games. Zenon had previously written that they should only continue his training if they were certain he would win a prize; otherwise, it would be a waste of money and a distraction from his academic studies. Hierokles responds that Ptolemaios, the master of the <term>palæstra</term> <note>A wrestling school or gymnasium.</note>, is very pleased with the boy’s progress and they fully expect him to be successful. He asks Zenon to send several items required for Pyrrhos’s upkeep.

There is a fragmentary letter from Hierokles in the British Museum (*Invent.*, no. 2312) that is very similar in meaning and language to the first part of this letter, though it seems to have been more detailed. This version appears to be a combination of the British Museum letter and document no. 59061. It is possible all three were sent due to a mistake; since Zenon opened this letter on the 3rd of the month of Xandikos and the others on the 2nd, they may have been delivered by different messengers.

It is very probable that the <term>palæstra</term> mentioned here is the same one discussed in *P.S.I.* 340, and that Hierokles was the author of that letter as well.

A small fragment of the missing text was found in a recently purchased lot (see no. 59071) and added to the collection, though it was found too late for the published plate.

1 Hierokles to Zenon, greetings. If you are in good health, it would be well; we too are healthy. You [wrote]
2 to me about Pyrrhos, that if we know for certain [that he will win <note>Original: "ὅτι νικήσει" — supplied from the London fragment.</note>], we should train him, but if not,
3 we should not incur a <gloss>and</gloss> useless expense <note>Original: "ἀνήλωμα μάταιον"</note> and have him distracted from his studies <note>Original: "ἀπὸ τῶν γραμμάτων ἀποσπαθῆναι" — literally "pulled away from his letters."</note>. Now, as for
4 [knowing for certain], the gods would best know that, but to Ptolemaios it appears, as far as a [human can tell],
5 that [he is] much better than those currently being trained, who have already had a long start...

Line 2. The London fragment shows that "that he will win" is to be supplied after "know for certain": "you told us to train him if we are sure that he will win."
Line 2. Hierokles uses a non-standard spelling for "not" <note>Original: "μέ" for "μή".</note>.
Lines 3-4. For the double "ss" in the words for "distracted" and "knowing," this is a dialectical or scribal variation.
Line 4. A non-standard form of the verb "to know" <note>Original: "εἰδείησαν" for "εἰδήσειαν".</note>.
Line 5. Scholars suggest "he is by nature" <note>Original: "πέφυκεν"</note>, but the visible letters are unclear. "Pyrrhos is" or "he is a boxer" would also fit the context but are difficult to confirm.

<column-break/>

6 ...and in a very short time he will far surpass them; and he is making progress both in [this training]
7 and in his other studies; and with the gods' help, I hope that you will be crowned <note>Original: "στεφανωθήσεσθαι" — Zenon, as the financial sponsor of the athlete, would receive the honorary crown if the boy won.</note>.
8 Send <gloss>and</gloss> to him a bathing-skin <note>Original: "ἐγλουστρίδα" — a leather apron or loincloth used for bathing.</note> as quickly as possible, and ideally let the skin be goatskin, but if not, [thin calfskin],
9 and a tunic and a cloak and the bedding and a coverlet and [pillows]
10 and the honey. You wrote to me that you wonder if I do not realize that a tax <note>Original: "τέλος"</note> [is applied] to all these things.
11 I know, but you are capable enough at managing things so that they may be sent as safely as possible.

->VERSO:<-
Hierokles regarding Pyrrhos. To Zenon.
Year 29, Xandikos 3,
in Memphis.

Line 7. "You will be crowned": a reference to success in the games; Zenon would be considered the victor as the boy's patron.
Line 8. "Bathing-skin": defined by ancient lexicographers as a leather garment worn by women or those assisting them while bathing.
Line 10. "You wonder I do not understand that there is a toll on all these things."
Line 11. Hierokles uses a slightly incorrect grammatical form for "managing."
Line 11. There may have been a closing "Farewell" in the missing portion, but there is little space for it.

BIBLIOGRAPHY: *Journal d’entrée du Musée*, no. 48509; *P. Edg.*, 11; ROSTOVTZEFF, *Large Estate*, pp. 172-174.

### **59061.** Letter from Hierokles to Zenon. — 0.08 m. x 0.215 m. — Date of reception: about 4th May, 257 B.C.

This is almost a duplicate of the second part of document no. 59060. The jars <note>Original: "κάδια"</note> mentioned in line 3 no doubt contained the honey. The only articles omitted here are the tunic and cloak, and it is possible that these were mentioned in the related letter now held in the British Museum.

1 Hierokles to Zenon, greetings. If you are in good health and in other matters are getting along [to your] liking, it [would be well];
2 I too am healthy. Be sure to send the bedding for the boy and [a coverlet]...

### 59062. Letter from Theodoros to Zenon. — (*b*) 0.215 m. x 0.18 m. — Date of reception: about 5th May, 257 B. C.

Fragment (*a*), which seems to be the beginning of Theodoros' letter, is in the British Museum (*Inventory*, no. 2352) and Mr. Bell has kindly allowed me to publish his transcript of it. Theodoros, as we learn from the filing note and from no. 59084, was an <term>assistant</term> <note>original: "ὑπηρέτης" (hyperetes); a subordinate official or servant.</note> in the household of Apollonios and, as appears from (*b*), line 6, a brother of Amyntas, Zenon's colleague and correspondent. He writes to say that, having found Python, the well-known banker, at Athribis, he had received from him the sum of one thousand drachmæ. He also asks Zenon to deliver, at a propitious moment, a letter from Amyntas to Apollonios about Python and to see that an order is written about the payment of his salary. The word "therefore" in fragment (*b*), line 3, and indeed the whole context suggest that the person whose salary is in question is not Amyntas but Python the royal banker. That Theodoros should say a word for Amyntas would be only natural; to find him interceding for Python is a little surprising; and I feel doubtful whether after all the "to him" in lines 9-12 does not refer to Amyntas.

->(*a*)<-
->Theodoros to Zenon, greeting. When<-
->I departed from you for Athribis, I found<-

<column-break/>

<page-num>87</page-num>
<header>ZENON PAPYRI.</header>

Python and Ammonios
besieged <note>A figurative term meaning they were being heavily pressured or harassed for payment.</note> by Antiochos
5 concerning money. Nevertheless, when
I delivered to Python the letter
from you, he made every effort
[to...] immediately [...]
*traces of text*

->(*b*)<-
[...] pack-animals
to send along with them. You would therefore do well
to also take from the person delivering
5 this letter to you—the one from Amyntas
->to Apollonios<-
my brother <note>It is debated whether Amyntas is the brother of Theodoros or Zenon.</note> concerning Python—and, having delivered it
at a favorable time, to also join in
the effort with Philotimos regarding the
salary <note>original: "ὀψωνίου" (opsonion); a salary or food allowance.</note> due to him, so that
10 it might be written for him quickly and so that
he may receive it for the first time, and
by making it clear to him that you also
are eager regarding whatever he may write to you.

**BACK SIDE (VERSO):**
Theodoros the assistant concerning the things ->To Zenon.<-
15 he has from Python.
Year 29, Xandikos 3. <note>Roughly May 257 B.C.</note>

(*a*) *Line 3.* Ammonios: the author of *P.S.I.* 489, possibly a banker like Python and Promethion.
(*a*) *Line 8.* Perhaps "having done everything immediately"?
(*b*) *Line 2.* The money was perhaps required for purchasing donkeys.
(*b*) *Line 3.* "to send along with" is doubtful.
(*b*) *Line 5.* "the letter": meaning this specific letter. The omission of "Farewell" at the end is rare in a formal letter, though common in shorter memoranda.
(*b*) *Line 6.* "The brother" would naturally mean "my brother," but might mean "your brother," and it is therefore questionable whether Amyntas was a brother of Theodoros or of Zenon. However, Amyntas never addresses Zenon as "brother" elsewhere.
(*b*) *Line 13.* The Greek suggests Python, or Amyntas, was writing to Zenon.

*Bibliography: Journal of entry of the Museum, no. 48511.*

### 59063. Fragment of letter from Metrodoros to Apollonios. — 0.205 m. x 0.105 m. — Date of reception: about 7th May, 257 B. C.

Metrodoros, who here announces the dispatch of three Kythnian cheeses for Apollonios' table, was an influential person in the Alexandrian household (see *P. S. I.*, 340, in which Hierokles (?) complains about him and calls him <note>original: "ἄνθρωπος ἀνελεύθερος" (anthropos aneleutheros)</note> an illiberal or stingy man). His letters are written in a large hand like that in which several of Apollonios' own letters are written, and he probably made use of the office scribes for his correspondence.

->Metrodoros to Apollonios, greetings.<-
->[Sending] to you <term>Kythnian</term> cheeses... <note>From the island of Kythnos, famous for its cheese in antiquity.</note><-
->...to send...<-
->Farewell. Year [X]<-

**Back side (Verso):**

->5 Metrodoros, concerning three Kythnian cheeses, to Apollonios.<-
->From those with Hegemon, which Menekrates is bringing. Year 29, Xandikos 5.<-

<note>Line 6. Hegemon: mentioned in connection with Metrodoros in P. S. I., 340, 18.</note>

### 59065. Fragment of letter to Apollonios. — 0.135 m. x 0.175 m. — Date: 257 B. C.

The hand resembles that of no. 59064 and the writer may perhaps be Metrodoros. He speaks of sending Apollonios a consignment of <term>thrissai</term> <note>original: "θρίσσαι" (thrissai); a type of fish, likely shad, which were often salted for transport.</note> by Apollonios "the <term>interpreter</term>".

<note>Line 2. The same person is mentioned in P. S. I., 409, 15. But it is not quite clear what the functions of a <term>hermeneus</term> (interpreter/dragoman) were; they may have acted as local liaisons or translators.</note>

*Bibliography: Journal of entry of the Museum, no. 48515.*

### 59066. Letter from Amyntas to Zenon. — 0.19 m. x 0.125 m.

Amyntas announces that he has sent Apollonios a consignment of fish of various kinds and regrets that he has been unable to send Zenon the Chian wine and the other things which he had ordered. The excuse he gives is interesting: the king, he says, is summoning Apollonios back to town and urging the dispatch (?) of the boat in order that his messenger may use it. Unfortunately the date of the letter is lost and we cannot say whether it refers to the journey which ended at the beginning of year 29 or to the tour on which Apollonios started shortly after his return.

to Alexandria. But it is not surprising to find that the king sometimes grew impatient at the long absences of his chief minister.

->Amyntas to Zenon, greetings.<-
->As for the <term>Chian wine</term> <note>original: "Χῖα" (Chia) - jars from the island of Chios, famous for high-quality wine</note> and the rest of the things that<-
->you ordered to be sent to you,<-

5 ->for you because the King<-

->in two other pans<-
->[[perch]] along with the <term>fins</term> <note>original: "σὺν τοῖς ὁπλοῖς" (syn tois hoplois) - literally "with their armor," referring to the scales and fins of the fish</note><-

*Line 2. Chian* : Chian jars presumably containing Chian wine.
*Line 8. The verb* is quite illegible, but seems to end in *sai* or *lai*, while the first letter might be chi (χ). To provide (*choregesai*) is of the right length, but does not give the expected sense. Perhaps *not to be idle* (*me argesai*).
*Line 11. Read pans (bataniois)* and *fish-cubes (kybiois)* (Mayser, *Gramm.*, p. 174). The *fish-cube* (*kybios*) was apparently the whole fish (compare the word for dicing fish, *kybeias*), whereas in other papyri (*P. S. I.*, 428, 70 and 535, 37) "a jar of cubes" (*kybion keramion*) may mean, in accordance with the dictionaries, 'cubes of salted tunny'.

### 59068. Letter from Nikon (?) to Zenon. — 0.08 m. X 0.285 m. — Date: May, 257 B.C. (?).

The name of the writer is lost, but the handwriting is that of Nikon (see no. 59049, plate XV). Apollonios had ordered him to take over some sheep from Artemidoros, and he asks Zenon to inform Apollonios that they have no sheep-pens and to give an order for the supply of building materials. From the expression "inform him" (*emphasison autoi*) I infer that the letter was written in the month of <term>Phamenoth</term> <note>The seventh month of the Egyptian calendar</note> of 257 B.C., when Zenon was in attendance on Apollonios, rather than in 256 B.C., when he was residing at Philadelphia. (Regnal year 29 began about the 29th of Phamenoth and ended about the 17th of the next Phamenoth).

->[Nikon] to Zenon, greetings. Apollonios wrote to us to receive from Artemi-<-
->doros sheep...<-
->...inform him therefore that there are no sheep-<-
->pens. If therefore...<-

*Line 1. Artemidoros* : see also no. 59136.

### 59069. List of articles. — 0 m. 31 $\times$ 0 m. 31. — Date : about 13th May, 257 B. C.

The list, which is written in a large hand on a large sheet of papyrus, was drawn up at Hermopolis on the voyage down from Memphis to Alexandria. The heading says: ‘we have left behind the following articles which Charmas has handed over to Apollodotos’. Charmas is well-known as a commercial agent of Apollonios and Zenon. If the articles belonged to a cargo which he had brought from abroad, Apollodotos might be identical with the author of no. 59036; but this is very doubtful; there may have been another Apollodotos living at Hermopolis and the articles may have belonged to the miscellaneous stores carried by Apollonios’ party (see nos. 59087-89).

The writing is along the fibres of the front of the papyrus, as is usually the case when a text is written in columns.

->Column I.<-

> Year 29, Xandikos 11,
> in Hermopolis. We have left behind
> the items which Charmas handed over
> to Apollodotos.
> 5 In a <term>basket</term> <note>original: "θίβει" (thibei) - a chest or basket made of reeds</note>, 5 small bags of <term>nard</term> <note>original: "νάρδου" (nardou) - an expensive aromatic ointment</note>, sealed
> and 1 small pouch, sealed
> 1 pouch of <term>gazelle</term> [items], sealed <note>original: "δορκαδέων" (dorkadeōn) - likely referring to knuckle-bones or products made from gazelle</note>
> purple [wool or dye] in one pillow
> 1 variegated <term>girth</term> <note>original: "κιρία" (kiria) - a woven band or strap, often for bedding</note>

*Line 5.* basket: the 'e' was corrected over an 'i'.
*Line 8.* purple: the 'p' has been corrected.
*Line 9.* girth: the usual spelling in the Zenon papyri (see *P.S.I.*, 387, 4 and 616, 33). For the half-girths compare half-towels in *P.S.I.*, 387, 5, half-brushes and half-ribbons in *P.S.I.*, 858, 3, 9 (a papyrus which is probably, like ours, one of the

<header>ZENON PAPYRI.</header>
<columns>2</columns>

### 59070. Personal description. — 0 m. 045 $\times$ 0 m. 10. — Date : about 28th May, 257 B. C.

A slip of papyrus containing a <term>description</term> <note>original: "εἰκών" (eikōn) - a formal list of physical features used for identification</note>, perhaps of a runaway slave. There are two duplicates in the British Museum (*Invent.* 2353 B and 2356 B). Written across the fibres and folded parallel to the writing.

> [Year 2]9, Xandikos 26. Thorax, a Cilician:
> [stature] <unclear>tall</unclear>, [complexion] <unclear>honey-colored</unclear>, [hair] <unclear>straight</unclear>, [face] <unclear>round</unclear>
> [face] <unclear>round</unclear>, a scar under the <unclear>left</unclear> eyebrow
> [and on the ri]ght, and under the eye, age 18.

*Line 2. Full reading:* Straight-haired, honey-skinned, round-faced, a scar under the left eyebrow.

*Line 3.* Not on the right [hand/side].

<meta>Bibl. : Journal d’entrée du Musée, no. 48518.</meta>

<summary>This page contains an inventory of luxury goods (including nard, myrrh, and saffron) left at Hermopolis and a formal physical description of a young Cilician man, likely intended to track a runaway.</summary>
