## 59071. Letter from Nikon to Zenon. — 0 m. 08 x 0 m. 08. — Date of reception : about 30th May, 257 B. C.

Only a fragment of this letter, comprising the beginnings of the lines and the docket, is in our collection. The right hand portion belongs to a newly purchased lot which is to be divided between the British Museum, Columbia University and the University of Michigan; but through the courtesy of the purchasers I am allowed to publish it here. It measures 0 m. 075 x 0 m. 19, and a clay sealing was found attached to it.
Nikon, the author of no. 59049, had left some <term>trays</term> <note>original: "ἀβάκεια" (abakeia) – these could be sideboards, serving trays, or shallow dishes</note> in the hands of Artemidoros the <term>table-servant</term> <note>original: "ἐλέατρος" (eleatros) – a specialized household official, likely a carver or one who arranged the tables; see no. 59059</note> and now asks Zenon to get the price of them from Artemidoros and send it to him, or, if they have not been sold, to return them. I suspect that this Artemidoros was the author of *Papyri of the Italian Society (P.S.I.)*, 326, which is slightly later in date, and it is even possible that the <term>silverware</term> <note>original: "ἀργυρώματα" (argyromata)</note>, about whose sale he is negotiating, are the trays of Nikon. From the docket on the present letter we learn that the travellers had now returned to Alexandria.

->To the table-servant<-

Nikon to Zenon, greetings. [We have writ]ten to Artemidoros about the price of the trays, which we deposited with him, to give [the money to you, but if] we have not sold them, the trays themselves. If then you receive them, send them to us, unless [there is some hindrance] to you [ ... ]

->Farewell.<-

BACK SIDE (VERSO):
<margin>5</margin> Nikon concerning the price of trays, [to Zeno]n.
To be received from Artemidoros.
Year 29, Xandikos 28, in Alexandria. <note>Xandikos was a Macedonian month; in the 29th year of Ptolemy II, this date fell in late May</note>

*Line 2.* "We have sold": the original uses the first person plural <note>original: "πεπράκαμεν" (peprakamen)</note> rather than "he sold" or "you all sold," because Nikon thinks of himself as the real seller.
*Line 3.* For example, [hindrance].

BIBLIOGRAPHY: *Journal d'entrée du Musée*, no. 48519.

## 59072. Letter to Zenon. — 0 m. 105 x 0 m. 22. — Date : May-June, 257 B. C.

The writer acknowledges receipt of a consignment of shoots (?), brought to Philadelphia by donkey, and informs Zenon that he is bringing water from Tanis <note>This refers to Tanis in the Fayum region, not the famous city in the Delta</note> to irrigate the young olive trees. One is tempted to ascribe the letter to Panakestor <note>Zenon's predecessor as manager of the estate at Philadelphia</note>, but the hand is not identical with that of Panakestor in *The Petrie Papyri (P. Petr.)*, II, 13, 5 and in no. 59124. It must, however, have come either from Panakestor or from another of Apollonios'

---

<page-num>95</page-num>
<header>ZENON PAPYRI.</header>

agents at Philadelphia, such as Maron, of whose letters we have no authenticated specimen in Cairo.
The edges of the papyrus are ragged and probably both ends have perished.

[To Zenon] greetings. We received the pack-animals [ ... ] sent to us for Philadelphia, being [ ... ] seventeen they brought us <term>unmarked</term> <note>original: "ἀχάρακτα" (acharakta) – this may mean the plants were unlabelled, or perhaps 'unstaked,' meaning they lacked the wooden supports usually used for young trees</note>. We wrote to you therefore [ ... ] all the olive plants are flourishing and we are watering them,
<margin>5</margin> [ ... ] bringing the water from Tanis. Farewell. Year 29, Pharmouthi. <note>Pharmouthi is an Egyptian month corresponding here to May or June</note>

BACK SIDE (VERSO):
[ ... ] to Zenon.

*Lines 1-2.* For example, "carrying the plants."
*Lines 2-3.* For example, "being forty, and others."
*Line 3.* "Unmarked": perhaps 'unlabelled'.
*Line 6.* The letter "m" ($\mu$) may be a numeral.

BIBLIOGRAPHY: *Journal d'entrée du Musée*, no. 48520.

## 59073. Letter to Zenon. — 0 m. 265 x 0 m. 23. — Date : May-June, 257 B. C.

This fragment is in the same hand as no. 59072 and in even worse preservation. The writer seems to be speaking about work on the Philadelphian estate and about certain difficulties which he has encountered. He mentions Zoilos the Arsinoite <term>oeconome</term> <note>original: "οἰκονόμος" (oikonomos) – a high-ranking financial official in the Ptolemaic administration</note> and Artemidoros, the <term>administrator of the land grant</term> <note>original: "ὁ ἐπὶ τῆς συντάξεως" (ho epi tes syntaxeos) – likely the official in charge of the land assigned to military settlers (cleruchs)</note> in Memphis. The latter title probably means administrator of the land granted to the <term>cleruchs</term> <note>military settlers given land by the King in exchange for service</note> in the land grant; see the similar titles collected and discussed by Lesquier in *Military Institutions of the Ptolemies*, p. 196.

[To Zen]on greetings. If you are well and other things are to your [liking, we] give much thanks [to the gods; we ourselves are healthy] ... money neither for the <term>cross-dykes</term> <note>original: "διαχώματα" (diachomata) – internal embankments within the irrigation system used to manage water flow into specific plots</note> ...
<margin>5</margin> [ ... at Philadel]phia because Zoilos the oeconome does not provide [funds]. For he says [ ... ] of these things. Take care then so that [ ... ] be written to me so I may know. Having arrived at Memphis [ ... ] thinking we would receive something of a talent [ ... ] did not agree [ ... ]

*Line 2.* Probably "we give much thanks to the gods."
*Line 3.* "Cross-dykes": as in *P.S.I.*, 337. See Schnebel, *Agriculture*, p. 36.
*Line 5.* Probably "at Philadelphia."

### 59074. Letter from Lykourgos to Zenon. — 0 m. 085 x 0 m. 085 and 0 m. 085 x 0 m. 15. — Date of reception: about 16th June, 257 B. C.

Lykourgos had already written to Zenon asking him to recover a sum of money from Agathokles after giving back to him some silver plate. Evidently the money had been lent on the security of the plate (<term>pledged collateral</term> <note>original: "κείμενα ἐνέχυρα" (keimena enechyra)</note> as in *P. S. I.*, 608, 3). If Zenon has not carried out the former request, he is now to give the plate to Hermias, the bearer of the letter, who will himself recover the money. As Zenon notes in the <term>docket</term> <note>A filing note written on the back of the papyrus</note> that the plate is to be given to Hermias, we may infer that he had paid no attention to the former letter.
The papyrus is in two pieces. I have restored the <term>lacunae</term> <note>Gaps or missing parts of the text</note> provisionally in order to make the sense clear.

> Lykourgos to Zen[on, greetings. If you are we]ll, it would be good; I too am healthy. We wrote
> to you
> ev[en previous]ly ab[out the silver plate, so that having recover]ed the <term>cash</term> <note>original: "κερμάτιον" (kermation) — small change or ready money</note>
> from Agathokles
> you might return it to him. I[f therefore you did this, it] would be well; but if not, give it
> to Hermias, the one deliver-
> ing the letter to you, [so that he may recover from him th]e cash.
> 5 Farewell.

<column-break/>

<page-num>97</page-num>
<header>ZENON PAPYRI.</header>

->ON THE BACK:<-
->Lykourgos concerning silver plate, [to Zenon.]<-
->So that it may be given to Hermias. Year 29, Artemisios 16,<-
->in Alexandria.<-

### 59075. Letter from Toubias to Apollonios. — 0 m. 25 x 0 m. 35. — Date: about 12th May, 257 B. C. (plates XVII, XVIII).

Toubias the <term>Ammonite</term> <note>The Ammonites were a people living in the region of modern-day Amman, Jordan; Toubias was a powerful local chieftain or "sheikh" allied with the Ptolemies</note> chief announces that, in response to Apollonios' letter, he has sent to the king, who was a great collector of strange animals, a gift consisting of horses, dogs, asses, and various specimens of cross-breeding with the wild ass, all of them domesticated. He also sends a letter to the king about the gift, to be delivered through Apollonios; and to reassure him about the contents of this letter he gives a copy of it below. It contains in fact nothing but a list of the animals.
The letter is written in a beautiful, large hand, no doubt by a Greek scribe, with frequent interspacings, especially on each side of the numerals.

> Toubias to Apollonios, greetings. Just as you wrote to me to sen[d...
> ] month, I have sent <gloss>the one from us</gloss> on the tenth [of the month] of Xandikos
> [Aineas bringing] two horses, [si]x dogs, one <term>half-wild-ass</term> <note>original: "ἡμιονάγριον" (hemionagrion) — a hybrid animal</note>
> from a donkey, two white Arabian <term>pack animals</term> <note>original: "ὑποζύγια" (hypozygia)</note>, [two] colts from a half-wild-ass,
> 5 one colt from a wild ass; and these are domesticated. I have also sent [to you]
> the letter written by us concerning the gift[s]
> for the King, and likewise the copies of it so that you may know.
> Farewell. Year 29, Xandikos 10.

*Line 2. To make this line of the same length as lines 3, 4 it would be necessary to read "month" after Xandikos. But it is probable that the lines were not all exactly equal.*
*Line 3. Aineas: restored from document no. 59074, on the assumption that the animals and the slaves were despatched together.*
*Line 4. Pack animals: Father Vincent suggests that these were camels. But in the papyri "hypozygion" is the ordinary word for donkey; and if camels had been sent, I think they would have been called "kameloi" (camels).*
*Lines 5-6. These lines, as restored, are shorter than the two preceding ones, but see note on line 2.*

<meta>Catalogue of the Museum, no. 59001.</meta>

### 59076. Letter from Toubias to Apollonios. — (a) 0 m. 225 x 0 m. 195; (b) 0 m. 22 x 0 m. 15. — Date: about 12^th^ May, 257 B. C.

> In this letter, written on the same day as no. 59075 and received along with it on the 16^th^ of June, Toubias informs Apollonios <note>The dioiketes or finance minister of Egypt</note> that he has sent him (not for the king, but for himself) a eunuch and four boy slaves of a superior class, whose descriptions are given below. It seems probable that the present letter was written before the other; for, as I have already pointed out, the effusive greeting with which it begins is to be taken as an introduction to both (cf. 59036, 9, 19). The hand is the same as that of no. 59075. Fragment (a) has already appeared as no. 84 of my *Selected Papyri*. Fragment (b), comprising the right half of the text, came to light among a new lot of Zenon papyri (see no. 59071, introduction) and is published here, from my copy, by kind permission of the purchasers. The letter is now almost complete.

Toubias to Ap[ollonios, greetings. If you are well and all your affairs]
and the rest of your [concerns are to your mind, it is a gr]eat cause of thanks to the gods; I too
am in good health, con[stantly keeping a mem]ory of you, as is only right. I have sen-
t you, with Aine[as as the escort, o]ne [eunuch] and four boy [slaves...]-tic
<margin>5</margin> and of noble birth, of whom [there are] two uncircumcised. We have also
written below for you the descriptions of the b[oy sl]aves so that you may know them.
->Farewell. Year 29, 10th of Xandikos.<-

*Line 8.* Haimos: cf. <term>JUVENAL</term>, III, 99, <note>original: "cum molli Demetrius Hæmo" — "with the soft Demetrius Haemus"</note>. The names, which are not Greek, may perhaps serve to indicate the region from which the boys came.

### 59077. Letter from Krotos to Zenon. — 0 m. 115 x 0 m. 28. — Date: about 7^th^ May, 257 B. C. (plate XVIII).

> Krotos, who writes from somewhere abroad, was a commercial agent of Apollonios or Zenon. In no. 59093, 7 we find him in Joppa <note>Modern Jaffa, Israel</note>; in *P. S. I.*, 863 (*g*) he is mentioned in connection with the harbor of Gaza. He complains here that Alexis has taken no steps with regard to the judgment given against the persons who sailed in charge of the oil, neither repaying the money nor handing over the slave girl whom they had offered as security. Moreover, he has let Theron, the sailor from the *cybaea* <note>A type of merchant ship</note>, escape from prison and disappear from sight.

Krotos to Zenon, greetings. Know that Alexis has done nothing regarding
the slave girl whom we took as security from those who sailed with the o[il,]

*Line 1.* Alexis: mentioned again in no. 59008, 38, in connection with oil. He may have been the exporter of the oil; at any rate he seems to have had some responsibility in the trade.
*Line 2.* oil: for the importation of foreign oil into Egypt see nos. 59012, 59015. The cargo mentioned here probably came from Syria or Palestine (cf. *Revenue Laws*, column 54, 17).

## 59078. Letter from Charmos to Zenon. — 0.155 m x 0.25 m. — Date: July, 257 B.C.

Charmos, probably the "servant Charmos" mentioned in the <term>P.S.I.</term> <note>Papiri della Società Italiana</note> collection no. 489, was an agent employed by Zenon on all sorts of business. But the present letter is so badly preserved that it is difficult to say where and in what circumstances it was written.

Charmos to Zenon, greetings. [Regarding] the bedding [I have] sent the account to you, having received it from Artemon... having given [it] to Theodoros... Theodoros does not follow... 5 [so] that you are not in doubt about the price, I have written to you. Regarding the calves and the [sacrificial] pigs and the rest of the items purchased... he says that after bringing them out <note>perhaps meaning exporting them</note> he will give an account tomorrow. As soon as I receive it, [I will send it].
->Farewell. Year 29, Daisios [ ... ]<-

*Line 3. "to release" <note>original: "ἀφεῖναι"</note> is a possible reading. Theodoros may be the assistant mentioned in documents no. 59062 and no. 59089.*
*Line 7. Possibly "to the temple of Isis" <note>original: "εἰς τὰ Ἰσιεῖα"</note>. But it may be that "bringing them out" refers to the export of goods from Alexandria.*

*BIBLIOGRAPHY: Journal of Entries of the Museum, no. 48526.*

<column-break/>

## 59079. Fragment. — 0.105 m x 0.075 m. — Date: July, 257 B.C.

The writing is along the fibers and the papyrus was folded horizontally, so probably the lines were short. The text seems to have contained a list of articles, but it is too fragmentary to be of any interest, were it not for the note of reception at the top: ‘Received in the Arsinoe of Dion, year 29, Daisios’. On the 14th of Daisios Zenon was still in Alexandria (<term>P.S.I.</term>, 503); on the 20th, 21st and 23rd he was docketing his correspondence in a place called Arsinoe, and <term>P.S.I.</term>, 558 shows that he was travelling with Apollonios <note>The finance minister of Egypt</note>. Between the 23rd of Daisios and the 24th of Gorpiaios, when the main party had halted at Athribis (no. 59087, 24), we have no clue to the movements of the travellers, and we cannot even be sure that they had not returned in the meantime to Alexandria.
It is not clear where this Arsinoe was situated. Vitelli <note>a famous Italian papyrologist</note> thinks that it was the village of Arsinoe in the Fayoum (<term>P.S.I.</term>, 584) and Rostovtzeff <note>a renowned historian of the ancient economy</note> is inclined to identify it with the town of Krokodilopolis (<term>Large Estate</term>, pp. 39, 40). But we know that Zenon had not yet gone to live in the Fayoum, and neither of these views seems to me probable. On the other hand my own suggestion (<term>P. Edg.</term>, 15, intro.) that Arsinoe lay in Cyprus or Syria is based on very slender indications, the mention of Pelusium in no. 59081, of "the manager from Cyprus" in <term>P.S.I.</term>, 505, and the mention of a place which I take to be this same Arsinoe in a papyrus of year 26 (<term>P.S.I.</term>, 553), when Zenon seems to have been engaged on foreign service (see no. 59002). The present text, which speaks of the town as the Arsinoe of Dion, adds one detail to our information. If we could identify him with the Ptolemaic general who lost Damascus, his connection with the place would be in favor of my suggestion. But at present I confess to having no clear idea who this Dion was or where this Arsinoe lay.

->Received in the Arsinoe of<-
->Dion, Year 29, Daisios<-

->] [<-
->the [books?] of Dionysios<-
->5 [ ... ] and [ ... ]<-
->of tables [ ... ]<-
->] [<-
->]. . . . . . [<-

*Lines 1-2. Added in a small hand, by Zenon or his clerk.*

*BIBLIOGRAPHY: Journal of Entries of the Museum, no. 48527.*

**59080. Letter from Iollas to Zenon.** — (*a*) 0.125 m x 0.16 m; (*b*) 0.125 m x 0.16 m. — Date of reception: about 21st July, 257 B.C.

This is a fragmentary letter from Iollas the weaver about Addaios, who appears to have been an agent of Apollonios <note>The finance minister of Egypt under Ptolemy II</note> at Memphis. Iollas had fallen into disgrace and had been severely flogged. He had intended to run away to Zenon for protection, but Zenodoros had prevented him and made him write a letter instead. It is only from the docket <note>The summary written on the back of the papyrus</note> that we learn that the letter was about Addaios, but we may suppose that he was the person by whose order Iollas had been punished. The letter should be compared with *P. S. I.* <note>original: "Papiri della Società Italiana"</note>, 854, an obscure and fragmentary report from Addaios himself about weaving, slave girls, Zenodoros and Iollas. Whether Zenodoros is identical with the man mentioned in no. 59090 remains to be seen; if so, he cannot have been one of the permanent residents at Memphis. From *P. S. I.*, 854 one gets the impression that he was an expert in weaving, from whom Iollas was unwilling to learn.
Fragment (*b*) is not in our collection, but belongs to the recently purchased lot of Zenon papyri (see no. 59071, intro.). It is ill preserved and my copy given below is subject to correction. The break down the right side of (*a*) is nearly straight; the other break is irregular, as shown in the transcript; but I am unable to say how much is missing in the middle.

[Iol]las to Zenon, greetings. I gave to yo[u <note>Possible reconstruction: "an oath concerning the disobedience"</note>] and that I was being spoken of badly by Zenodoros. Bu[t when] I heard that he was angry with me, I fell a[t <note>Possible reconstruction: "the doors of Addaios and at"</note>] his knees and took hold of them; for he did not allow me [to enter?].
<gloss>Zenodoros to him.</gloss>
[[by force]] now therefore I [obey] the orders of Zen[odoros <note>Possible reconstruction: "having learned from you"</note>] ... he no longer listened to Zenodoros, [but brought in]
<gloss>To Zenodoros.</gloss>
five Egyptian men bearing whips [ ... ] whip-ping me arro[gantly]
5 [so] that he even cut my skin and no[w ...] he is treat-ed even worse by [him].
I was [in]tending to run away and come to you [ ... ] [Z]enodoros preven-ted me, so that we might not aban[don] the work,

*Line 1. E. g., I gave to yo[u an oath concerning the disobedience].*
*Line 2. E. g., at [the doors of Addaios and at] the knees. The last word might be [to enter]. The first "i" of the word for "angry" <note>original: "ὀργίζει"</note> is written over an "e".*
*Line 3. E. g., Zen[odoros, having learned from you, no] longer listened to Zenodoros, [but brought in].*
*Line 4. Probably "whipping" rather than "I was whipping," as in the next line we find the word "has cut".*
*Line 6. "abandon the work" <note>original: "ἐφήκωμεν"</note>: 'not to interrupt the work'.*

### 59085. Order from Philon to Sosibios. — 0.075 m x 0.095 m. — Date: about 14th September, 257 B.C.

Philon the baker asks Sosibios to give to Midas thirty bundles of dry reeds for firewood. A similar order was given the next day (see no. 59086). One can only guess what the circumstances were; but possibly Philon was travelling with Apollonios and Zenon (see *Cornell Papyri*, 1, *passim*), and Sosibios was a storekeeper or local purveyor who provided firewood every day or two on presentation of a written order, which he could keep as a voucher for his records. The name Midas, which was considered "inappropriate" or foreign for a free Greek, indicates that he was a slave, perhaps of Phrygian origin. The writing is along the fibers and the papyrus is folded parallel to the writing.

> <margin>17</margin> Philon to Sosibios,
> greetings. Give
> to Midas 30 bundles
> of brushwood reeds.
> <margin>5</margin> Year 29, Loios 17.

**VERSO:**
->To Sosibios.<-

<meta>BIBLIOGRAPHY: Journal of the Museum Entry, no. 48533.</meta>

### 59087. Account of linen. — 0.285 m x 0.195 m. — Date: 258-257 B.C. (plate XIX).

This is an account of linen garments and napkins issued from the store over a period of fourteen months. It was presumably preceded by a statement of the quantities which the writer had in his charge (compare no. 59088). The articles were given out for the most part during the tour of Apollonios <note>The finance minister of Egypt</note> and Zenon. Not only are the entries dated, but the scribe usually mentions the place where they were staying at the time. As Zenon followed the same practice in filing his correspondence during these months, we can reconstruct a fairly good itinerary of the first tour, which ended at Alexandria in the month of Xandikos of Year 29. However, we are not as well informed about the later movements of Apollonios and Zenon in Year 29 after their second departure from Alexandria.

> From these, Year 28, Panemos
> 22nd, in the city of Nikiou, Zenon
> <margin>[</margin> ]... having ordered Helenos
> the Ethiopian, 1 fine linen tunic <note>original: "chitōn byssinos"</note>
> <margin>5</margin> Gorpiaios 18, in Krokodilopolis
> for Zenon, which he sent
> for the reception of Kriton
> linen hand-towels <note>original: "cheiromaktra byssina"</note>
> Hyperberetaios 7 ...
> <margin>10</margin> ... linen hand-towels
> Dios 15, in Memphis, A...
> 1 fine linen tunic
> Audnaios 20, to Patron
> to convey to the ...
> <margin>15</margin> linen hand-towels

*Line 2. The city of Nikiou* : the capital of the Prosopite district <note>A "nome" or administrative division of Egypt</note>. The mounds of Zawiet Razin probably mark its site today.
*Lines 3-4. Helenos the Ethiopian* : mentioned again in other papyri as a servant of Apollonios.
*Line 5.* This date probably coincided with the 1st of Thoth in the Egyptian calendar.
*Line 7.* Restored from another account belonging to the same group.
*Line 9.* The place is not mentioned, which may mean they were still in the Fayoum region.
*Line 10. Perhaps "the men's quarters."*
*Line 13.* They were now at the harbor of Berenike, though this is not explicitly stated. Patron was likely the ship's captain mentioned in other documents.
*Line 14. For example, "to his light boat."*

### **59088. Account of rugs.** — 0.23 m x 0.08 m. — Date: 258-257 B.C.

This is the upper part of an account of rugs received and issued from the store during the tour of Apollonios. One sees from the dates that the party must have made long halts at the important towns on their route; and, as we know from the *Cornell Papyri* what a retinue of secretaries and accountants accompanied the finance minister <note>original: "dioiketes"</note>, a great amount of public business was no doubt being transacted all the time. The accounts filed by Zenon are of course private accounts, but here and there, as in lines 7-10 of the present text, we get a glimpse into matters of wider interest.

### **59089. Account of myrrh.** — 0.315 m x 0.21 m. — Date: 257 B.C.

In lines 1-6, the writer states that he has received from Zenon (no doubt on his return from Mendes to Memphis towards the end of Year 28) a certain quantity of Mendesian myrrh in small lead vessels. In lines 7-21, he gives an account of the quantities which he has dispensed to various persons in the first four months of Year 29, after Apollonios' party had returned to Alexandria.

Year 28, Dystros 20, in Memphis.
I have from Zenon:
of Mendesian myrrh, 26 lead flasks <note>original: "alabastrous"</note>
of one-cotyle capacity <note>A cotyle is roughly half a pint or 0.24 liters.</note>,
<margin>5</margin> and 1 two-cotyle [flask],
and 5 half-cotyle flasks.

From this: Year 29, Xandikos
] in Alexandria, to Zenon for [

*Line 3.* Mendesian myrrh: Mendes <note>A city in the Nile Delta.</note> was evidently famous for its myrrh. In the *Italian Society Papyri* (333), Promethion writes that he is sending a consignment which Apollonios had ordered him to buy, and we know that Promethion lived in Mendes.
*Line 4.* It is interesting to learn that myrrh was sometimes stored in flasks <note>original: "alabastroi"</note> made of lead, and it is worth mentioning that we have in the Museum a lead vase lately found at Mendes (*Journal of the Museum Entry*, no. 48823).

### 59090. Account. — 0.18 m x 0.14 m. — Date: 258-257 B.C.

Upper part of an account of money received by Zenodoros through Peisikles. The latter was a sort of treasurer in the service of Apollonios <note>The finance minister of Egypt.</note> and the former was also a travelling member of the household. On the 11th of Panemos, when Apollonios and Zenon were at Nikiou, we find him not far away at the apex of the Delta, and on the 7th of Loios he was in Krokodilopolis <note>The "City of Crocodiles" in the Faiyum oasis.</note> either with or in advance of the main party (see no. 59087). He is perhaps to be identified with the Zenodoros who appears in *P.S.I.*, 854 and no. 59080, in connection with the weaving establishment at Memphis, and also with the Zenodoros who writes to Zenon from Alexandria in year 35 (*P.S.I.*, 364).

->This is the account of what Zenodoros has<-
->through Peisikles, Year 28<-
Artemisiou 11: 140 silver drachmae
Daisios 23: for bedding,
<margin>5</margin> 100 silver drachmae
Panemou 3: 40 silver drachmae
11th, at the Delta

*Line 7.* Compare Strabo, 788: <note>original: "καλεῖται Δέλτα..."</note> "It is called the Delta because of the similarity of its shape; the area at the peak is called by the same name because it is the start of the aforementioned shape, and the village situated upon it is also called Delta."

### 59092. List of Zenon’s clothes. — 0.335 m x 0.185 m.

This list of the contents of Zenon’s portmanteau was given or sent to him by Peisikles, and perhaps the clothes described in it were his outfit for one of his long journeys with Apollonios. They are of various kinds and colours, both for summer and for winter, for indoors and outdoors, some new, some worn and others partly worn. It is a curious document, giving us a glimpse into the wardrobe of a rich but economical Alexandrian. Evidently the <term>chlamys</term> <note>A short cloak worn by men, often for riding or military use.</note> and the <term>chiton</term> <note>A basic tunic, the primary garment for men and women.</note> were the most important...

## 59093. Letter from Herakleitos to Zenon. — 0.225 m x 0.34 m. — Date: 257 B.C. (Plate 20).

For Herakleitos see no. 59003, line 8 and *P.S.I.*, 495. After describing his troubles in acquiring a new horse he proceeds: 
> "Apollophanes also has arrived in Syria and on our expedition to Massyas we have met him in Sidon and told him that Krotos is waiting in Joppa for an opportunity of exporting ... and mattresses. He replied that he could not sail there at present, for he had been ordered by Dionysodoros to bring his baggage to Herakleia. He informed us too that those at home were all well. And Menekles of Tyre, he said, had brought some slaves and merchandise from Gaza to Tyre and landed them in Tyre for transshipment without notifying the Customs officers and without having a permit to export the slaves, and on learning this they had taken them from him. So Apollophanes coming to the aid of Menekles declared that the slaves and the merchandise were yours, and Menekles was obliged to back him up."
The passage is far from clear, but the meaning seems to be that the declaration was a dodge to get the slaves out of the hands of the Customs officers. Herakleitos ends with a complaint about some delay in giving him an appointment, saying that Nikanor has treated him as if he were an enemy.

Herakleitos to Zenon, greetings. [If you yourself are] well and everything else for you is going 
according to plan, it would be as we wish. We too are [healthy], and we continue 
to make mention of you 
at every opportunity. Regarding the little horse, we asked Nikanor 
so that 
he might exchange it for us. Since he would not allow it, [we were forced] to buy a horse for 800 <note>The text uses symbols for 500 and 300 drachmae; this was a very high price for the period.</note> drachmae and 
brand it. 
5 But the horse from you is worn out and [...] Apollophanes has also 
arrived in Syria, 
and while we were traveling to Massyas [we met] him in Sidon and reported 
that Krotos is in Joppa, wishing to [...] export [...] and 
mattresses. 
He said, therefore, that he could no longer sail there, for he had been [ordered] by Dionysodoros 
to bring the baggage to Herakleia. He reported to us 
10 also concerning those at home that [all] are well. And Menekles, who is in Tyre...

Lines 3-4. Compare with *P.S.I.*, 495, lines 14-15, where Nikanor promises Herakleitos, without effect, to see that he does not lack for a horse. The price mentioned, 800 drachmae, is surprisingly high.
Line 6. <term>Massyas</term> <note>A valley region between the Lebanon and Anti-Lebanon mountains.</note>: see Wilcken in *Archive*, volume VI, page 451.
Line 9. <term>Herakleia</term> <note>Likely Herakleia in Phoenicia, near Latakia.</note>: compare with no. 59088, line 9.

<meta>Catalog of the Museum, no. 59001. Signature: 15.</meta>

...he said that having brought some <term>slaves</term> <note>original: "somatia," literally "small bodies," a standard term for slaves in this period</note> and goods himself from Gaza to Tyre, he unloaded them at Tyre without reporting to the tax collectors or possessing an export permit for the slaves; and when they [the officials] found out, they confiscated them from him. Apollophanes then came to Menekles and claimed the slaves and the goods belonged to you; <margin>15</margin> because of this, Menekles took possession of them. I have written to you, therefore, so that you may instruct Apollophanes not to register anything in your name, unless you think it is to your advantage. And know that we have suffered much hardship and were only just registered in the month of Daisios, on the 29th of Year 29 <note>around July 257 B.C.</note>. For Nikanor has treated us as if he were dealing with an enemy. You would do well to take care of yourself so that you stay healthy. <margin>20</margin> ->Farewell.<-

->VERSO:<-
->From Herakleitos.                       To Zenon.<-

*Line 15. took possession of them* : corrected by Vitelli from a misspelling in the first edition.
*Line 16. to you* : corrected by Grenfell from "to someone" in the first edition.
*Line 17. registered* : see similar usage in other Zenon papyri. The appointment sought by Herakleitos remains uncertain. I suggested formerly, on certain indications, that he wished for a post in the cavalry. This seems possible; but on the other hand Herakleitos strikes us as having been one of Apollonios' commercial agents.

### 59094. Duplicate receipt. — 0.33 m x 0.09 m. — Date: 20th August, 257 B.C. (plate XXII).

Written across the fibers and folded horizontally. Found with the inner text tied up and secured by three clay sealings (see plate XXII). The designs are indistinct: on 1) an object like a palmette; on 2) a pointed curving object; on 3) a bearded head, broken. Below the outer text is a Egyptian translation in Demotic with the Egyptian date, Year 28, Payni 29.
The text informs us that Kleitarchos, an agent of Damis, and Diodoros, employed on the estate of Apollonios, have received from the threshing-floor of Inaros, a native of Moithymis, one <term>artab</term> <note>a dry measure of capacity, roughly 30-40 liters</note> of Syrian wheat, 27 1/2 of local wheat and 45 1/3 artabs

<column-break/>

<page-num>115</page-num>
<header>ZENON PAPYRI.</header>

of barley. The object of the payment (it may have been rent) is not specified; but we know that about this time Damis and Diodoros were taking an active part in the development of the Philadelphian estate.
The inner text is written in the ordinary cursive script employed in such documents, whereas the outer text is written in a more ornamental hand, the most characteristic feature of which is the letter delta with a high crest.

->In the reign of Ptolemy<-
->son of Ptolemy the Savior, Year<-
->29, month of Payni 29. Kleitarchos<-

<margin>20</margin> ->of local wheat twenty-seven<-

*Line 3. Payni* : the scribe misspelled the month in the Greek text.
*Line 4. For Damis, see P.S.I., 500. In P.S.I., 518 (of year 35) he appears as a joint-district administrator (nomarch) with Etearchos.*
*Line 8. Moithymis* : from the village of Moithymis (modern Meidoum) in the Memphite district.
*Line 8. Syrian wheat* : Syrian wheat grown in Egypt; otherwise it would not have been taken to the threshing-floor of a native peasant.
*Line 9. Note on the fractional notation used for "half."*
*Line 18. Pais* : the letter 't' was corrected from 'd'. But the spelling of the name "Inaros" remains as it was in the inner text.*

## 59096. Letter from Zoilos to Zenon. — 0.125 m x 0.32 m. — Date: 22nd September, 257 B.C. (Plate XXI).

Zoilos, the Arsinoite <term>œconome</term> <note>a state official responsible for local financial administration</note>, asks Zenon to write and inform him whether Apollonios means to spend the festival of Arsinoe at Krokodilopolis, or in that district, in order that he may make preparations for the visit in good time.
From this and from other documents (see *Edgar Papyri*, 18, introduction) it appears that the <term>Arsinoeia</term> <note>a festival established in honor of Queen Arsinoe II</note> were celebrated in the second half of the month of Mesore. We do not know where Apollonios and Zenon were staying when the letter was written. It was received by Zenon on the 16th of Gorpiaios (corresponding to Mesore 23); and, if *P.S.I.*, 559 is correctly dated by Vitelli, he was in Philadelphia on the 14th of Mesore, while Apollonios was not far off. On the other hand, Apollonios' party seems to have been in Athribis on the 24th of Gorpiaios (see no. 59087, 24); and as the figure 29 in *P.S.I.*, 559 is marked as doubtful, it is just possible that the letter belongs to year 28 (compare no. 59087, 5). It remains uncertain, therefore, whether Apollonios and Zenon actually spent the festival in the Fayoum.

<column-break/>

<page-num>117</page-num>
<header>ZENON PAPYRI.</header>

->Zoilos to Zenon [greetings. If Apollonios]<-
->is well, and you, and those you wish,<-
->it would be as we [would wish; and] we ourselves are also doing well. And you would<-
->do well to write to [us concerning Apollon]ios, if he is celebrating the festival with us,<-
->5 so that we may prepare the things for [his arrival] and not suddenly<-
->be caught unprepared.<-
->Farewell. Year 29, Mesore 2.<-

**Verso:**

->Year 29, Gorpiaios 16. From Zoilos, To Zenon.<-
->If Apollonios will celebrate<-
->the Arsinoeia with him.<-
Above the docket : [[Of Apollonios]].

*Lines 1-2. Apollonios is probable, the other restorations more or less uncertain.*
*Line 3. The festival took place towards the end of Mesore, perhaps on the 27th (see P. Edg., 18, introduction).*
*Lines 8-9. Apollonios and Arsinoeia are written in monograms <note>shorthand symbols combining several letters</note>.*

<meta>Bibl. : Journal d'entrée du Musée, no. 48545; P. Edg., 18.</meta>

## 59097. Letter from Zoilos to Panakestor. — 0.135 m x 0.16 m. — Date of reception: 6th October, 257 B.C.

Zoilos was at this time the Arsinoite <term>œconome</term> and Panakestor the manager of Apollonios' private property in the Fayoum. When in the course of year 29 Zenon came to live at Philadelphia as Apollonios' representative, he apparently took over the documents which he found in Panakestor's office and filed them along with his own papers (*Edgar Papyri*, 19, introduction). This explains why among the papyri of year 29 we possess so many letters addressed to Panakestor and so many receipts from farmers and laborers concerning agricultural work at Philadelphia.
There may be a connection between the present fragment and *P.S.I.*, 559, a letter from Panakestor to Zenon, which begins: "When you had gone away to Philadelphia, Apollonios instructed us to sell the fodder." But see the note on no. 59096.

->Zoilos to Panakestor, greetings. . [<-
->of the fodder which Apollonios wrote [<-
->of the <term>sitologos</term> <note>a government official in charge of a grain storehouse</note>. Send therefore the [<-

*Line 2. "which": perhaps refers to <term>artabas</term> <note>a measure of volume</note> of hay-seed. Apollonios may have asked him to procure certain quantities of seed of fodder and <term>arakos</term> <note>a type of leguminous plant or wild pea</note>.*
*Line 3. For example: "the one who will carry it away."*

### 59098. Letter from Hierokles to Zenon(?). — 0.12 m x 0.115 m. — Date: 257 or 256 B.C.

The handwriting and the subject-matter show that this fragment belongs to a letter of Hierokles (see nos. 59060, 59061). Although a long-lined letter, it is, contrary to the general rule, written along the fibers and folded horizontally. I at first thought that *P.S.I.*, 645 was a fragment of the same text, but it is difficult to see how lines 3 and 4 of the two fragments can fit together. The subject of the letter is again the education of the boy Pyrrhos, and a proposal seems to have been made that he should be sent to another master.

[Hierokles to Zenon, greetings.] If you are well in [body and in other]
[matters you are faring] as you wish, it would be well. We ourselves [ ...
] you wrote to me that Pyrrhos [ ...
] to another teacher on the condition that [ ...
5 ] having taken a <term>grammarian</term> <note>a teacher of language and literature</note> for [ ... ] drachmas
] and for breakfast and for [ ...
] and for dinner, besides the [ ...
] you therefore send [ ...
] better than this.
10 Farewell.
Year 29, Month 19.

Line 11. "Month" may stand for Mesore; but as Hierokles usually dates by the Macedonian calendar, "Month" <note>original: "m(enos)"</note> is perhaps preferable. The back of *P.S.I.*, 645 has:
[Month of] Hyperberetaios
and Amphipolos.
**BIBL.** : Journal d'entrée du Musée, no. 48546.

Eunikos to Zenon, greetings. We have sent [to you] Polemaios
bringing [ ... ] 2 jars, 2 jars of apples <note>original: "melon," which can mean apples or other fleshy fruits</note>, 100 in number,

5 Year 29, Gorpiaios 23. Eunikos to Zenon.
Regarding the fact that he sent Polemaios.
[ ... ].

Line 2. Perhaps [pomegranates].
Line 3. "petalias": perhaps flat dishes or baskets. — "spheke": I do not know whether these are an additional item or refer to the figs (e.g., "in eight strings").
Line 4. The use of the Egyptian month shows that the fruit was probably sent from somewhere in the <term>chora</term> <note>the Egyptian countryside or provinces outside Alexandria</note>.
Line 6. "Sent" is written as a monogram.

### 59101. Letter from Melanippos to Zenon. — 0.115 m x 0.29 m. — Date: 14th October, 257 B.C.

A badly preserved letter, not very easy to read or to understand. A certain Ptolemaios (unless "Ptolemaios" in the filing note is a mistake for "Demetrios") was coming to see Apollonios, and Zenon is asked to assist him. But what the last sentence means I do not clearly see. Perhaps we might translate: "it is an advantage to us, with regard to the state and with regard to the man himself, that, as his affair has been settled, he should be allowed to go home."

> Melanippos to Zenon, greetings. Demetrios, the man delivering this 
> letter, was sent [ ... <unclear>lacuna</unclear> ... ] 
> to Apollonios the <term>dioiketes</term> <note>the chief financial minister of Ptolemaic Egypt</note>. You would do well then, in whatever 
> he may have need of you, to assist [ ... ] and by making 
> 5 it clear to him that we have written to you concerning him. For it is of 
> some use to us, both in respect to the city and to the man himself, 
> that once his business is settled, he should depart.
> -> Farewell. Year 29, Mesore 24. <-

Back of the document:

Year 29, Gorpiaios 29. Melanippos $~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~$ to Zenon.
10 [Note] that he should act quickly concerning Ptolemaios
. . 25. [
]. his

*Line 2. Ptolemaios was probably mentioned in the missing portion; see the filing note, line 10.*
*Line 9. Gorpiaios 29: Gorpiaios <note>a month in the Macedonian calendar</note> is supposed to be one of the short months without a twenty-ninth day, but the appearance of the 29th here should not be taken as absolute proof otherwise.*
*Line 10. "act quickly": the subject is Zenon (implied), and the filing note was probably not written by Zenon himself.*

<meta>Bibliography: Museum Entry Journal, no. 48549.</meta>

### 59102. Duplicate receipt. — 0.06 m x 0.06 m and 0.09 m x 0.085 m. — Date: September or October, 257 B.C. (plate XXII).

These two fragments probably belong together, forming a duplicate receipt for 14 <term>drachmae</term> <note>silver coins</note> paid for work to be done on a <term>diachoma</term> <note>a transverse embankment or dike used for irrigation</note>. Below is a <term>Demotic</term> <note>a cursive Egyptian script</note> version in which the date is given as year 28, Mesore 23. In another document (*P. S. I.*, 338) the same persons (or two persons whom I assume to be the same, though the names are read rather differently) receive money on the same day for <term>wood-cutting</term> and <term>burning</term> <note>likely clearing land by fire</note>.
Written along the fibers of the papyrus and folded horizontally.

> In the reign of Ptolemy, son of 
> Ptolemy the Savior, Year 29, Mesore 
> 23. Phernuthis son of Paos 
> and Horos son of Pokos acknowledge 
> 5 that they have received from Panakestor, the 
> agent of Apollonios, for the embankment 
> 14 drachmae.
> 
> In the reign of Ptolemy, son of 
> Ptolemy the Savior, Year 29, 
> 10 Mesore 23. Phernuthis son of 
> Paos and Horos son of Pokos acknowledge 
> that they have received from Panakestor, the 
> agent of Apollonios, for the embankment 
> fourteen drachmae.

-> Demotic version (see plate XXII). <-

*Lines 2-3. The day of the month in the Egyptian version is read by Selim effendi Hassan as 23 or 24.*
*Line 3. Phernuthis: read as Pherkythes in another publication.*
*Line 4. Pokos: spelled Pokas in another text, but here Pokos seems certain.*
*Lines 6-7. Embankment: this likely refers to a dike on Apollonios’ estate.*

<meta>Bibliography: Museum Entry Journal, nos. 48550, 48550 (a); Recueil Champollion, p. 120.</meta>

# 59105. Letter from Apollonios to Panakestor. — 0.135 m x 0.25 m. — Date : 3rd November, 257 B.C.

Apollonios orders Panakestor to take as much chickpea-seed <note>original: "ἐρεβίνθου", referring to chickpeas</note> and poppy-seed as he requires for sowing and to keep the remainder safely guarded.

> Apollonios to Panakestor [greetings. Of the chickpeas]
> and the poppy, whatever amount you [con]sume for seed,
> do so, but the rest keep under guard.
> ->Farewell. Year 29, Hyperberetaios 8, Thoth 9.<-
>
> 5 **Verso** <note>The back of the papyrus</note>:        To Panakestor.                Chickpeas,
>                                             Poppy.

<note>Lines 2-3. The sentence is ungrammatical in the original Greek, but the meaning is clear.</note>
<note>Lines 5-6. The position of the filing note (docket), to the right of the address and at the end of the adjoining fold, indicates that it was written by the sender's office.</note>

**Bibliography** : *Museum Entry Journal*, no. 48553.

---

# 59106. Letter from Apollonios to Panakestor. — 0.145 m x 0.28 m. — Date : 15th November, 257 B.C.

Apollonios informs Panakestor that he is not able to send him any wood and instructs him to buy up as much as he requires, not only in the Fayoum but in the neighboring <term>nomes</term> <note>Administrative districts of Egypt</note> as well. The wood may have been required for house-building, and the instructions show how difficult it was to procure a large quantity of it in the provinces.

> Apollonios to Panakestor greetings. Re[garding the wood,]
> we are not able to send any to you, but you yourself
> buy it up from the district itself and 
> send [an agent] to the Memphite <term>nome</term>, and like[wise to th]e Aph[ro]dit[o]-
> 5 polite and Herakleopolite districts; and from wherever else
> you are able, buy it up, as much as you have need of.
> ->Farewell. Year 29, Hyperberetaios 20, Thoth 21.<-
>
> **Verso** :
>       26                        To Panakestor.
>       Wood.

<note>Line 4. "Send" implies sending an agent specifically to purchase the materials.</note>
<note>Line 8. As the filing note seems to be complete, 26 must be the day of the month it was received or filed.</note>

**Bibliography** : *Museum Entry Journal*, no. 48554; *P. Edg.*, 85.

# 59107. Letter from Apollonios to Panakestor. — 0.195 m x 0.32 m. — Date: 18th November, 257 B.C. (pl. XXI).

It appears that Panakestor had asked Apollonios to let him have a boat on the river. Apollonios objects that the expense would be too great, as the boat would be lying idle for most of the year, while the sailors would be drawing wages all the time. But he offers to send him a boat if he can make an arrangement with some of the boatmen in Kerke, who will have the use of it on condition that they pay the sailors’ wages and lend it to Panakestor whenever he needs it. For a similar arrangement, see *P. Edg.*, 111.

Apollonios to Panakestor, greetings. <note>original: "Ἀπολλώνιος Πανακέστορι χαίρειν"</note> We would have sent the boat to you even sooner, but we see that there will be a great expense <note>original: "[π]λοῖον... μέ[γα ἀνή]λωμα"</note> for the sailors. If, therefore, you are able to give it to some of those in Kerke, who will work it and support the sailors, and whenever you have <margin>5</margin> need of the boat they will return it <note>original: "ἀποχρήσουσι" — literally "let you use it"</note>, write to us; for we will send it to you. But if not, I do not see how we can give wages all the time to sailors who are sitting idle for most of the time.
->Farewell. Year 29, Hyperberetaios 23, Thoth 24.<- <note>Hyperberetaios is a Macedonian month; Thoth is the first month of the Egyptian calendar</note>

**VERSO :**

Year 29, Phaophi 2. ->To Panakestor.<-
<margin>10</margin> Apollonios
regarding planting. <note>original: "φυτείας"</note>

<margin>Line 3. Kerke :</margin> identified by Grenfell with the modern Rekka. It served as a port to Philadelphia, with which town it was connected, as at the present day, by a road across the desert.
<margin>Line 4. ‘Who</margin> will ply their trade on it and pay (not merely feed) the sailors’. Cf. *P. Edg.*, 111, 20.
<margin>Line 6. sailors :</margin> <note>original: "ναύτοις" — the editor notes this as a specific spelling or grammatical form in the original</note>.
<margin>Line 11. regarding planting :</margin> no. 59107 has apparently received the docket intended for no. 59108, the two letters having arrived together.

**BIBL. :** *Journal d’entrée du Musée*, no. 48555; *P. Edg.*, 19.

---

# 59109. Letter from Zoilos to Panakestor. — 0.08 m x 0.28 m. — Date: 18th November, 257 B.C.

Zoilos the <term>oeconome</term> <note>A financial administrator of a district</note> asks Panakestor to send Komoapis the engineer to repair a damaged dyke <note>original: "χώματος"</note> in the village of Tanis. Compare *P. Edg.*, 110, introduction.

Zoilos to Panakestor, greetings. One of the dykes at Tanis has <note>original: "τῶν κατὰ Τάνιν χωμάτ[ων ἕν]"</note> suffered some damage. Send therefore Komoapis so that he may assist.

[Year 29, Tho]th 25. ->To Panakestor.<-
<margin>5</margin> [From Zoi]los regarding the dyke
[a]t Tanis.

<margin>Lines 1-2.</margin> It is uncertain whether **something** <note>original: "τι"</note> is the subject or whether **dykes** was followed by **one** (cf. no. 59024, 1).

**BIBL. :** *Journal d’entrée du Musée*, no. 48557; *P. Edg.*, 20.

# 59110. Letter from Amyntas to Zenon. — 0.305 m x 0.095 m. — Date: about 26th November, 257 B.C.

The first part of the letter is about Patron (cf. nos. 59012, 59019), the captain of a boat which was cruising between Alexandria and Apollonios’ temporary place of residence. Amyntas has heard that Patron is accustomed to excuse himself when he arrives late by saying that he has been retarded by Amyntas not giving him the letters in good time. The next sentence is mutilated and rather mysterious; it may perhaps mean that Amyntas did not care to write to Apollonios because he was

# 59113. Duplicate receipt. — 0.30 m $\times$ 0.07 m — Date: 30th November, 257 B.C.

Stotoetis acknowledges that he has received from Kleitarchos and Andron three artabs of barley as seed for the land which he is farming and a loan of three more artabs, the measure being the <term>receiving measure</term> <note>original: "δοχικόν" — a standard of measurement used when the estate received or issued grain, often slightly larger than the standard measure</note>. The land in question was probably on Apollonios’ estate, the work on which was at present controlled by Damis and his employees (*P. S. I.*, 500, 3). Kleitarchos was one of these employees (see no. 59094), though later on he appears as an agent of Zenon (*P. S. I.*, 358, 4).
Written along the fibres and folded horizontally.

-> acknowledges that he has [received] from <-
-> Kleitarchos and An- <-
-> dron for the land which <-

BIBLIOGRAPHY: *Museum Entry Journal*, no. 48564.

### 59117. Duplicate receipt. — 0.125 m x 0.08 m. — Date: 23rd January, 256 B.C. (pl. XXII).

Found with the inner text sealed up (see pl. XXII). On the clay sealing, now broken, was a human figure, indistinct. The text is written along the fibres and the papyrus is folded horizontally.

Pelois acknowledges receipt of 6 drachmæ, 4 obols, as a loan, for cutting down the brushwood on 10 aruræ and the same amount for burning the stumps on the same piece of land. The land, as in the other receipts of this type, was presumably part of Apollonios' property; but it is not clear why in some cases the money is said to be given as a loan. Vitelli suggests (*P. S. I.*, 323, note) that *loan* <note>original: "δάνειον"</note> may mean payment in advance. Or perhaps the receiver of the money was the farmer of the land to whom Apollonios' agent advanced such sums as were necessary to enable him to prepare the ground for cultivation.

<margin>brushwood <note>original: "κοπάς"</note></margin>
-> [Year] 29, Hathyr 30. Pelois <-
-> son of Peteinios, from Psoap- <-
-> ris, has [received] for the wood-cutting of the <-
-> brushwood as a loan for 10 arurae <-
-> 5 at the rate of / — / 6 — and <-
-> for the burning of 10 arurae <-
-> at the rate of / — / 6 —. <-

-> Year 29, Hathyr 30. Pe- <-
-> lois son of Peteminios, from Pso- <-
-> 10 apris, has [received] for the wood-cutting <-
-> of the brushwood as a loan <-
-> for 10 arurae at the rate of / — / 6 — and for <-
-> the burning of 10 arurae at the rate of / — <-
-> / 6 [ — ]. <-

Lines 2-3. *from Psoapris*: the use of *ex* <note>original: "ἐξ"</note> before *s* is common, but Mayser has no example to give of *x* <note>the Greek letter 'xi'</note> before *ps* <note>the Greek letter 'psi'</note> (*Grammar*, p. 225). But no doubt *Psoapris* was pronounced without the initial *p* (cf. *Psembeus* and *Sembeus* etc.). For the name, cf. *P. S. I.*, 675, 7.

BIBLIOGRAPHY: *Museum Entry Journal*, no. 48565.

# 59120. Letter from Artemidoros to Zenon. — 0.09 m $\times$ 0.23 m (Cairo) and 0.10 m $\times$ 0.10 m (Florence). — Date of reception: 27th January, 256 B.C.

Artemidoros (see no. 59136, introduction) had deposited two silver dishes with Dionysodoros as security for some money advanced to him for traveling expenses. He now sends Zenon the receipts for the dishes, asking him to settle with Dionysodoros and after recovering the dishes to forward them by any suitable messenger who may be coming that way.
Part of this letter, comprising the ends of lines 1-5, is in Florence and has been published as *P.S.I.*, 679. I give below the complete text without marking the division between the two fragments.

> Artemidoros to Zenon, greetings. We left with Dionysodoros, the son of Dionysios, two silver dishes <note>original: "βατιάκια" — small deep bowls or cups</note> in a case because we had received from him an advance of cash <note>original: "κερμάτιον"</note> for travel expenses <note>original: "ἐφόδια"</note>. Since we have now 
> sent the receipts <note>original: "τὰ σύμβολα"</note>, let [the money] be repaid to them and recover the dishes from Dionysodoros. I have also written to him myself so that he may give them back to you. When you have recovered them, if anyone 
<margin>5</margin> suitable happens to be coming this way, give them to him so that he may bring them to us. Farewell.

VERSO:

Year 29, Audnaios 4, Choiak 4. <note>The letter identifies the date using both the Macedonian and Egyptian calendars</note> To Zenon.
Artemidoros, regarding the dishes.

*Line 2.* *advance*: cf. *P.S.I.*, 483, 2.
*Line 3.* *let [the money] be repaid to them*: the subject of "repaid" might be "the receipts," but is more probably "the cash." For the use of "them" <note>original: "αὐτοῖς"</note> instead of the singular, cf. no. 59025, 21.

BIBLIOGRAPHY: *Museum Entry Journal*, no. 48568; *P.S.I.*, 679.

## 59121. Letter to Zenon from Ktesias. — 0.09 m x 0.195 m. — Date: 28th January, 256 B.C.

> Ktesias writes, probably from Alexandria, that he is unable to sell the two jars of caviare <note>original: "ἀντακαίου" (antakaios), referring to the pickled or salted roe of the sturgeon, a luxury import from the Black Sea region</note> and that Hierokles (see no. 59060) advises him to use them for private consumption and not let them be wasted. The pomegranate seed <note>original: "κόκκωνα"</note> also is too old to sell. 
> The text shows that the cargoes of comestibles imported by Apollonios and his friends (see no. 59012 and P. S. I., 428) were intended for sale as well as for their own use. Ktesias may perhaps have been a store-keeper.

-> Ktesias to Zenon, greetings. Know that I am not able <-
-> to dispose of the two jars of sturgeon-caviare; <-
-> and Hierokles instructs me to consume them, so that they might not be spoiled just like <-
-> the rest. Likewise, I am also not able to <-
-> 5 sell the pomegranate seed because it is old. <-
-> Farewell. Year 29 <note>The letter "L" is the standard symbol for "Year"</note> [ ... <-

VERSO <note>the back of the papyrus</note>:

-> Year 29, Audnaios, Choiak 5. To Zenon. <-
-> Ktesias, regarding the sturgeon-caviare <-
-> 2 jars, [noting] that they were not <-
-> 10 sold. <-

*Line 7.* Doubtful whether the "5" <note>original: "ε"</note> is meant to apply to both months or whether another "5" has been omitted after Audnaios <note>Audnaios was the Macedonian month, Choiak the Egyptian month</note>.

## 59124. Letter from Panakestor to Zenon. — 0.115 m x 0.07 m and 0.115 m x 0.15 m. — Date: 17th February, 256 B. C.

> In answer to Zenon’s letter Panakestor informs him that Iason has brought him a thousand <term>drachmae</term> <note>the standard silver or copper coin of the Greek world</note> in copper. He asks Zenon to send him the salaries for three months and a supply of wine. He would be glad if Zenon could find time to come and see to the work. He has already sent down 3600 <term>artabs</term> <note>an Egyptian unit of dry capacity, roughly 30–40 liters</note> of barley and is now sending down the rest. He is short of papyrus and asks Zenon to send him some. Panakestor probably writes from Philadelphia, and as in P. S. I., 499 he speaks of bringing up (i.e., from the river across the desert) some fruit-trees which Zenon has sent him, I think it is probable that Zenon was at present staying in Memphis, while Apollonios himself was in Alexandria (see P. Edg., 21, intro. and note 1). Zenon was paying visits to Philadelphia and giving instructions about work, but he was not yet settled there permanently.
> The hand is the same as that of P. Petr., II, 13, 5.

### 59124. Letter from Panakestor to Zenon. <note>Panakestor was the local manager at Philadelphia before Zenon took over.</note>

## 59129. Letter from Zenon to Panakestor. — 0.245 m x 0.10 m. — Date: 22nd March, 256 B.C. (plate XXIII).

Zenon informs Panakestor that he has sent him the <term>transport animal<note>original: "πορεῖον" (poreion), which could refer to a beast of burden, a wagon, or a boat</note></term> and a hundred drachmae, which was all the money that he could procure. He requests Panakestor to send to Krokodilopolis <note>a major city in the Fayum region</note> two loads of barley to make <term>green grain<note>original: "χῖδρα" (chidra), likely roasted unripe grain used for porridge or snacks</note></term> and also to send him some cabbage from time to time.

This is one of a few of Zenon's own letters which by some accident have been preserved in the archives. Perhaps a fair copy was sent instead, or perhaps it was filed by Panakestor and afterwards recovered by Zenon. The phraseology suggests that Zenon was at present staying at Krokodilopolis; but this is not certain.

->Zenon to Panakestor, greetings.<-
We have sent to you the 
transport animal and 
one hundred silver drachmae, 
5 [[having]] counted them out 
to Eutychides; for we were 
not able to get more. 
Order also two loads 
of the freshest 
10 and fullest barley 
to be sent [[and so that]] 
to Krokodilopolis, 
so that green grain may be made.

Line 3. transport animal <note>original: "πορεῖον"</note>: see *Tebtunis Papyri*, 5, 196, note.

<column-break/>

<header>ZENON PAPYRI.</header>

[[and on the same day]] [[and so th]] as soon as 
15 they have [[cut them off]] rubbed 
them out <note>original: "ἀποτρίψωσιν" (apotripsōsin), referring to threshing or rubbing grain from the ears</note>, let them bring them immediately, 
so that it does not get overheated 
and become sweet <note>original: "ἔγλευκος" (egleukos), meaning musty or unnaturally sweet due to heat</note> 
and useless. Also send 
20 us some cabbage. 
Farewell. Year 29, Tybi 28.

VERSO <note>The back of the papyrus</note>: ->To Panakestor.<-

Line 14. "on the same day" <note>original: "αυθη," likely the start of "αὐθημερόν" (authemeron)</note>.
Line 15. "cutting them off" <note>original: "ἀποκόψωσιν"</note>: cutting the ears off the stalk; "rubbing them out" <note>original: "ἀποτρίψωσιν"</note>: rubbing the grain off the ears.
Line 16. them: i. e. the barley.
Line 21. Zenon is now beginning to use the Egyptian calendar.

## 59130. Letter or memorandum to Zenon containing a copy of a letter of Apollonios. — 0.31 m (approximately) x 0.085 m. — Date of the letter of Apollonios: 16th April 256 B.C. or 254 B.C.

A farmer on Apollonios' land had been arrested because he had failed to pay the <term>salt-tax<note>original: "ἁλική" (halikē), a universal tax in Ptolemaic Egypt</note></term> or some other tax. Zenon is asked to write to Boubalos and Spendates to set him at liberty until the tax-collectors arrive, in order that the land may be weeded. The writer adds a copy of a letter addressed to Thrason and Paramonos by Apollonios, ordering them not to trouble the farmers in Tapteia about the salt-tax. As Boubalos, Spendates and Paramonos are all mentioned in connection with the Memphite nome <note>the administrative district around Memphis</note> (*Papyri Società Italiana*, 354, 382; *Edgar Papyri*, 54, 99), it is evident that the letter refers to an estate of Apollonios in that district. All these persons appear to have been employees of his, and apparently it was part of their duties to collect certain taxes from the peasants on his land and pay over the sum to the regular tax-collectors.

... we are 
privileged <note>or "placed under special care"</note>, because we 
farm the land 
of Apollonios. You 
5 would therefore do well 
to write to Bouba-

Lines 1-2. "we are privileged" <note>original: "προνομευόμεθα" (pronomeuometha)</note>: perhaps 'privileged', though the dictionaries give no example of this meaning.

<meta>This page continues the administrative records of the Zenon Archive, moving from correspondence about transport and basic supplies to more complex issues of tax collection, land surveying, and the management of soldier-settlers (cleruchs). We see Apollonios, the finance minister, intervening in local tax disputes, and Melas and Mys reporting on agricultural logistics. The timeframe remains early 256 B.C., during the 29th and 30th years of Ptolemy II Philadelphus's reign.</meta>

<page-num>140, 141</page-num>
<header>CATALOGUE OF THE CAIRO MUSEUM, ZENON PAPYRI.</header>

...to [Mela]s and Spendates concerning the farmer, so that he may be released until the tax-collectors <note>original: "τελῶναι" (telonai)</note> arrive, so that the land may be weeded. I shall come to you with all speed. I have also appended below the copies of the letter from Apollonios.

Apollonios to Thrason and Paramonos, greetings. Do not trouble the farmers in Tapleia <margin>Lines 18-19. Tapleia: a village on the estate of Apollonios in the Memphite district.</margin> concerning the salt tax <note>original: "ἁλικῆς" (halikes)</note>. Farewell.

Year 30, Peritios intercalary, Mecheir 23. <margin>Line 22. This double date, appearing to belong to Apollonios's letter, is problematic. There was no extra (intercalary) month in the 30th year. The number 30 is likely a mistake for 31 or 33, or it refers to the financial year starting on the 1st of Mecheir rather than the king's regnal year. If we assume it is the 31st year, we must assume the writer omitted the day '16' after the word 'intercalary'. It is a choice between two different clerical errors.</margin>

Patroklos <margin>Line 24. Patrokos: written as Patroklos.</margin> is also bringing two wild birds and 6 goose eggs.

VERSO:
->To [Zen]on.<-

->Melas to Zenon, greetings.<-

VERSO:
->Year 29, Peritios intercalary [To] Zenon.<-
->From Melas, regarding the portion he has sent.<-

<margin>Line 1. Likely says "we have sent you such things."</margin>
<margin>Line 2. Aristandros appears to be the last word, though it is uncertain.</margin>
<margin>Line 4. "Sent" is written as a symbolic monogram.</margin>
<margin>BIBLIOGRAPHY: Museum Entry Journal, no. 48579; Annals, XVII, p. 210.</margin>

### 59132. Letter from Mys to Zenon. — 0.115 m x 0.085 m and 0.115 m x 0.225 m. — Date: 23rd April, 256 B.C. (plate XXIII).

Mys writes about a man named Symbotes, perhaps a <term>cleruch<note>a soldier granted a plot of land in exchange for military service</note></term>, for whom a plot of land had been chosen but not yet officially measured. Because of this delay, Mys could not manage the crops, which were at risk of failing. Symbotes ignored these concerns and instead argued with the <term>royal scribe<note>a high-ranking provincial official responsible for land surveys and tax records</note></term>, demanding the land be measured by a different standard that would increase its size by ten percent. Zenon is asked for immediate instructions.

The use of the word "measured out" <note>original: "καταμετρηθῆναι"</note> suggests this was a military land allotment. Zenon's interest in the crops might stem from Apollonios's agents cultivating unoccupied portions of military land. Mys is identified elsewhere as an associate of Artemidoros, an official in Memphis who managed the settlement of these soldier-farmers.

Mys to Zenon, greetings. Concerning Symbotes, I wrote to you previously because we have often written to him and met with him <margin>Line 2. "Met with him" implies a verbal face-to-face meeting as opposed to writing.</margin> requesting that he take possession of the land, and now I thought it appropriate to remind you; for because it has been designated...

<summary>This page contains correspondence regarding agricultural management and legal disputes, including a tax exemption for farmers and a conflict over land surveying standards for a soldier-settler.</summary>
<keywords>Apollonios, Melas, Mys, salt tax, land survey, cleruch, Symbotes, Memphite nome, Ptolemaic administration</keywords>

<margin>Line 4. For example, [if it is necess]ary.</margin>

<margin>Line 8. "o(u)" (which): or the letter "d". There is an empty space after the letter "o", but no sign of a further letter.</margin>
<margin>Lines 8-9. "As it may... seem best": ‘however you may decide’.</margin>

### 59133. Royal oath. — 0.16 m. x 0.135 m. — Date : 30th April, 256 B. C.
**(plate XXIV).**

Found folded up and sealed with two clay sealings with indistinct designs. The writing is across the fibers and the papyrus was folded parallel to the writing. The part preserved no doubt belongs to the inner text of a duplicate deed, in which five Egyptian brickmakers swear to Zenon that they will remain in Philadelphia until they have fulfilled their contract. In addition to the religious sanction there was a penalty clause, beginning at line 16, but the details of this are lost (compare with no. 59011, verso, 1).

In the reign of Ptolemy son of Ptolemy Soter <note>Ptolemy I Soter, the "Savior," father of the current king.</note>
Year 29, in the priesthood of Antiochus son of Kebbas, of Alexan[der and]

<gloss>7th</gloss> [[the seventh]],
daughter of Philon, in the month of Dystros—and according to the Egyptians, [Phamenoth]—
the seventh.

<margin>Line 7. Perhaps "Pharytes"; see *P. S. I.*, vol. VI, p. x, note on 323.</margin>
<margin>Line 8. It is surprising to find an Egyptian brickmaker described in the first instance as a "Hellenomemphite" <note>An "Hellenomemphite" was technically a Greek living in Memphis, but the term eventually came to describe Egyptians who lived in the Greek quarter or adopted Greek status.</note>, even though it turns out that he really came from Troia <note>A village near Memphis.</note> on the other side of the river. But in such a case the word has a purely geographical connotation and might be applied to an Egyptian living in the old Greek quarter.</margin>
<margin>Line 12. "we will work": or some other word such as "we will fulfill" or "we will complete".</margin>
<margin>Line 14. Perhaps "handing over to others".</margin>
<margin>Line 17. This probably refers to the money which they had received from Zenon in advance.</margin>
<margin>Line 20. The name ending in "osos" is probably the one beginning with "P" in line 6.</margin>

### 59134. Letter from Agathinos to Zenon. — 0.135 m. x 0.115 m. — Date: 4th May, 257 or 256 B.C.

Theodoros, probably the <term>assistant</term> <note>Greek: hyperetes; a subordinate official or attendant</note> of no. 59062, had asked Agathinos, of whom nothing else is known, to give him some money, saying that Zenon had written to that effect, but Krotos <note>Another well-known agent of Apollonios</note> had arrived and forbidden him to give it. He now awaits instructions from Zenon.
Written across the fibers and folded horizontally. The date Phamenoth 11 of regnal year 29 is ambiguous, as it might belong either to 257 or to 256 B.C. In the early summer of 257 Krotos was probably in Palestine (see nos. 59077, 59093), and the present letter was evidently written in Egypt. Nevertheless the date is doubtful.

->Agathinos to Zenon, greeting.<-
->Theodoros approached me<-
->concerning money, saying that you<-
->had written to give it to him,<-
<margin>5</margin> ->but Krotos, having arrived,<-
->forbad me to give it.<-
->As it may seem best to you, then,<-
->write to us.<-
<margin>10</margin> ->Farewell. Year 29, Phamenoth<-
->11.<-

*Verso:* ->To Zenon.<-

*Line 4.* Read "to him" <note>Correction of a spelling variation in the original Greek</note>.

*Bibliography: Journal d’entrée du Musée*, no. 48582.

### 59135. Letter from Mys to Zenon. — 0.09 m. x 0.30 m. — Date: 10th May, 256 B.C.

A friendly letter expressing anxiety about Zenon’s health. As Phamenoth 17 occurs twice in regnal year 29 (due to the way the calendar was adjusted at the time), it might date either from 257 or 256 B.C. I have assigned it to the latter year, as we know that Mys was then in close communication with Zenon.

Mys to Zenon, greeting. If you are well and [everything else] is in order, it would be as we 
wish; we ourselves are also healthy, and we remember you always, 
and we are anxious, hearing that you are overexerting yourself. You would do

<column-break/>

<page-num>145</page-num>
<header>ZENON PAPYRI.</header>

a favor to me then by taking care of yourself at the same time so that you stay healthy 
<margin>5</margin> and by writing to us how you are, so that knowing it we might not be anxious. 
->Farewell. Year 29, Phamenoth 17.<-

*Verso:* ->To Zenon.<-

*Bibliography: Journal d’entrée du Musée*, no. 48583.

### 59136. Letter from Artemidoros to Mys. — 0.08 m. x 0.34 m. — Date: 11th May, 256 B.C. (plate XXIII).

Artemidoros tells Mys to hand over the sheep to Nouraios and to give him a receipt for any hay that they take for the draught animals (but see note on line 2) and also to order the guards to keep the sheep under observation. 
Though Artemidoros is described in the filing note as a <term>collector</term> <note>Greek: praktor; an official responsible for enforcing payments or debts</note>, I incline to think that he was not only the Artemidoros of whom Mys was an agent (*P. S. I.*, 551) but was also the person entitled <term>the one in charge of the contributions</term> <note>Greek: ho epi tes syntaxeos; an official overseeing specific financial allocations or levies</note> in no. 59073. It may have been part of his duties to act as a collector in the case of cultivators over whom he exercised authority. From indications in papyri of rather later date (e.g. *P. S. I.*, 564) I take it that the same Artemidoros was an agent of Apollonios at Memphis, a sort of inferior Zenon, but I admit the possibility that all these documents may refer to more than one man of the same name. The writing of the present letter is not identical with that of no. 59120, being rather more cursive, and seems to be by another hand.

<margin>10</margin>
Artemidoros to Mys, greeting. Hand over the sheep to Nouraios, and whatever hay
you receive for the livestock, give him a receipt. And order the
guards to keep watch over them.
->Farewell. Year 29, Phamenoth 18.<-

*Verso:*

<margin>5</margin> Year 29, Phamenoth 18. ->To Mys.<-
From Artemidoros, collector, 
regarding Nouraios's sheep.

*Line 2.* Or possibly "and whatever he receives," in which case <term>the livestock</term> <note>Greek: ta ktene</note> would refer to the sheep and <term>receipt</term> <note>Greek: symbolon</note> would mean a warrant or token rather than a standard receipt.

*Bibliography: Journal d’entrée du Musée*, no. 48585.
*Catalogue of the Museum, no. 59001.*

## 59137. Duplicate receipt. — 0.175 m. x 0.12 m. — Date: 11th May, 256 B.C. (pl. XXIV).

Written across the fibers and folded horizontally. It is a receipt for four drachmæ, paid in advance (cf. *P. Petr.* III, 43, 2) to a certain Horos for the excavation of fifty *aoilia* <note>A technical term for a unit of earthwork or excavation, likely representing a specific volume of soil or a pit of a certain size</note> of earth, probably on the estate of Apollonios. It is to be noted that Zenon is now definitely called the agent of Apollonios in Philadelphia.

> In the reign of Ptolemy son of Ptolemy <note>Ptolemy II Philadelphus</note>
> Soter, year 29, Dystros 18, and
> in the Egyptian calendar, Phamenoth 18. Horos
> son of Aryotes has received from Zenon,
> <margin>5</margin> the agent of Apollonios in Philadelphia
> in the Arsinoite nome,
> for 50 <gloss>himself</gloss> <note>original: auton</note> *aoilia* which he must complete,
> 4 drachmae. <margin>⊢ 4</margin> <note>The symbol ⊢ represents the drachma</note>
>
> In the reign of Ptolemy son of Ptolemy
> <margin>10</margin> Soter, year 29, Dystros 18, and in the Egyptian calendar,
> Phamenoth 18. Horos son of Aryotes has received through the
> surety <note>original: di' engyou</note> of Pasis of Heliopolis
> from Zenon, the agent of Apollonios
> in Philadelphia in the Arsinoite
> <margin>15</margin> nome, for 50 *aoilia* which he must
> complete, 4 drachmae. <margin>⊢ 4</margin>

*Lines 11-12. through a surety* : the restoration is doubtful, though the same phrase occurs elsewhere in our papyri. Possibly *through Entous son of Pasis*; but there seems little room for a proper name.
*Line 15. For the cost, 4 drachmæ for 50 aoilia, see P. Petr., III, p. 345 and P. Lille I, 1.*

BIBL. : *Journal d’entrée du Musée, no. 48586 ; P. Edg., 23 ; ROSTOVTZEFF, Large Estate, p. 60.*

# IV. — YEARS AND MONTHS.

## 1. YEARS (OF PTOLEMY II). <note>Dates in these papyri are given in the regnal years of Ptolemy II Philadelphus, who reigned from 283–246 BC. The symbol "L" is a common papyrological shorthand for "Year".</note>

<columns>2</columns>
Year 12 <note>original: "Lιβ"</note> **59001**, 2, [27].  
Year 13 <note>original: "Lιγ"</note> **59001**, 10, [37].  
Year 24 <note>original: "Lκδ"</note> **59002**, 6, 7.  
Year 27 <note>original: "Lκζ"</note> **59003-59018** in various places <note>original: "passim"</note>. See also **59036**, 25;  

<column-break/>

**59041**, 2.  
Year 28 <note>original: "Lκη"</note> **59020-59059** in various places; **59087-59090** in various places.  
Year 29 <note>original: "Lκθ"</note> **59059-59139** in various places.  
Year 30 <note>original: "Lλ"</note> **59130**, 22 (see note in that location <note>original: "ad loc."</note>).

## 2. DOUBLE DATES. <note>Under the Ptolemies, documents often used two dates: the Macedonian lunar calendar (used by the court) and the Egyptian solar calendar (used for administration and by the local population).</note>

### VIII. — SYMBOLS.

Monograms and abbreviations whose meaning is not self-evident are resolved in the text or explained in the notes. The following signs are assumed to be known:

| | | | |
|:---|:---|:---|:---|
| L | year | — | obol <note>A small Greek coin/weight.</note> |
| <note>L with a hook</note> | thereof, of these or from this, from these | = | two obols |
| / | total, it becomes | 𐅵 | three obols |
| ∠ | 1/2 | 𐅷 | four obols |
| γ', δ' etc. | 1/3, 1/4 etc. | 𐅸 | five obols |
| 𐅀 | 900 | c | half-obol |
| Π<sup>α</sup>, Π<sup>β</sup> etc. | 1000, 2000 etc. | <note>χ with bar and tick</note> | quarter (1/4 of an obol) |
| M<sup>α</sup>, M<sup>β</sup> etc. | 10,000, 20,000 etc. | χ, χ etc. | 1, 2 etc. copper coins (*chalcoi*), dry measures (*choinikes*), or liquid measures (*choes*) depending on context |
| <note>Λ with bar</note> | talent <note>A very large unit of weight/currency.</note> | | |
| ⊢ | drachma | | |

<meta>catchword: Catalogue of the Museum, no. 59001.</meta>
<sig>21</sig>

<summary>This index identifies geographical locations and origins (ethnics) across the Ptolemaic Empire and provides a guide to the shorthand symbols for currency and measurements used by the scribes of Zenon's archive.</summary>

<summary>This index covers Greek terms from Delta to Epsilon found in the Zenon Papyri, highlighting key administrative roles like the Dioiketes and various Hellenistic units of measure and taxation.</summary>
<keywords>Zenon Papyri, Cairo Museum, Greek Index, Dioiketes, Drachma, Papyrology, Taxation, Trade Vocabulary</keywords>

--- Page 92 ---
<meta>This page continues the alphabetical index of the Zenon Papyri, spanning the late letter Epsilon through early Theta. The entries highlight terms related to administrative orders (letters and notices), labor (workers and contracts), and a significant variety of precise measurements for commodities like wool, oil, and land.</meta>

<summary>This index listing provides a vocabulary of common terms in the Zenon Papyri, ranging from trade goods like honey, wine, and vinegar to agricultural and labor terms like wood-cutting and basket-carrying. It highlights the administrative and economic language used in Ptolemaic Egypt during the 3rd century BCE.</summary>
<keywords>Zenon Papyri, Greek vocabulary, Ptolemaic Egypt, agricultural terms, administrative terminology, ancient measures, economic history</keywords>
