### ADDITIONS AND CORRECTIONS.
---

59002, bibliography. Read *Journal of entries* <note>original: "Journal d'entrée"</note>, no. 48449.
59003, 19. Read **Timopolis** <note>original: "Τιμ[όπο]λις"</note>.
59004, 15. Read **containing forty choinixes** <note>original: "τεσσαρακονταχοινίκωι"; a choinix was a dry measure of about 1 quart</note>.
59008, 28, note. For **written against** <note>original: "παραγραφέντα"</note>, read **written upon** <note>original: "ἐπιγραφέντα"</note>.
59009 (a), 5. Read **of Zenodoros** <note>original: "Ζηνοδώ[ρου"</note>.
59011, back, column 1, 4. The break in the papyrus descends in a straight vertical line.
59012, 35. Perhaps **three-foot vessels** <note>original: "τριπόδια"</note> rather than **three-kotyle vessels** <note>original: "τρικότυλα"; a kotyle was about half a pint</note> (see no. 59014 (a), 5); but the latter word seems a more suitable adjective for **small jars** <note>original: "κεραμύλλια"</note> (cf. *P. S. I.*, 535, 12, 17) <note>P. S. I. refers to "Papiri della Società Italiana," a series of published papyri</note>.
59016, 9, note. Viereck <note>Paul Viereck, a German papyrologist</note> has kindly verified the reading of *P. S. I.*, 429, 56 and informs me that **tettaphou** <note>original: "τετταφου"</note> is certain. My suggestion is therefore cancelled.
59026, introduction. The probable date of the letter is the autumn of 258 B. C., and the autumn of 257 B. C. is *not* a possible alternative.
59029, 6. Read **would ask** <note>original: "ᾐτήσειν"</note> rather than **he requested** <note>original: "ἠξίωσεν"</note>.
59032, introduction. The author is probably *not* Amyntas, who never begins his letters to Zenon with such an effusive greeting.
59034, 1. Read **to Apollonios** <note>original: "Ἀπολλωνίωι"</note>. To the bibliography add *Lumbroso Collection* <note>original: "Raccolta Lumbroso"</note>, pp. 475-478 (Crönert).
59066, 13, 14. Read **perkaia** <note>original: "περκάια"; likely a type of perch fish or a related tax</note>.
59068. A letter of Mys (see no. 59132), written in the month of Payni in year 30, is in the same hand as the letters of Nikon; and as in no. 59136 we find Artemidoros writing to Mys about sheep, it is possible that the present letter was written by Mys rather than Nikon and that it dates from May, 256 B.C. At any rate it is a doubtful case and warns us not to lay too much weight on handwriting as evidence of authorship.
59072. From the date and the figure **40** <note>the Greek numeral μ</note> in the **docket** <note>a filing note written on the back of a letter</note> it seems probable that this letter was written in continuation of *P. S. I.*, 556.
59093, 10-15. In the introduction I have taken all this sentence to be part of the news related by Apollophanes, supposing him to have dropped midway into indirect speech; but it is certainly more grammatical to make Menekles the subject of **he said** <note>original: "ἔφη"</note>, and we can equally well regard the sentence as a story about Apollophanes told to Herakleitos by Menekles.
59099, 3, **petalias** <note>original: "πεταλίας"; meaning uncertain, perhaps broad-horned cattle or thin metal plates</note>: the same word occurs in *Oxyrhynchus Papyri* <note>original: "P. Ox."</note>, 1850, 1 in the form **pedalias** <note>original: "πεδαλίας"</note>.

<summary>This page concludes the lexical index for the Zenon archive with words starting with Chi, Psi, and Omega, and provides academic corrections regarding dates, authorship, and specific Greek readings in the papyri.</summary>

# CATALOGUE OF THE CAIRO MUSEUM. — ZENON PAPYRI.

Plate I

*A black and white plate from a catalogue showing two papyrus manuscripts. The top image, catalogued as 59002, is a horizontal fragment of papyrus with multiple lines of Greek script. The bottom image, catalogued as 59004, consists of three main vertical fragments of papyrus arranged to show columns of Greek cursive handwriting. Both manuscripts show signs of aging, including fraying at the edges and horizontal or vertical tears.*

<unclear>Transcription of the Greek manuscript 59002: approximately eight lines of Greek cursive script <note>original: "cursive"; a flowing style of handwriting used for everyday documents in antiquity.</note>, too faded for reliable transcription at this resolution.</unclear>

->59002<-

---

<unclear>Transcription of the Greek manuscript 59004: multiple vertical fragments containing columns of Greek script in a cursive hand. The text is interrupted by significant physical gaps in the papyrus.</unclear>

->59004<-

<term>Zenon Papyri</term> <note>A massive archive of 3rd-century BCE documents belonging to Zenon, an agent of the finance minister under Ptolemy II.</note>, <term>Cairo Museum</term> <note>The Egyptian Museum in Cairo, the primary repository for these artifacts.</note>, <term>papyrus</term> <note>Writing material made from the pith of the Cyperus papyrus plant.</note>, <term>Greek cursive</term>, 59002, 59004, <term>epigraphy</term> <note>The study and interpretation of ancient inscriptions.</note>, <term>papyrology</term> <note>The study of ancient literature and documents preserved on papyrus.</note>

# CATALOGUE OF THE CAIRO MUSEUM. — ZENON PAPYRI.

## 59003

**Plate II**

*A large, vertical fragment of dark brown papyrus with Greek text written in two distinct sections (Inner Text and Outer Text). The papyrus is mounted on a light background. There are several large holes throughout the center and left side of the fragment. The text is written along the fibers.*

<note>This document is a loan contract from the Zenon Archive, dated to the reign of Ptolemy II Philadelphus (roughly 259–258 BCE). It is a "double document," a common legal format in antiquity where the text was written twice on the same sheet. The top version (Inner Text) was rolled up and sealed by witnesses to prevent tampering, while the bottom version (Outer Text) was left open for regular reference.</note>

<insert>

<term>In the reign of Ptolemy</term> <note>Refers to the official dating formula using the regnal years of the King.</note>, <term>Brother-Gods</term> <note>The "Theoi Adelphoi," the divine title for the ruling couple Ptolemy II and Arsinoe II.</note>, <term>Basket-bearer of Arsinoe Philadelphus</term> <note>The Canephorus, a prestigious eponymous office held by a young noblewoman in Alexandria.</note>, <term>Berenike’s Harbor</term> <note>Berenike Hormos, a major Ptolemaic port on the Red Sea used for trade and military expeditions.</note>, <term>Red Sea</term> <note>The Erythra Thalassa, vital for the Ptolemaic elephant-hunting expeditions and trade with India.</note>, <term>Zenon Archive</term> <note>A collection of over 2,000 papyri providing a detailed look at the administration and daily life in 3rd-century BCE Egypt.</note>

<summary>This page presents a loan contract from 259/258 BCE, notable for being drafted at Berenike's Harbor on the Red Sea. It follows the "double document" format, preserving the official dating formulas and religious titles used during the reign of Ptolemy II Philadelphus.</summary>

*This plate features two distinct papyrus fragments from the Zenon Archive. The images showcase the variety of physical conditions in which these ancient documents are preserved.*

It contains approximately twelve lines of script.* <note>The text is written in a dense Ptolemaic Greek cursive. Ptolemaic cursive was a fast, flowing style of handwriting used by Greek administrators in Egypt during the 3rd century BCE.</note>

These documents provide incredible detail on ancient economics, law, and daily life.</note> *The text is arranged in three main horizontal sections, typical of the administrative records found in the collection.*

## PLATE VII

<margin>
59021
</margin>

*This plate displays a monochrome photograph of a significant papyrus fragment from the Zenon Archive, cataloged under inventory number 59021.*

The fragment is rectangular and contains approximately thirty lines of <term>Greek cursive</term> <note>A fast, flowing style of handwriting used by Greek administrators in Egypt during the 3rd century BCE for letters and accounts.</note> written in dark ink. The material shows clear signs of its great age, including horizontal fold lines—likely from when the document was originally folded for storage—and frayed, irregular edges. A significant portion of the text is lost due to a large <term>lacuna</term> <note>A gap or missing part in a manuscript or ancient text.</note> in the lower right-hand section.

<note>The Greek text on the papyrus fragment is too degraded and the image resolution is insufficient for a reliable line-by-line transcription. The document is part of the extensive Zenon archive found in Philadelphia, Egypt.</note>

The handwriting is small and densely packed, which was typical for documentary and administrative records during the reign of the Ptolemies in the 3rd century BCE.

## PLATE X

*Plate X illustrates the challenges of papyrology. The two fragments shown here highlight the varied ways in which history is lost or preserved: one through the physical fading of ink, and the other through the literal breaking of the material.*

*Fragment 59029 is shown at the top. The papyrus is severely darkened and the ink has faded so significantly into the fibers that the rapid cursive script is almost entirely lost to the naked eye. Only faint traces remain of what was once a complete document.*

# CATALOGUE OF THE CAIRO MUSEUM — ZENON PAPYRI. PLATE XIII

*Plate XIII displays a single papyrus sheet containing four short, professional communications. In the 3rd century BCE, papyrus was often used for multiple short messages to save material, or these may have been "day-book" copies of outgoing or incoming correspondence. The handwriting is a practiced, fluid business script typical of the Ptolemaic administration.*

->**Communication 1**<-

Apollonios to Zenon, greetings. Regarding the <unclear>wheat</unclear> sold into the granary in Ptolemais,
amounting to <unclear>100</unclear> <term>artabas</term><note>original: "artabōn" — a standard dry measure in Egypt, roughly 30 to 40 liters, used primarily for grain.</note> of wheat, for which the seals are both Apollonios's and mine;
and the remainder of the wheat in the same granary, <unclear>140</unclear> artabas, for which the seals
are both Apollonios's and mine—you would do well to count up the <term>tokens</term><note>original: "symbola" — these were official receipts, tallies, or vouchers that served as proof of a transaction or delivery.</note>.
Take care of yourself so that you stay healthy.
Farewell. Year 29, Panemos 22. <note>The 29th year of the reign of Ptolemy II Philadelphos, approximately 257 BCE. Panemos was a month in the Macedonian calendar.</note>

---

->**Communication 2**<-

Lykomedes to Zenon, greetings. If you are healthy and other things are to your liking,
it would be as we wish; I myself am also healthy. I had sent word to you
previously among other matters regarding the <term>stay</term><note>original: "hypomonēs" — this term could refer to a delay, a remaining balance of goods, or a deposit of security.</note> which Jason has, so that you might accept it
if it is useful. Jason has also written to me regarding the boat, asking that
you sign off on the accounts for him, so that it may be clear to us how
we must act. Regarding the other matters about which you have been sent instructions, you will do
well to take care of them.
Farewell.

---

->**Communication 3**<-

Apollonios to Zenon, greetings. <unclear>Please</unclear>, for the sake of those things we have instructed you,
take care of them and write to us what will be done so that we also may know.
Take care of yourself so that you stay healthy.
Farewell.

---

->**Communication 4**<-

Apollonios to Zenon, greetings. Regarding the camels sent to you, so that
they may be counted and you may receive the <term>tokens</term><note>original: "symbola" — official tallies or receipts confirming the handover of livestock.</note> from the <term>nomarchs</term><note>original: "nomarchōn" — local Greek officials responsible for the administration and taxation of a specific district (nome).</note>, you will do well to
take care that nothing remains without a receipt.
Farewell.

## PLATE XVI

*This plate provides a fascinating look at the lifecycle of an ancient letter. *The photograph above shows the exterior of a papyrus letter as it would have appeared upon delivery. In the 3rd century BCE, letters were rolled or folded into tight strips and tied with string. A lump of clay or wax was then pressed over the knot and impressed with a signet ring to ensure privacy and authenticity.*

## PLATE XVIII

*Plate XVIII presents four monochrome photographs of ancient papyrus fragments from the Zenon Papyri collection. These pieces exhibit the varied states of preservation and the fluent cursive Greek script used by administrators and individuals in the mid-3rd century BCE.*

### Unnumbered Fragment (Top Left)
This small, roughly rectangular piece of papyrus contains dark ink script. A prominent horizontal lacuna <note>original: "lacuna," refers to a gap or hole in the material</note> is visible near the center of the fragment, where the delicate plant fibers have broken away.

### Fragment 59082 (Top Right)
<margin>59082</margin>
A wider rectangular fragment showing several lines of Greek cursive. There is significant loss of material in the upper right corner and along the bottom edge, illustrating the fragile nature of the documents recovered from the dry sands of Egypt.

### Fragment 59075 (verso)
<margin>59075 (verso)</margin>
<note>The "verso" is the back of the papyrus sheet. In the Zenon archive, the verso often contains the address of the recipient or a brief summary for filing purposes.</note>
This vertical fragment displays text written perpendicular to the long axis. It contains large, irregular vertical gaps where the papyrus has frayed, obscuring parts of the endorsement. This is the reverse side of the letter from Hierokles concerning the dogs found on the previous plate.

### Fragment 59077 (Bottom Right)
<margin>59077</margin>
A tall, narrow vertical strip of papyrus containing many lines of dense, relatively continuous Greek cursive script. While the edges are frayed and small holes are scattered throughout, the continuity of the lines suggests a longer administrative list or a formal legal document.

## PLATE XX

*Plate XX presents a high-resolution monochrome photograph of a single, substantial papyrus fragment from the Zenon archive. These documents provide an intimate look at the administrative and personal life of Zenon, a Greek official managing a large estate in the Fayum region of Egypt during the 3rd century BCE.*

->**59093**<-

*This image shows a rectangular fragment of dark papyrus, identified by the inventory number 59093. The material exhibits prominent horizontal fibers—characteristic of the "recto" or front side of the sheet—and several vertical cracks and folds, including a significant break running down the right side. The text consists of approximately 30 to 40 lines of Ancient Greek script.*

<note>The handwriting is a "documentary hand," a style of cursive used by professional scribes in the Ptolemaic period for business, legal, and administrative records. It is more fluid and faster than the formal "book hand" used for literary works.</note>

<unclear>The text of this document is largely illegible due to the preservation state of the original material and the photographic limitations of the time. Consequently, it has not been transcribed here to avoid historical inaccuracy.</unclear> <note>While the script is visible, the nuances of the ink and the aging of the papyrus make a definitive reading impossible without direct physical examination of the original artifact.</note>

# CATALOG OF THE CAIRO MUSEUM — ZENON PAPYRI

## PLATE XXI

*This plate displays two distinct fragments of papyrus from the Zenon archive. These pieces are typical of the "rubbish heaps" of ancient Oxyrhynchus and Philadelphia, where discarded files from the estate of Zenon—the business manager for the Ptolemaic Treasurer Apollonios—were preserved by the dry desert sands. These fragments, though damaged, offer clues about the logistics and dating of ancient Egyptian bureaucracy.*

->59096<-

*This fragment is a narrow horizontal strip of papyrus, severely worn with significant loss of text in the center. Only a few isolated words can be identified amidst the staining.*

<unclear>...</unclear>

<unclear>...</unclear>

<unclear>...</unclear>

<unclear>...</unclear>
<unclear>...</unclear> <unclear>we are sending</unclear> <unclear>...</unclear> <note>original: "apostellomen," a common verb in administrative correspondence for dispatching goods or messengers.</note>

# CATALOGUE OF THE CAIRO MUSEUM — ZENON PAPYRI

## PLATE XXII

*This plate displays six fragments of papyrus from the Zenon archive. A distinctive feature of several pieces in this group (59094, 59111, 59117, and 59119) is the presence of intact clay seals. These small lumps of clay were impressed with a signet ring to secure the document or authenticate the sender, providing a rare physical glimpse into the security measures of Ptolemaic administration.*

<note>Warning: The text on these papyrus fragments is written in an ancient Greek cursive hand; it is significantly faded, damaged, and difficult to transcribe accurately from the photographic plate. The following representations are partial and approximate.</note>

->59094<-

*A large, vertical rectangular papyrus. This document is significant for having three circular clay seals still attached to its upper edge, suggesting a high degree of formal verification. It contains approximately 18 lines of Greek text.*

# CATALOGUE OF THE CAIRO MUSEUM — ZENON PAPYRI

## PLATE XXIII

*This plate features four fragments of papyrus from the mid-3rd century BCE, belonging to the archive of Zenon, the manager of a large estate in the Fayum region of Egypt. The handwriting varies from quick, professional cursive to more formal administrative hands. These documents illustrate the constant flow of information—greetings, financial requests, and formal accounts—that sustained the Ptolemaic economy.*

<note>Fragment No. 59132 (Top and middle left portions)</note>

# CATALOGUE OF THE CAIRO MUSEUM — ZENON PAPYRI

## PLATE XXIV

*This plate displays two distinct papyrus fragments from the Zenon archive, dating to the mid-3rd century BCE. These documents are written in the common Greek of the period (Koine) using a cursive script that allowed for rapid writing. Such fragments provide a glimpse into the sophisticated postal and record-keeping systems of the Ptolemaic administration in Egypt.*

### 59137

<note>This horizontal fragment contains approximately ten lines of text. Although the ink has faded or been lost in places, the fluid "hand" or handwriting is typical of a professional scribe.</note>

<unclear>
[The text begins with a standard greeting formula, likely: "To Zenon, greetings"] <note>original: "Ζήνωνι χαίρειν" (Zenoni chairein), the conventional opening for Greek correspondence of this era.</note>
[Approximately ten lines of cursive text follow, containing various administrative instructions or personal news.]
[Traces of a closing formula appear at the end, typically: "Farewell"] <note>original: "ἔρρωσο" (errōso), literally meaning "be strong" or "stay healthy."</note>
</unclear>

---

### 59133

<note>This vertical fragment is significantly more damaged, particularly along the right-hand edge. The visible horizontal creases are evidence of how the papyrus was rolled or folded into a small packet before being sealed for delivery.</note>

<unclear>
[Densely packed lines of Greek cursive text arranged in a single column.]
[The right side of the text is lost due to the fraying of the papyrus fibers.]
</unclear>
